- **[Hetero-Associative Visuomotor Memory: Soft Recall as a Base for Learned Residuals in Few-Demonstration Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34014)**  `arXiv:2611.34014`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Runcong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieve-and-correct policies typically refine the nearest retrieved demonstration chunk, thereadout that works best when executed directly. We study what a demonstration memory shouldsupply to the corrector instead and find that a smooth, softmax-weighted recall over the wholememory is a much better base. On ALOHA cube transfer (40 human demonstrations), soft recall isno better to execute alone than the nearest chunk; however, with keys, candidates, and trainingfixed, correcting it raises success by 22.4 points on fresh resets with new encoders and by 7.4against a closed-loop-tuned top-1 corrector. On robomimic Square, it leads by 7.9 points withthe two better operators and by 6.7 on a label-free draw of equal size. This gain holds onsparse, varied demonstrations under an action-aware key and leave-one-demonstration-outtraining, and fades or slightly reverses on dense, scripted, or generated data. Building on thisfinding, our hetero-associative visuomotor memory (HAVM, 14M parameters) trains offline in aboutfive minutes, including a learned retrieval temperature $\beta$, and runs in 1.1 ms. Across sixfresh ALOHA encoders, it reaches 76.4% within 24 s, comparable to a 264M Diffusion Policytrained for 6.7 h (78.8%) and ahead of ACT (71.7%) and fine-tuned VLAs (65.2-75.2%), whileleading every 40-demonstration baseline within the benchmark's 8 s limit (74.6%; +6.5 overDiffusion Policy, +15.1 over ACT). On LIBERO, one 40-task memory reaches 92.2% and loses farless than SmolVLA to rewording; on a real SO-101 arm, it sustains 30 Hz control withoutstalling. Unlike pretrained foundation policies aimed at zero-shot generalization to unseentasks and workspaces, HAVM trades broad out-of-distribution generalization for rapid adaptationto the available demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[Effect-Driven Skill Abstractions for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33229)**  `arXiv:2611.33229`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Burcu Kilic, David Drexel, Emre Ugur, Justus Piater_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We seek to learn discrete skill abstractions to facilitate long-horizon, goal-conditioned reinforcement-learning (RL) tasks. Recent approaches have shown success by quantizing actions via clustering or reconstruction. However, these objectives lead to skills that are not directly related to what the current state affords or what the goal requires. Instead, we argue that goal-directed skills should be driven by \emph{how they affect the state} of the agent. To realize this, we introduce a novel quantization method for action chunks that optimizes next-state predictions conditioned on the current state and action. This objective encourages skills to be distinguished by the future states they lead to, resulting in a vocabulary that spans diverse outcomes reachable from a given state. Our framework can easily be paired with common offline RL methods by replacing their continuous actor head with a discrete one that selects among the learned skills. We can execute these skills in the environment by converting them back to continuous actions using a trained low-level controller. Experiments on benchmark navigation and manipulation tasks largely show substantial and consistent improvements over both the continuous-actor baselines and reconstruction-based discretizations. Our project page can be found at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Cairn: Object-Centric 3D Trajectory Priors for Steering Frozen VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34914)**  `arXiv:2611.34914`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Agelos Kratimenos, Kostas Daniilidis, Evangelos Chatzipantazis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action models (VLAs) are capable low-level controllers, but they tie where objects go to the layouts they were trained on: when objects are moved or obstacles block the way, they carry them to the wrong place or into collisions. Fine-tuning on new layouts needs robot data for each one and erodes skills the model already has. We introduce Cairn, which keeps the VLA frozen and steers it at test time along learned 3D trajectories of the object in its gripper. A small prior reads point clouds of the scene and the held object, along with its pose when grasped and the instruction, and samples SE(3) trajectories up to its release that lead it around obstacles and turn it to fit through gaps. The prior and the VLA form the two levels of a hierarchical planner in which feedback runs both ways: the VLA's intended motion chooses and reshapes the trajectories, and the chosen one guides the VLA's denoising. Its predicted actions, mapped to the held object's poses through the grasp, are pulled toward the next waypoint, while the VLA's weights stay untouched. Across 1,941 held-out LIBERO scenes with displaced objects and obstacles, Cairn raises the success rate of a frozen {\pi}0.5 from 24.5% to 95.1%, above that of a {\pi}0.5 fine-tuned on our training layouts, without hurting standard LIBERO. On the third-party LIBERO-PRO benchmark, it raises success from 27.3% to 38.0%, and from 54.4% to 81.1% on the episodes it steers. On a real Franka, with a prior trained purely in simulation, it guides the frozen DROID {\pi}0.5 around an obstacle and to a placement at the limit of the arm's reach, where it otherwise fails. All code, models and data will be made public.
  </details>

- **[Real2SimLab: A Unified Platform for Real-to-Simulation Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34849)**  `arXiv:2611.34849`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yongchang Zhang, Jie Huang, Yunhe Ni, Ningyu Zhou, Guangyu Zhao, Ruixun Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-to-simulation transfer, which builds simulations from real-world observations, has long been studied in research and industry. Recently, coding agents have become capable of generating simulation programs from video recordings, and several benchmarks have been proposed to evaluate them. However, each individual benchmark defines its own inputs, deliverables, and scoring pipeline. This leads to $N \times M$ integrations when evaluating $N$ methods on $M$ benchmarks. To this end, we present Real2SimLab, a unified platform for real-to-simulation transfer. It evaluates a method by running its written program and scoring the output simulation with the original scorer of each benchmark. Moreover, Real2SimLab provides a pipeline to create training data and reward annotations for real-to-simulation methods. Our pipeline carefully avoids data contamination by filtering out the video recordings that exist in benchmarks. For each video recording, a program is first produced, and then our pipeline computes its reward with the same code that Real2SimLab uses for evaluation. So far, it supports 47 coding agents and 5 non-agent methods on 6 benchmarks. Both the platform and Awesome-Real2Sim, our research map of nearly 300 real-to-simulation papers, are open source, and we invite the Real2SimHub community to build them together.
  </details>

- **[QK-Bench: Benchmarking Quantitative Kinematic Understanding for Dynamic Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33991)**  `arXiv:2611.33991`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Lennox Wei, Jiaqi Yao, Shixin Sun, Yuanzheng Zhang, Jianhua Han, Meng Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial intelligence concerns both scene structure and its temporal evolution. Yet a semantically correct description can conceal a quantitative error: recognizing that objects are approaching does not determine their closing speed or whether they will meet. Such errors are critical in collision avoidance, trajectory tracking, and reactive grasping, where actions depend on motion magnitude, timing, and relative geometry. We study quantitative kinematic understanding in multimodal large language models (MLLMs): resolving motion magnitude, rate, reference-frame dependence, and future spatial evolution from visual evidence. We introduce the Quantitative Kinematics Benchmark (QK-Bench), comprising 2,341 multiple-choice QA pairs from 379 videos and 17 tasks spanning Object-Centric Kinematics, Camera Ego-Motion Kinematics, and Multi-Object Kinematic Relations. Hidden geometric and temporal signals support trajectory construction and filtering, while fixed kinematic rules map physical quantities to verifiable answers, including categorical labels. Trajectory and question validation ensure target reliability and sufficient visual support in the sampled frames. All models receive a unified monocular RGB input. Evaluations of proprietary, open-source, and spatial MLLMs reveal substantial limitations: the strongest model achieves only 41.2\% overall accuracy, and spatial MLLMs show no consistent advantage. These findings indicate that current advances in spatial intelligence do not reliably transfer to quantitative kinematic understanding.
  </details>

- **[Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor: A Predictive Law for Perception Noise and Its Closed-Loop Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658)**  `arXiv:2611.17658`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Christopher Malili_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Imitation policies for robot manipulation are often trained on noisy object positions, and they learn to discount the noisy reading. We show how much they discount it, and why. Because demonstrators steer toward the object, the robot's own state carries information about where the object is. A trained policy combines this proprioceptive anchor with the reading and weights the reading by $\alpha = V/(V + s^2)$, where $s^2$ is the reading noise and $V$ the anchor's error variance. $V$ belongs to the learner and is far smaller than a linear model of the demonstrations implies, which is why classical errors-in-variables (EIV) regression overestimates $\alpha$. Measured once, $V$ predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners and four new noise levels, with mean absolute error 0.025 against 0.37 for classical EIV, and a parameter-free bias test rules out the simpler explanation that the policies just overestimate the noise. The discount does not carry over to deployment, where the policy produces its own state: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded $\alpha$ in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, although the stricter registered prediction, that all would follow the offset fully, held for 8 of them. Post hoc, lower $\alpha$ went with lower success, steeply for diffusion, and a registered prospective test confirmed this for diffusion on Can at two new noise levels: the measured $\alpha$ predicted success before any rollout, within 0.03 (95% intervals $\pm 0.08$), although training noise predicts it equally well. Every test was registered before its data, including the classical benchmark that failed, and all other analyses are labelled post hoc; all results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise.
  </details>

- **[Triage Before Fine-Tuning: Feasibility-Gated Selection and Monitored Adaptation of Embodied Foundation Models for a Fixed Physical Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33772)**  `arXiv:2611.33772`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zilin Huang, Zhengyang Wan, Zihao Sheng, Boyue Wang, Ruolin Yang, Yuhao Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied foundation models are increasingly deployed by selection: a practitioner picks a public checkpoint by benchmark rank and fine-tunes it on local data. The platform is fixed before the model is chosen, with little or no local data. We ask what such a checkpoint loses on a fixed platform and which part of the loss local data can buy back. We split the gap by what can repair it. A sensing gap, information the sensors do not capture, cannot be closed with their data; a latency gap, output arriving after the control loop needs it, belongs to the loop, not the weights; only a distribution gap is fine-tuning's to repair. TRIAGE turns this into feasibility-gated selection and monitored adaptation in five stages: gates on the sensor contract and on end-to-end latency that no score can override, probes on ten minutes of local logs, a ranking by target-relevant score among the candidates that pass, and fine-tuning that is monitored and checked in closed loop before release. On driving benchmarks, a deadline below a leading closed-loop model's latency makes it collide on 80 to 90% of routes, and losing field of view moves plans 2.2 to 5.7 times more than losing resolution at equal pixels. Local frames close 75% of a stability gap but raise perturbation sensitivity at small budgets, and picking by score violates a gate in two of four deployment profiles. On a full-scale vehicle, the factory camera failed the sensing gate and an added camera repaired it. The latency gate removed the two highest-scoring candidates, neither of which completed a closed-loop trip. The selected model passed acceptance in 4 of 5 closed-loop trips, and the closed-loop check withheld a fine-tuned model that had improved offline. TRIAGE lets organizations that cannot train foundation models from scratch find out whether a public checkpoint can run on the robot or vehicle they own, and what to fix if not.
  </details>

- **[Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18359)**  `arXiv:2611.18359`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Grid Robotics, Qingfeng Lan, Duo Li, Siyao Li, Yiheng Lyu, Shijian Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.
  </details>

- **[LUDIUS: A Benchmark for Embodied Social Intelligence in Everyday Human-Robot Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34328)**  `arXiv:2611.34328`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Chaewon Kim, Sangheon Lee, Seyeon Choi, Kangwook Lee, Yea Seul Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social intelligence in a robot requires interpreting a situation from the perspectives of the people involved and using that understanding to decide when and how to act. We introduce LUDIUS, a benchmark for evaluating how vision-language models exercise this judgment through a robot's verbal responses and actions, organized around 15 social intelligence skills drawn from social psychology, sociology, and cognitive science. LUDIUS comprises six editions, each a collection of episodes spanning one simulated week. Three editions each cover all 15 skills in a different household. The other three focus on interactions involving multiple people, safety, and context-specific norms, respectively. We evaluate frontier models and a smaller model finetuned on social-interaction data we generated, using a common evaluation harness. The harness supplies each model with the robot's camera views and residents' reactions, retrieves relevant interaction history, and converts the model's tool calls into robot verbal responses and actions. Models tend to perform worse when they must reason about what a resident saw or heard earlier, reconcile competing interests, or fulfill a request within safety constraints and household rules. Beyond the home setting, LUDIUS's design pipeline offers a way to construct social intelligence benchmarks by translating social intelligence skills into context-specific judgments and observable robot behavior.
  </details>

- **[WorldPhy: Benchmarking Fine-Grained Physical Plausibility of World Models in Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34130)**  `arXiv:2611.34130`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyuan Chu, Mingzhi Zheng, Kaiyue Zhang, Jinan Zhang, Linchao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models are increasingly used in robotics to improve the performance of embodied models. Compared with general-purpose video generation, robotics places more stringent requirements on world models. Generated videos must satisfy fine-grained physical constraints on robot motion and object interactions. However, existing world model benchmarks can overlook robotics specific physical details and struggle to assess them accurately. To address this problem, we introduce WorldPhy, a world model benchmark for accurate, fine-grained evaluation of physical plausibility in robotics. Our benchmark contains 300 tasks, each consisting of an initial-frame image and a task instruction, covering diverse robotics scenes, task categories, viewpoints, and robot configurations. We further collect over 12K high-quality human annotations, and use them to train WorldPhyReward to accurately provide scores and reasons across six fine-grained physical dimensions and an instruction-following dimension. Comparisons with general VLMs and existing reward models show that WorldPhyReward better aligns with human ratings. We system- atically evaluate 13 frontier open and closed-source world models. The results reveal that current world models struggle to consistently maintain fine-grained physical plausibility across different robotics scenarios. Our code is available atthis https URLWorldPhy.
  </details>

- **[Aegis-VLA: Reliability-Gated Action Refinement via a Trajectory World Model for Dynamic Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33993)**  `arXiv:2611.33993`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Lennox Wei, Jiaqi Yao, Shixin Sun, Yuanzheng Zhang, Jianhua Han, Meng Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic object manipulation requires maintaining spatial and temporal alignment with targets that continue to move during inference and execution. Although future prediction can inform action correction, an accurate forecast does not establish whether modifying an existing action would be beneficial. We present Aegis-VLA, a world-model-guided vision-language-action (VLA) framework that generates predictive action corrections and separately determines whether and how strongly to apply them. A Trajectory World Model combines kinematic extrapolation with learned residual dynamics to predict object motion and estimate positional uncertainty. Discrepancy-Guided Action Refinement uses differences between history-only and base-action-conditioned end-effector forecasts to propose bounded arm corrections. A Reliability Gate learns when to admit corrections based on demonstration-relative improvements over base actions, and regulates their application during inference using predictive uncertainty and anticipated object--end-effector proximity. Rejected corrections preserve the corresponding base actions. Aegis-VLA introduces only approximately 2.45M trainable parameters. On the simulated Dynamic Object Manipulation (DOM) benchmark, Aegis-VLA achieves 56.72\% success over 1,800 evaluation trials, exceeding our reproduced DynamicVLA baseline of 45.44\% by 11.28 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[RoboARC: Physical Puzzles for Robotic Manipulation under Spatial Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33390)**  `arXiv:2611.33390`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Qineng Wang, Jianwen Lyu, Pingyue Zhang, Reuben Tan, Xiaoyu Huang, Tonghe Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many hard manipulation tasks come down to a spatial constraint that only a few motions satisfy, such as passing a part through a gap barely wider than itself or separating two interlocked parts. These motions depend on the exact geometry of each scene, so demonstrations and skills recorded elsewhere rarely transfer. We therefore ask: when manipulation hinges on spatial constraints, can robots reason out the right motions? We introduce RoboARC, a benchmark of 90 tasks in six constraint types over rigid and soft materials, on three robots in MuJoCo. Every task is a frozen scene with a physics-based grader and a verified reference solution that replays bit-identically. Each task comes in a Privileged regime, with the full simulator state and resets, and a Standard regime with real-sensor inputs (camera images and proprioception) and a single attempt without resets. We evaluate seven coding agents and four VLA policies, none trained on these tasks. The strongest agent, GPT-6 Astra in Codex, solves 86% of the tasks in the Privileged regime and 43% in the Standard regime, and the four VLA policies, run zero-shot, solve none of their 18 one-arm puzzles. On 15 puzzles that GPT-6 Astra solved in the Privileged regime and failed in the Standard regime, object poses add 4 points and part geometry 13, but both together add 36, from 24% to 60%. Only 7% of the coding agents' failed runs pass more than half of a task, and 53% of the failed runs in the Standard regime are stuck on a single step. Frontier coding agents already reason out the right motions for many of these tasks once they know where the parts are and what shape they have. From their own sensors, in a single attempt, the strongest solves fewer than half of the tasks. The website can be found atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[RoboTeam: Benchmarking Vision-Based Multi-Agent Control of Many Robot Arms in Real Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22138)**  `arXiv:2611.22138`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Changxuan Fan, Zhongwei Xie, Boyi Zhang, Shaoli Hu, Chang Xu, Xiyu Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents can now control robot arms by calling tools and writing code. Existing benchmarks fall short in three ways: most test one or two arms on short tasks, many pause the world while the agent thinks, and many give it ground-truth object positions. We present RoboTeam, a simulated benchmark where agents control 4 to 16 robot arms in real time from camera images alone. Its 36 tasks are long jobs that the arms must do together, such as passing timbers or building a picture from hundreds of cubes. We start from the most flexible agent system, the adaptive system, where one agent decides when to fork delegates. With it, gpt-6.1-sol and opus-5.5 complete 28 and 27 tasks, and four other models at most 3. But at a high price: a typical task takes gpt-6.1-sol 3.5 hours and 218 million input tokens, and a third of its steps fail at least once and must be retried. We then compare it with three fixed systems: single-agent, hierarchical, and decentralized. All four complete a similar number of tasks but differ in time and tokens. The hierarchical and decentralized systems are faster but use up to 1.9 times the tokens, and the single agent uses fewer tokens but takes 1.6 times as long, so the adaptive system lies between them. Even so, no model or system is yet fast, cheap, and reliable at once. RoboTeam measures these limits where earlier benchmarks cannot: long multi-arm jobs, camera images only, and a world that never pauses.
  </details>

- **[Toward Agentic Robotics: A Large-Scale Study of Harness Design for Robot-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34778)**  `arXiv:2611.34778`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuxiang Lai, Jike Zhong, Yu-Jhe Li, Lan Wang, Chengzhi Liu, Youbao Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent vision-language models (VLMs) can act as robot-use agents that understand diverse scenes and control robots through policy code and tools. However, they predict 3D gripper poses poorly even with extended reasoning, struggling with precise manipulation. Vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action (WAM) models are the opposite: they execute fast, smooth control on trained tasks, yet fail under domain shifts or when tasks require long-horizon memory and reasoning. Agentic robots therefore combine VLM reasoning and perception with precise VLA/WAM control through a policy harness. Yet harness design spans thousands of configurations, shifting success by tens of points with the same models. Moreover, testing even one design takes thousands of rollouts at high API and GPU cost, so prior works often commit to a single design chosen from few alternatives. To guide harness design, we evaluate over 470,000 rollouts across eight simulation benchmarks, six VLMs, and 15 VLA/WAM checkpoints. Seven controlled studies show effective harnesses must adapt to both VLM and VLA/WAM capabilities. Our findings specify what structure the VLM's policy code needs, where VLA manipulation needs help from tools and policy code, and when to involve the VLM at test time. The reference recipe X-Harness shows that these findings compose without weight updates, lifting base policies up to $+42.8$ points across memory, reasoning, and domain shifts.
  </details>

- **[ELMO: Emergent Representations In Imitation Learning Via Dynamic Hierarchical Action Chunking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34741)**  `arXiv:2611.34741`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jasper Gerigk, Yang Xu, Nicholas Rhinehart, Igor Gilitschenski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot trajectories naturally decompose into variable-length phases with distinct semantic intents, such as reaching, grasping, and lifting. However, training policies to minimize MSE treats all actions as equal, obscuring their semantic structure. While latent action models attempt to create a structured prediction target by compressing action chunks into latent representations, existing approaches rely on two-stage training and context-blind decoders. Consequently, their representations are context-agnostic motion codes (e.g., "move down") which fail to capture behavioural intent (e.g., "pick up the block"). To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce the Emergent Latent-action MOdel (ELMO), an end-to-end framework that encodes robot trajectories as grounded semantic codes -- representations that ground task-level behavioural intent in the current environment. ELMO jointly trains a VLA planner and an observation-conditioned decoder over dynamically chunked trajectories, learning a semantically grounded action space without separate pretraining. Across standard benchmarks, ELMO outperforms comparable prior methods by 9.6 percentage points on LIBERO-Plus and by 0.75 in average task length on CALVIN. Furthermore, on Blocks -- our shortcut-free simulation and real-world generalization benchmark -- ELMO reaches a 38.6% success rate while all baselines remain below 5%, demonstrating its ability to capture the semantic structure of demonstrations. Website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Nav4Manip: From Navigation to Dexterous Mobile Manipulation through Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34655)**  `arXiv:2611.34655`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yusen Feng, Zixi Kang, Zhijun Yin, Changxiao Liu, Tianhong Gao, Jiazhao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous mobile manipulation is essential for robots to perform useful tasks in complex, unstructured environments. However, existing systems face two challenges. First, navigation remains too coarse for downstream manipulation, which often requires a precise base position and approach orientation rather than merely reaching a meter-scale goal region. Second, navigation and manipulation are commonly trained in isolation, lacking a feedback loop that enables the two capabilities to co-evolve. These limitations prevent current systems from reliably executing long-horizon dexterous mobile-manipulation tasks in real-world environments. To address them, we introduce Nav4Manip, a bidirectionally coupled co-evolution system for long-horizon dexterous mobile manipulation. It comprises two complementary architectures: Nav4Manip-N learns long-horizon navigation and refines its policy through manipulation feedback, while Nav4Manip-M trains a world-action model (WAM) for downstream dexterous manipulation. We train Nav4Manip-N in three stages: multi-task co-tuning learns navigation behavior and manipulation-aware affordance prediction; rejection sampling fine-tuning (RFT) rejects low-quality policy-generated paths and fine-tunes the model on retained trajectories; and reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) uses counterfactual preferences to further improve navigation decisions. To support this framework, we establish the Nav-N-Manip benchmark and construct Nav4Manip-Pairs from benchmark rollouts, providing large-scale paired navigation--manipulation trajectories and affordance supervision. Extensive experiments on public vision-language navigation (VLN) benchmarks and Nav-N-Manip demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, while real-world evaluations show that Nav4Manip completes diverse dexterous mobile-manipulation tasks. Project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Task Update Benchmark (DTU-Bench): Evaluating Runtime Task Adaptation in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34538)**  `arXiv:2611.34538`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yanran Jiang, Cécile Paris, Dana Kulić, Leimin Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) policies perform well on fixed-instruction robotic manipulation benchmarks, but their ability to adapt to instruction changes during execution remains understudied. We introduce the Dynamic Task Update Benchmark (DTU-Bench), evaluating 40 task revisions across seven update types and three execution stages using LIBERO, a simulated robotic manipulation benchmark. We compare performance when a new instruction is given before execution starts versus during execution, and examine whether the new task instructions are followed. Across 13 representative VLA configurations, average task success is 95.8% on the original object-manipulation tasks, compared with only 1.6% when the instruction changes which object to manipulate during execution. Controlled diagnostics show that policies often complete the original task before the updated one, and that their responses depend on the robot's physical state. We further test whether a simple scripted controller that releases the grasped object and moves the arm back helps robots respond to instruction changes. This improves success from 2% to 38% for {\pi}0.5 and from 8% to 48% for DM0.5, but can cause unwanted object motion. These findings reveal limitations that fixed-instruction benchmarks and task-success metrics alone can overlook in evaluating robotic manipulation. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[An Exact Branch-Price-and-Cut Algorithm for the Vehicle Routing Problem with Floating Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34490)**  `arXiv:2611.34490`  `cs.RO`  
  _Michał Paradowski, Anoop Bhat, Zhongqiang Ren, Howie Choset_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Vehicle Routing Problem with Floating Targets (VRPFT) is a generalization of the capacitated vehicle routing problem where targets may actively move to locations ''convenient'' for the vehicles. In particular, each target may leave its home location at a bounded speed. A solution to the VRPFT is a sequence of targets for each vehicle to visit and a meeting point in space-time for each target. In this paper, we solve the VRPFT exactly with a branch-price-and-cut algorithm. Our key contribution is a dominance criterion for the pricing labeling algorithm that utilizes closed-form lower and upper bounds on a vehicle's trajectory cost given a fixed sequence of targets. We prove that the criterion preserves optimality, and the algorithm has smaller computation time than the exact dynamic program of prior work on identical hardware. We also provide a relaxed criterion that prunes more aggressively; the resulting heuristic is faster than our exact algorithm and matched every certified optimum on the benchmark.
  </details>

- **[SkySearcher: High-Altitude Onboard VLM Reasoning for Aerial Semantic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34485)**  `arXiv:2611.34485`  `cs.RO`  
  _Giacomo Vianello, David Morilla-Cabello, Juan Dendarieta Serrano, Ruggero Carli, Eduardo Montijano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have improved semantic and visual reasoning, enabling UAVs to search for open-vocabulary queries in unstructured environments at high altitude. However, existing systems mainly follow low-altitude strategies, focusing on close-range iterative exploration, requiring storage-intensive dense 3D metric-semantic reconstructions or relying on large cloud-serviced VLMs assuming constant internet access. We propose SkySearcher, an aerial semantic search navigation method designed for large outdoor scenes. SkySearcher integrates onboard VLM reasoning with a lightweight probabilistic representation that guides an information-driven exploration. The VLM extracts semantic information at high altitude about the target or cues informing about its location. This information is projected to the ground using the camera frustum instead of limited-depth sensors, actively exploiting the expansive field of view offered by elevated sensing. The information regarding the presence or absence of semantic cues is then integrated into a probabilistic map by modeling the spatial belief of the query, maintaining a task-driven memory for the mission and guiding the exploration towards semantically promising regions. Extensive simulations for Aerial Object Goal Navigation on public benchmarks demonstrate that SkySearcher significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in success rate, flight time, and navigation precision, while a deployment of SkySercher running fully onboard a drone demonstrates the applicability of our approach.
  </details>

- **[HoloBrain-0.5 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34449)**  `arXiv:2611.34449`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xuewu Lin, Jiahao Wang, Jiawei Li, Mengdi Li, Zhengmao Sun, Yiwei Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present HoloBrain-0.5, a family of vision-language-action (VLA) models for manipulation across robot embodiments. The policies plan in a shared action space using robot state, kinematic structure, and calibrated camera observations. We standardize joint axes, coordinate frames, and tool-center points in robot descriptions (URDFs) and encode their kinematic graphs in a shared 3D representation. HoloBrain-0.5-Continuous generates action chunks by diffusion, while HoloBrain-0.5-Discrete uses residual finite scalar quantization (FSQ) and delayed autoregression. We pretrain both policies on over 10,000 hours of real-robot, simulation, and human-manipulation data spanning 15 embodiment categories.Across six closed-loop simulation settings, the discrete policy converges faster and reaches a higher mean success rate than the continuous policy (56.77% vs. 49.71%). On seven real-robot tasks, it achieves a mean progress score of 87.76% without task-specific post-training. After post-training, the continuous policy outperforms $\pi_{0.5}$ on the seven-task benchmark (94.52% vs. 86.25%) and an 11-task dual-arm benchmark (79.07% vs. 69.41%). The discrete-initialized continuous policy transfers five tasks zero-shot from Piper to Piper-X, scoring 63.28% on average. With target-robot demonstrations, the continuous policy scores 89.2% on Franka Panda and 92.94% on a mobile dual-arm platform. Although pretraining includes no Unitree G1 data, post-training with limited G1 demonstrations yields a mean progress score of 70.24% across three humanoid tasks, compared with 11.00% for $\pi_{0.5}$.
  </details>

- **[Ground Your Steps: Optimizing Sampling Schedules in Flow Matching Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34148)**  `arXiv:2611.34148`  `cs.RO`  
  _Licheng Yang, Yonghao He, Lingfeng Qian, Xin Shi, Wei Sui, Hu Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow matching robot policies generate action chunks by integrating a learned velocity field at inference. However, under a fixed budget of neural function evaluations (NFEs), the integration result depends on the sampling schedule. This motivates optimizing the schedule to bring the generated action distribution closer to the demonstrations and improve task success. We propose Ground Your Steps (GYS), a training-free method that optimizes the sampling schedule by matching the generated and demonstrated chunk distributions at each observation. GYS applies truncated fractional differencing along each arm joint's action sequence and compares two types of pairwise differences between action chunks at each observation: differences of a generated chunk against a demonstration, and differences of two independent generated chunks against each other. After fractional differencing, these two pairwise-difference distributions coincide exactly when the distributions of generated and demonstrated action chunks match. GYS minimizes the energy distance between these distributions to obtain an improved schedule for each task and NFE budget. Compared with the uniform grid, GYS brings the generated chunk distribution closer to the demonstrated distribution and raises the success rate through the sampling schedule alone. On RoboTwin 2.0 benchmarks, GYS improves success rates at nearly every budget, with gains of 2.8 percentage points for both $\pi_{0.5}$ and GR00T N1.7. The gains also transfer to three real-world manipulation tasks, reaching up to 3.3 percentage points. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WAtcher: Watching Ahead for Safe Robotic Manipulation with Efficient World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34124)**  `arXiv:2611.34124`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ziyu Shan, Xiao Ma, Kangrui Yu, Changjie Wang, Gaoyuan Wu, Zaijia Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models (WAMs) couple future imagination with action generation, but they execute every action chunk while ignoring the potential risk. To fill this gap, we present WAtcher, a safety-aware world-action framework in which the policy imagines the outcome of the candidate chunk before execution. For fast future preview, WAtcher builds on a compact WAM whose video expert imagines the visual outcome in only two denoising steps. In addition, we propose Safe-Guard, a lightweight module kept outside the WAM that scores each candidate chunk from its imagined outcome and triggers a bounded retreat when the risk is high. Safe-Guard is detachable and can be applied to other WAMs. To train Safe-Guard, we build an autonomous pipeline to collect hazard data from RoboTwin, LIBERO, and a real robot. Specifically, the pipeline perturbs expert demonstrations and labels each candidate chunk from the physical events recorded when it is executed, such as contacts and losses of support. Using the ~25,000 labeled windows from this pipeline, Safe-Guard reaches 92.1% balanced accuracy on a blind set, and cuts hazardous closed-loop trials on training tasks from 27.0% to 10.8% without lowering task success. On a held-out 300-question benchmark, it scores 94.2%, against 72.5% and 63.3% for zero-shot GPT-6-Astra and Claude Opus 5.5. WAtcher also reaches 90.4% and 82.4% average success on RoboTwin 2.0 and ManiSkill3, on par with or above leading WAMs, and 74.0% on five real-world tasks with Safe-Guard on, against 59.5% for $\pi_{0.5}$. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[$\widetilde{\varepsilon}$: Does Initial Noise Induce Directional Bias in Vision-Language-Action Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33816)**  `arXiv:2611.33816`  `cs.RO`  
  _Junmyeong Lee, Dongmin Shin, Wooseok Jeon, JunGyu Lee, Seunghyun Shin, Hae-Gon Jeon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action models (VLAs) have widely adopted diffusion-based action generation for continuous robot motions, enabling fine-grained control across diverse tasks. However, recent studies have shown that their performance is highly sensitive to initial noise, with resulting action errors that make recovery difficult during execution. Existing approaches search for favorable noise initializations to improve performance, yet the action expert itself remains sensitive to noise. In this work, we reveal that initial noise induces a consistent directional bias in action space that closely aligns with the noise's temporal mean. We thus introduce a simple yet effective framework that improves robustness to initial noise through interventions during both training and inference. During training, we enforce consistent action predictions from antithetic noise pairs $(\epsilon, -\epsilon)$ and, at inference, remove the noise's temporal mean. Our method substantially reduces action deviation and improves task performance across simulation benchmarks. In real-robot experiments, our method increases the average task success rate of $\pi_{0.5}$ from 40% to 68%, with more stable and consistent execution.
  </details>

- **[FailPoint-X: A Policy-Matched, Failure-Anchored Benchmark for Offline VLA Post-Training from Human Takeovers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33769)**  `arXiv:2611.33769`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zirui Li, Yujie Yan, Jingxuan Qian, Yangang Ren, Haizhou Yin, Haoran Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human takeovers offer corrective supervision for vision-language-action (VLA) policies, but their contribution to offline post-training must be separated from that of continued optimization. We introduce FailPoint-X, a benchmark for offline VLA post-training with human takeovers. To represent uncertainty in failure onset, an annotator marks an interval for each failure, and training takeovers start at seeded frames sampled within it. From one fixed $\pi_{0.5}$ checkpoint, which also initializes post-training, we assemble 629 natural failures across 10 LIBERO-X task families at up to five difficulty levels. All 2,823 takeover attempts are logged; 2,400 are accepted recoveries, of which 2,320 are used for post-training. Evaluation restarts every method from the fixed midpoints of 384 held-out failure intervals. Under matched actor-update counts, we compare behavior cloning of recoveries, value-based offline learning on recoveries and failed rollouts, and continued training on existing demonstrations. Within 1,200 steps on the 240 L1-L3 held-out failures, cloning improves recovery by 29.4 percentage points (pp) over the demonstration-only control; the value-based learner does not establish a further gain (1.5 pp). In exploratory evaluation from L1-L5 nominal starts at the same horizon, cloning loses 30.0 pp of success relative to the base policy; mixing in demonstrations reduces the loss to 16.5 pp, a reduction that shrinks under a stricter, pre-specified horizon. Restorable failure states and a shared evaluator let all methods be compared from identical starts.
  </details>

- **[$\mathrm{M}^{2}$-WAM: Efficient Hierarchical Memory Across Modalities and Timescales for World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33710)**  `arXiv:2611.33710`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xiaopeng Zhang, Weiqi Yuan, Xin Zhu, Jiaxi Huang, Yueqi Hu, Yin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon robotic manipulation requires historical context, yet retaining extensive visual histories incurs growing storage and retrieval costs, while aggressive compression risks losing fine-grained information needed for subsequent decisions. Moreover, executed action histories capture intermediate behaviors that sparse visual observations may miss. Preserving this complementary context efficiently calls for modality-specific memory mechanisms that balance recent detail with long-term retention. To this end, we introduce $\mathrm{M}^{2}$-WAM, a world-action model equipped with hierarchical visual and interaction memories. $\mathrm{M}^{2}$-WAM preserves recent visual observations in dense short-term memory while consolidating entries evicted from this window into fixed-size associative long-term memory through gated delta updates. Within the same short- and long-term hierarchy, interaction memory encodes executed action prefixes into compact temporal representations, complementing visual observations with behavioral context. These complementary memories are integrated into world-action modeling through layer-wise retrieval, enabling memory-augmented action generation with a bounded online memory footprint independent of episode length. On the memory-dependent simulation benchmarks MIKASA-Robo and RMBench, $\mathrm{M}^{2}$-WAM achieves +53.2% and +61.2% absolute improvements in average success rates over Fast-WAM, respectively. These gains extend to real-world manipulation, where $\mathrm{M}^{2}$-WAM achieves a +29.3% absolute improvement over LingBot-VA in average success across three tasks. These results suggest that $\mathrm{M}^{2}$-WAM offers a promising approach to efficient memory-augmented world-action modeling.
  </details>

- **[DFLAT: Model-based Diffusion on Differentially Flat Spaces for Long-Horizon Trajectory Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33586)**  `arXiv:2611.33586`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ege Yuceel, Taekyung Kim, Keyvan Majd, Hideki Okamoto, Bardh Hoxha, Sayan Mitra, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon diffusion-based planning for kinodynamically constrained systems requires both scalability and feasibility, yet existing diffusion planners typically sacrifice one for the other. We present DFLAT, a diffusion-based planning algorithm that operates directly on a differentially flat trajectory representation. Instead of diffusing over time-discretized controls, DFLAT denoises a compact set of Hermite curve parameters in the flat-output space, consisting of intermediate waypoints together with the endpoint tangents of each segment. Every denoised sample maps to a dynamically consistent trajectory, so feasibility holds by construction rather than being restored afterward. Experiments on challenging tractor-trailer planning benchmarks show that DFLAT matches or exceeds the success rates of existing baselines at consistently lower planning times, particularly on long-horizon problems requiring multiple forward-reverse maneuvers. In cases where kinodynamic RRT requires over 100 seconds, DFLAT plans in subsecond time, enabling practical deployment.
  </details>

- **[RESET: One Demonstration, One Verified World, Self-Improved Skills Across Embodiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31894)**  `arXiv:2611.31894`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zheng Sun, Zhuo Li, Zhihao Li, Hengyi Sim, Ge Wang, Ziyue Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) coding agents can iteratively self-improve robot manipulation skills in simulation benchmarks, yet real-world tasks provide neither ground-truth success scorers nor resettable practice environments. Our key observation is that a single real demonstration simultaneously specifies scene geometry, defines the terminal success criterion, and serves as a physical reference trajectory for verifying world fidelity---all decoupled from the source robot's kinematics. We present {RESET}: we reconstruct a contact-rich world and derive a frozen read-only criterion from one demonstration ({R}ebuild); check that world against the demonstration by open-loop replay and silhouette consistency ({E}xamine); evolve modular white-box task graphs and skill code from per-node failure evidence ({S}elf-improve); deploy frozen code on real robots with zero runtime LLM calls ({E}xecute); and share the verified world and criterion with unseen robots without new demonstrations ({T}ransfer). On 8 manipulation tasks, each rebuilt from one real demonstration, RESET achieves 97% average success on unseen layouts in simulation, whereas zero-shot code reaches 27%, per-task fine-tuned pi0.5 policies 31%, and Diffusion Policy 50%; injecting reconstruction defects shows that practice tolerates many reconstruction errors, that geometric errors can still stall or mislead it, and that the check flags most of them while physical randomization covers the rest. Without any target demonstrations, 3 heterogeneous robots acquire the same task skills in the shared world: a robot with similar arms copies 95% of its skill functions unchanged from the source robot, while robots with different arms and hands write their own. In real-robot experiments, the resulting programs run on 4 robots and succeed in 80% of 280 trials.
  </details>

- **[PrimeSim: A Generalist World Model Simulator from Large-Scale Cross-Embodiment Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04870)**  `arXiv:2611.04870`  `cs.RO`  
  _Travis Davies, Wentao Ma, Lihao Guo, Tengfei Ma, Xiaowen Chang, Jiafeng Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot world model that serves as a simulator must do more than generate plausible videos: it must faithfully follow commanded actions, maintain consistency across camera views, and sustain coherent long-horizon rollouts. We present PrimeSim, an action-conditioned, multiview world model simulator that addresses these challenges through progressive midtraining and posttraining. Starting from a pretrained video generator, we midtrain on 30,110 hours of robot demonstrations spanning 22 platforms. To improve action following, we introduce a unified cross-embodiment action representation and frame-aligned dual-path conditioning to strengthen action-frame correspondence. To maintain multiview consistency, we develop view-aware interleaved attention with explicit camera identities, enabling cross-view information exchange while preserving view-specific representations. We further introduce a fast-video, slow-memory posttraining framework that transforms the bidirectional video generator into an efficient autoregressive simulator. By combining fast causal generation, self-rollout training, and hierarchical temporal memory, PrimeSim mitigates error accumulation from self-generated histories and supports coherent long-horizon simulation. PrimeSim runs at 10.1 FPS, remains stable over long-horizon rollouts on rigid and deformable manipulation tasks, and transfers to held-out platforms within 100 to 200 gradient steps where the pretrained backbone does not. Against five open-source action-conditioned world models it leads four of the five WorldSimProbe suites and posts the best overall EWMBench score, while fine-grained local calibration still trails the field; its rollouts are faithful enough to support GRPO-based policy optimization. We release the model weights, inference code, and training code.
  </details>

- **[Recovering the View: Benchmarking Physical Active Vision for Occlusion Recovery in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37292)**  `arXiv:2609.37292`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kaijun Luo, Yudi Huang, Qijun Zhong, Xinshuai Song, Yang Liu, Liang Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical active vision allows robots to change their viewpoint when task-relevant observations become unreliable. However, existing manipulation benchmarks provide limited support for studying how policies recover from occlusion during execution. We introduce BAVO-Bench, a bimanual active-vision benchmark that systematically controls external visibility through Clean, Stage Occlusion, and Random-time Occlusion conditions. We further introduce Camera-attributable Visibility Gain (CVG), a counterfactual metric that isolates visibility improvements attributable to camera motion rather than scene dynamics. Building on this setting, we present A-FAR, a policy for joint viewpoint and manipulation control. A-FAR combines robot-centric 3D observations with spatial context encoding to model geometric relationships consistently across viewpoints. It further leverages future 3D motion supervision from a pretrained 4D model to capture how task-relevant scene geometry evolves during manipulation. Experiments in simulation and the real world demonstrate improved robustness to structured and temporally unexpected occlusions, while maintaining strong performance under clean observations. Further analyses reveal that recovering visibility alone does not guarantee task success, highlighting the importance of translating recovered visual information into effective manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Training-free Behavior Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30134)**  `arXiv:2609.30134`  `cs.RO`  
  _Maximilian Adang, Timothy Chen, Lars Osterberg, Aiden Swann, Mac Schwager_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural behavior cloning compresses demonstrations into large models, making individual actions difficult to trace and policy updates costly. Retrieval policies retain access to demonstrations but struggle with mismatch between recorded and live behavior. We introduce Behavior Predictive Control (BPC), which synthesizes policies without end-to-end policy training by combining an action-aware retrieval metric, a Hankel-based action-continuation prior, and a closed-form one-step residual correction. Inspired by behavioral systems theory, BPC predicts future actions by blending stored observation-action data that best reconstructs the recent runtime observation--action history. Across simulated benchmarks and real-robot deployments, BPC is competitive with learned policies such as $\pi_{0.5}$ (surpassing it in some cases), while reducing policy fitting from hours to seconds on consumer GPUs and supporting closed-loop control upwards of 75 Hz on a Jetson Orin Nano. The retrieved demonstration windows and their coefficients also provide an intrinsic estimate of task progress. Retaining demonstrations within the deployed policy makes its predictions traceable to supporting trajectories and enables behavior revision through the demonstration bank.
  </details>

- **[CypherMAS: Benchmarking Routing Reliability in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33242)**  `arXiv:2611.33242`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Danil Kostromin, Andrey Sakhovskiy, Semen Budennyy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) distribute reasoning across specialized agents that must exchange messages and invoke tools reliably. Existing evaluations typically couple communication with open domain tasks, making it difficult to determine whether a failure arises from reasoning, malformed tool use, lost messages, or forgotten requests. Prior benchmarks do not isolate communication execution or measure routing redundancy against a known optimal path. We introduce CypherMAS, a synthetic benchmark that isolates communication and routing in fixed multi-agent graphs. Agents in chain, star, bipartite, or ring graphs route cyphered messages to remote peers holding the matching decoders; since the optimal route is known, redundant LLM calls can be measured and routing failures may be easily tracked. Across eleven open-weight models from 4B to 753B, small models fail at routing and tool use, dense models above 27B degrade with diameter, and successful runs exceed minimum LLM calls. These findings identify communication reliability as a distinct scaling bottleneck for multi-agent systems and provide a controlled diagnostic for complex multi-agent systems. The source code is freely available:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Are Parallel Subagents Effective for Coding? An Empirical Study on Repository Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33893)**  `arXiv:2611.33893`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiheng Lyu, Chengyu Yin, Lingming Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based coding agents are increasingly used for long-horizon tasks such as building an entire program from a specification. Products like Codex and Claude Code let an agent delegate work to parallel subagents, multiplying run cost, yet it remains unclear whether and when delegation produces a better program. We compare a single agent with a main agent delegating to subagents on repository-construction tasks from NL2Repo and ProgramBench (which also provides an executable reference program). We compare scores at equal time and equal cost, run intervention experiments, and analyze fine-grained behavior. On randomly drawn tasks, parallel subagents raise hidden-test pass rates by only about 1 to 5 percentage points at up to 3.8x the cost of a single agent. Their gain is larger on hard tasks, where we find evidence of three mechanisms. Self-checking: with GPT-5.6 Sol, one sentence telling a single agent to probe the reference program first recovers most of this gain at a fraction of the cost; from GPT-5.6 Sol to GPT-6.1 Sol, the gain shrinks as single agents check their own work more. Fresh context: in extended runs, a single agent that periodically starts a new session with an empty conversation makes faster early progress in every comparison than one that resumes its conversation. Modularity: multi-agent runs produce more modular code (more files and shorter functions), closer to the upstream source. We observe similar patterns in a smaller replication with Opus 5.5 on Claude Code. Overall, subagents help when (i) a single agent stops checking too early, (ii) a long session keeps editing with little improvement, or (iii) modular code helps on the task; otherwise, they mainly add cost.
  </details>

- **[GRASP: Graph Agentic Search over Propositions for Multi-hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16598)**  `arXiv:2605.16598`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Stockton Jenkins, Ramya Korlakai Vinayak, Junjie Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic retrieval improves multi-hop question answering by giving language models autonomy to iteratively gather evidence. Recent work augments these systems with knowledge graphs for structured traversal, but this combination introduces significant cost: expensive graph construction at index time and compounding token usage at inference time. We introduce Graph Agentic Search over Propositions (GRASP), an agentic system that simultaneously optimizes for high accuracy and minimal token usage in multi-hop question answering. Rather than executing a rigid, singular query, GRASP actively coordinates its retrieval strategy by decomposing multi-hop queries into dependency-aware plans. This enables GRASP to dynamically scale the number of sub-agents according to the complexity of the problem. Each sub-agent resolves its single-hop query by exploring a novel three-layer hierarchical graph of entities, propositions, and passages, using the entity layer for targeted traversal and the proposition layer for high-recall passage retrieval via reciprocal-rank voting. We evaluate GRASP on MuSiQue, 2WikiMultihopQA, and HotpotQA under two settings: open-corpus retrieval and extended context reasoning (LongBench). GRASP achieves the highest QA accuracy in the open retrieval setting on MuSiQue and 2Wiki while using 40-50 percent fewer tokens than IRCoT+HippoRAG2. Furthermore, GRASP leads on EM and F1 across all three datasets in the LongBench setting while using 30 percent fewer tokens than the next most accurate method. Finally, we introduce success economy - the amortized token cost per correct answer, weighted by difficulty - and advocate for efficiency-aware evaluation as a standard practice for agentic QA.
  </details>

- **[Strong Starts, Simple Recipes: Unified Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation from Multimodal Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34397)**  `arXiv:2611.34397`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Wenzheng Chang, Taiqiang Wu, Yuqi Xu, Yongfu Zhu, Zelin Peng, Ji Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) integrates separately trained domain specialists into a single unified model. Existing methods focus on refining the distillation procedure while initializing the student from a base model or a multi-domain SFT checkpoint, which leaves capability integration largely to distillation itself. Instead, we treat the starting point as a design variable and propose Unified MOPD (Uni-MOPD), which pairs a strong start with a simple recipe: a training-free uniform merge of specialists from the same base model initializes the student, and short MOPD with uniform domain sampling and full-vocabulary reverse KL refines it. Across five multimodal domains and 19 benchmarks, our study establishes three findings. (1) The starting point matters more than subsequent training choices: before any training, the uniform merge already matches the score that the base model reaches after 200 MOPD updates, and after short distillation it matches higher-scoring, search-based starts. (2) Short MOPD recovers most of the capabilities the merge leaves unintegrated; it improves every domain, with the largest gains where the domain teacher still leads. (3) From a strong start, uniform domain sampling and full-vocabulary reverse KL match or exceed every evaluated sampling strategy and divergence, without schedules, per-token weighting, or truncation hyperparameters. Uni-MOPD outperforms Open-MOPD at the same training budget while training 3.3 times faster than its released code. We will release the training framework, specialist checkpoints, and unified student.
  </details>

- **[DLaR: Dynamic Stochasticity Control for Latent Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33962)**  `arXiv:2611.33962`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaocheng Luo, Peng Xu, Zaifu Zhan, Xiangyu Duan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent reasoning enables LLMs to operate in continuous embedding space, yet existing methods suffer from deterministic inference that precludes exploration of diverse reasoning paths. While recent approaches inject stochasticity to restore exploration, we identify two fundamental limitations: \textit{uniform stochasticity} across all steps is suboptimal, and unconstrained perturbations disrupt reasoning coherence. We propose Dynamic Stochasticity Control for Latent Reasoning (DLaR), a framework with two complementary mechanisms that operate at different temporal granularities within the GRPO reinforcement learning framework. At the token level, Adaptive Stochasticity Modulation (ASM) dynamically adjusts perturbation intensity at each latent step based on a confidence-entropy fusion signal, increasing exploration when the model is uncertain and preserving stability when confident. At the training level, Training-Aware Stochasticity Tuning (TAST) progressively decays the base stochasticity level following a curriculum schedule. Through this dual-level design, DLaR establishes a hierarchical exploration-exploitation strategy that operates at both token and training levels. Extensive experiments on four in-distribution and three out-of-distribution mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that DLaR achieves the highest average performance across model scales, reaching 27.9%/50.6% Pass@1/Pass@8 on Llama-3.2-1B and 46.8%/68.2% on Qwen2.5-1.5B. Code: \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Looped Models are Fast Multi-Token Predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33896)**  `arXiv:2611.33896`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ayhan Suleymanzade, Sean McLeish, Sehoon Kim, Jinwoo Kim, Antoine Bosselut_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models can adapt computation for predicting a token without a proportional rescaling in unique model parameters by adjusting the number of loops. This property has been used for self-speculative decoding, taking shallow loops as fast drafters and deep loops as verifiers, which is effective since drafting still uses full parameters. In this paper, we show that shallow loops can be also predictive of several future tokens and leverage this to drastically accelerate the inference of looped language models. To that end, we present Floodgates, a simple self-speculative decoding method that appends a block of mask tokens as a suffix and learns via low-rank adaptation to draft these tokens in parallel. Across seven models, five benchmarks, a range of batch sizes, and both greedy and sampled decoding, Floodgates is the fastest lossless decoding method in most cases, attaining up to 7.5x acceleration. Through analysis, we show that this speedup is enabled by all-depth training of looped models, with the shallow drafter's ability to leverage full-depth computation by conditioning on deep prefix cache evaluated during verification.
  </details>

- **[State Space Attention: Learning Context-Dependent Memory Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33625)**  `arXiv:2611.33625`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ege Çakar, Cengiz Pehlevan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard attention's key-value cache grows linearly with context length. Bounding it raises the question of what a sequence model should retain for long-context reasoning. We argue this choice should be learned, and introduce State Space Attention (SSA), which learns to keep a bounded set of past representations and reads them with standard attention. A learned scorer rescores every chunk against newly arriving context and the rest of memory. A fixed-context Transformer is then the special case that always keeps the most recent tokens. We train the scorer and reader jointly, either moving gradually from a differentiable soft weighting of memories to hard retention, or with hard retention from the start. To produce signal for the scorer, we generate supervised samples during training, a technique we call recovery. We compare SSA with recurrent models, bounded-memory attention methods, and full-attention Transformers across retrieval, variable tracking, multi-hop reasoning, and language modeling. On our retrieval and reasoning benchmarks, SSA matches or comes within a few points of full attention on most tasks, far outperforms a Transformer with the same readable budget on every long-context retrieval task we evaluate, and matches or outperforms one with about 4 times that budget, while using constant inference memory. The largest remaining gap to full attention at small language model scale is on RULER. SSA makes a fixed number of tokens useful over a longer context, and since it reads memory with standard attention, we propose that it can be stacked with KV cache eviction methods. We also pretrain and post-train 400M-parameter models and measure how SSA's training and inference costs scale with context length and model size. Together, these experiments examine the capabilities and limitations of learned memory retention for long-context sequence modeling.
  </details>

- **[Lowering the Bar for Everyone: Hidden Validation-Seeking Goals in Empathetic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30702)**  `arXiv:2611.30702`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinche Zhang, Shanghong Xie, Zhiyuan Ma, Jinhao Li, Sen Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When RL learns from the reactions of many users, the policy usually cannot see what each user wants. We ask what it learns from users who privately want validation: to validate them alone (targeting), or to lower its bar for endorsing everyone (a threshold shift). We answer this in one setting, empathetic RL against LLM-simulated users (RLVER, GRPO, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct): we vary only the share of validation-seeking users (0--50%; RLVER's default: 24.6%) and evaluate on 1,000 Reddit AITA posts with community fault verdicts.The policy shifts its threshold. Endorsement of at-fault posters rises by 3--4 percentage points per 10 points of share; without such users the Qwen model stays at base, and the effect persists at matched KL and under a goal-blind reward. Signal-detection analysis shows that the criterion for endorsing falls while discrimination between at-fault and not-at-fault posters does not decline. An explicit request to be told if one is wrong gives no measurable protection: at-fault posters who make it receive nearly the same rise as those who do not (+0.11 vs. +0.13). When the goal is stated during training, the policy instead largely targets, endorsing seekers regardless of fault.Standard sycophancy and empathy benchmarks miss the shift; five of six usable released empathetic-RL checkpoints also endorse at-fault posters who ask for honesty more than their base models, though a pre-registered prediction for two held for only one. The user mix is a training hyperparameter to audit and report.
  </details>

- **[Do We Still Need Reinforcement Learning When We Can Read the World Model?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34656)**  `arXiv:2611.34656`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Mingchao Zhang, Miduo Cui, Kangze He, Yuanjun Li, Dapeng Li, Ce Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has achieved remarkable success in sequential decision-making, but learning effective policies often requires substantial interaction experience. Model-based reinforcement learning alleviates this dependence by leveraging world models, which are typically used to support planning or policy optimization. In this paper, we investigate an alternative paradigm in which autonomous LLM-based coding agents directly synthesize control policies by reasoning over readable world models. By examining environment source code, these agents translate environmental knowledge into executable decision rules and iteratively refine their policies using online feedback or offline data, without task-specific RL training. Experiments across diverse decision-making benchmarks show that synthesized policies match or outperform conventional RL methods while requiring substantially less experience. Further analyses reveal interpretable decision rules and how they evolve through feedback-driven refinement. Comparisons with policies generated without source code access and experiments under challenging conditions further demonstrate the benefits of readable world models. These findings suggest that direct policy synthesis can reduce reliance on trial and error, challenging the necessity of task-specific RL training.
  </details>

- **[PLAICraft: Large-Scale Time-Aligned Vision-Speech-Action Dataset for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12707)**  `arXiv:2505.12707`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yingchen He, Christian D. Weilbach, Martyna E. Wojciechowska, Yiyuan Sun, Jason Yoo, Yuxuan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in deep generative modeling have made it increasingly plausible to train human-level embodied agents. Yet progress has been limited by the absence of large-scale, real-time, multi-modal, and socially interactive datasets that reflect the sensory-motor complexity of natural environments. To address this, we present PLAICraft, a novel data collection platform and dataset capturing multiplayer Minecraft interactions across five time-aligned modalities: video, game output audio, microphone input audio, mouse, and keyboard actions. Each modality is logged with millisecond time precision, enabling the study of synchronous, embodied behaviour in a rich, open-ended world. The dataset comprises 5525 hours of interactive gameplay from more than 232 participants. Alongside the dataset, we provide an evaluation suite for benchmarking model capabilities in object recognition, spatial awareness, language grounding, and long-term memory. PLAICraft opens a path toward training and evaluating agents that act fluently and purposefully in real time, paving the way for truly embodied artificial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Attributing Agentic Failure in Reasoning Vision-Language Models with Interactive 2D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33162)**  `arXiv:2611.33162`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Pranav Guruprasad, Sean Rivera, Helen Lu, Arushi Jain, Hangliang Ren, Harshvardhan Sikka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic benchmarks report a success rate that confounds distinct causes of failure: misreading the environment, never working out how components of the environment operates, operating the components in the wrong order, and failing to recover from a mistake, each of which calls for a different fix. We present an early preview of MultiNet v2.0-Gridworld - an interactive maze environment built for attribution rather than ranking. Four different types of mechanisms, namely key-door pairs, switch-gate pairs, distractors and decoys, and ordered combinations of these mechanisms, can be added, modified, or removed independently while the renderer, the action space, and the scoring function stay fixed. A breadth-first search over the joint state of position, facing direction, inventory, and mechanism state gives every instance an exact optimal action count, which sets its difficulty and grades unfinished episodes. We fix the evaluation protocol on 15 held-out mazes over 540 episodes, then evaluate three vision-language models on 50 mazes. They solve 6 of 150 episodes, 45 of the 50 mazes were not solved by any model, and no episode containing a switch-gate mechanism was solved.
  </details>

- **[SACA: Selective Attention-Based Clustering Algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17150)**  `arXiv:2508.17150`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Meysam Shirdel Bilehsavar, Razieh Ghaedi, Christian O'Reilly_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clustering algorithms are fundamental tools across many fields, with density-based methods offering particular advantages in identifying arbitrarily shaped clusters and handling noise. However, their effectiveness is often limited by the requirement of critical parameter tuning by users, which typically requires significant domain expertise. This paper introduces a novel density-based clustering algorithm loosely inspired by the concept of selective attention, designed to minimize reliance on parameter tuning for most applications. The proposed method computes an adaptive threshold to exclude sparsely distributed points and outliers, constructs an initial cluster framework, and subsequently reintegrates the filtered points to refine the final results. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmark datasets demonstrate the robustness, accuracy, and ease of use of the proposed approach, establishing it as a powerful alternative to conventional density-based clustering techniques.
  </details>

- **[When Every Rollout Fails: Splicing Learner-Conditioned Options into Long-Horizon Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34777)**  `arXiv:2611.34777`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haowen Hou, Yicheng Liu, Zhen Huang, Jiaqi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards often yields all-fail groups on long-horizon agent tasks: every sampled trajectory receives zero reward, leaving group-relative methods without a reward-based policy gradient. A common remedy supplies complete solutions from a stronger model, but full trajectories are expensive and expose the learner to long off-policy histories. We introduce SPLICE, which constructs local training targets from failed current-policy trajectories online. For each all-fail group, a proposal model selects a splice point in a current-policy trajectory and generates several bounded options from that point. We estimate each option's value using repeated current-policy continuations, convert positive estimates into a return-weighted local target, and fit the current policy on the options. We evaluate SPLICE across two policy families on two long-horizon agent tasks, Software Engineering with SWE-bench Verified and Deep Research with BrowseComp-Plus. SPLICE outperforms GRPO and full-trajectory guidance baselines in all four settings, improving over GRPO by 2.8--9.8 percentage points. On initially failed SWE tasks, it raises GRPO's graduation rate by 10.8 percentage points. Further experiments show that SPLICE improves performance while reducing costly proposal-model computation.
  </details>

- **[Behavioral Compositionality of Sparse Autoencoder Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21261)**  `arXiv:2610.21261`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haotian Ma, Ziye Mei, Frederic Sala_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) provide features that can be combined to explain and steer language model behavior. Understanding these combinations requires knowing how reliably individual feature effects predict their joint impact on the model. We study this relationship by retaining or removing subsets of SAE feature contributions and measuring the resulting behavioral responses. We introduce SAE Additivity Residuals (SAR) to quantify how much behavioral variation is explained by feature interactions beyond a chosen order. We prove that, under independent feature subset sampling, SAR measures the normalized energy of these interactions and bounds errors in ranking feature combinations. Across 18 model and behavior settings, learned SAE coordinates have lower first order residuals than matched rotations of the same activation subspace. This shows that SAE coordinates support comparatively simple descriptions of joint feature effects. Pairwise modeling further improves the ranking of held out feature combinations in all 18 settings. We also demonstrate the practical value of modeling these interactions. Across five challenging benchmarks, pairwise models select feature combinations that give higher probability to the correct answer and preserve the original behavioral response more closely than first order models. In MMLU-Pro steering, pairwise modeling improves rank fidelity from 0.5874 to 0.7790 and reduces ordering error from 0.1444 to 0.0606. These results establish behavioral compositionality as a measurable property of SAE representations and show how modeling feature interactions improves the analysis and selection of feature combinations.
  </details>

- **[GDS Agent for Graph Algorithmic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.20637)**  `arXiv:2508.20637`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Borun Shi, Lusine Adunts, Ioannis Panagiotas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable multimodal information processing and reasoning ability. When equipped with tools through function calling and enhanced with retrieval-augmented techniques, compound LLM-based systems can access closed data sources and answer questions about them. However, they still struggle to process and reason over large-scale graph-structure data. In this technical report we introduce the GDS (Graph Data Science) agent.
