The GDS agent introduces a comprehensive set of graph algorithms as tools, together with preprocessing (retrieval) and postprocessing of algorithm results, in a model context protocol (MCP) server. It is accompanied by a skill that guides the LLM through graph analysis. The agent can be used with any modern LLM out-of-the-box. GDS agent allows users to ask any question that implicitly and intrinsically requires graph algorithmic reasoning about their data, and quickly obtain accurate and grounded answers. We introduce new benchmarks that evaluate intermediate tool calls as well as final responses, and the results indicate that the GDS agent solves a wide spectrum of graph tasks. We also demonstrate how the GDS agent can perform open-ended analyses at the level of domain experts, on tasks such as money laundering tracing and pattern mining in biology. Finally, we discuss the remaining challenges and the future roadmap.
  </details>

- **[Maco-RL: Scaling Agent Capabilities with Mixed-Task Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34703)**  `arXiv:2611.34703`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiacheng Zhang, Qixing Zhou, Haiyang Wang, Qipeng Gu, Shuzhe Wu, Feiyang Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixed-task reinforcement learning (MixRL) directly trains a unified policy across domains to continually expand agent capabilities without training separate specialists or requiring an extra distillation stage. Despite its appeal, scaling MixRL poses significant challenges in achieving stable and efficient mixed-task learning, effectively allocating rollout compute, and supporting continual domain expansion. To address these challenges, we propose Maco-RL, an approach that effectively scales MixRL. Specifically, Maco-RL employs a quota-based scheduler for asynchronous rollouts across multiple task domains, thereby stabilizing training by balancing gradient contributions and mitigating off-policy effects. Along an orthogonal axis, Maco-RL further introduces Tri-Buffer Sampling (TBS) to allocate compute among samples with varying levels of mastery, promoting sustained frontier exploration while preserving acquired capabilities. Experiments on five benchmarks show Maco-RL raises Qwen3.5-27B's overall score from 43.6 to 54.2 and then to 60.1 with continual expansion, reaching the frontier held by Qwen3.8-27B, which is further pushed outward by +5.7. Maco-RL also outperforms single-task RL with less compute for policy updates. These results support MixRL as a practical and compute-efficient post-training approach with substantial potential to strengthen general agent capabilities.
  </details>

- **[SysBio-SR: Benchmarking Symbolic Regression for Trajectory Fidelity and Mechanism Recovery in Systems Biology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34106)**  `arXiv:2611.34106`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Heng Rao, Dan Gao, Benjamin J Hoffman, Yu Gu, Zhenghao Liu, Ge Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression seeks to discover interpretable governing equations for dynamical systems from data. In systems biology, however, accurately reproducing system trajectories does not necessarily imply recovery of the underlying reaction mechanisms. Existing benchmarks evaluate numerical accuracy, expression complexity, or equation-level similarity, but not agreement between recovered reactions and reference mechanisms in biological systems. We introduce SysBio-SR, a benchmark of 198 literature-based biological ODE systems with reaction annotations spanning mass action, Michaelis--Menten, Hill, and complex kinetics. Trajectory fidelity is evaluated across all systems under in-distribution reconstruction and out-of-distribution (OOD) extrapolation, while mechanism recovery is assessed on the subset of 161 systems. We evaluate trajectory fidelity across 11 symbolic-discovery methods and assess mechanism recovery for three reaction-based methods. Joint analysis across systems highlights how severely trajectory-based evaluation overestimates true mechanism recovery. Motivated by these findings, we develop KineSelect, a framework that searches candidate reaction topologies under biologically grounded kinetic laws to select optimal mechanisms that balance trajectory fidelity with structural parsimony. Compared with the strongest baseline, KineSelect improves aggregate OOD extrapolation \(R^2\) by 19.2\% and overall reaction F1 score by 14.3\%.
  </details>

- **[AlignPre: Skill-Aligned Post-Training for Latent-Free Generative Precipitation Nowcasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34101)**  `arXiv:2611.34101`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Demin Yu, Kenghong Lin, Xutao Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precipitation nowcasting requires accurate short-term predictions under rapidly evolving spatiotemporal dynamics. Generative diffusion models can produce detailed and competitive radar forecasts by modeling conditional distributions. However, most existing approaches rely on separately trained components, such as deterministic branches or latent compression modules. More fundamentally, distribution matching alone fails to explicitly optimize operational forecast skill. We propose AlignPre, a streamlined pixel-space nowcasting framework that bridges this gap via skill-aligned post-training (SAPT). AlignPre employs an end-to-end flow-matching forecaster with a hierarchical spatiotemporal architecture for distribution matching and direct radar-field synthesis, without latent compression. During post-training, SAPT exploits the analytic structure of forecast verification scores to construct a differentiable objective. By backpropagating gradients of this objective from radar fields to the generator, SAPT turns forecast verification into direct training feedback. Across three real-world benchmarks spanning different forecast horizons, AlignPre consistently surpasses state-of-the-art deterministic and generative baselines in forecast skill, demonstrating that aligning generative modeling with domain-specific verification provides an effective paradigm for meteorological forecasting.
  </details>

- **[Assessing the Physical Interpretability of AlphaEarth Foundation Embeddings Through Multi-Sensor Satellite Time-Series Benchmarking for Agricultural Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34019)**  `arXiv:2611.34019`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _El Houcine El Moussaoui, Chouaib El Hachimi, Saïd Khabba, Salwa Belaqziz, Bouchra Ait Hssaine, Abderrahman Lahrouni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate and dynamic maps of agricultural LULC are important for agricultural water resource management, particularly in areas where water scarcity is prevalent, such as Morocco. Here, we evaluate the utility of AlphaEarth Foundation Model (AEF) embeddings for agricultural LULC and irrigation-type mapping in Morocco. AEF embeddings were compared to single-date and time-series remote-sensing features using three machine-learning classifiers: XGBoost, CatBoost, and TabPFN. We also evaluated whether AEF embeddings can: (a) provide an efficient and transferable alternative to conventional direct use of Earth observation products and (b) reconstruct biophysical information. Results showed that multi-temporal feature time series achieved the highest performance, with accuracies of 0.93 for agricultural LULC and 0.87 for irrigation-type mapping when using TabPFN. However, AEF embeddings yielded comparable results, achieving accuracies of 0.91 for agricultural LULC and 0.85 for irrigation-type mapping, while reducing the need for extensive data processing and manual feature engineering typically required for satellite products. Spatial LULC mapping using TabPFN achieved comparable overall accuracies (0.85-0.86) across the three feature configurations, but revealed substantial differences in mapped tree cover, ranging from 14.57% for surface reflectance to 29.79% for AFE. Physical probing revealed that AEF embeddings strongly encode spectral indices (A30-SWIR: rho=0.58-0.67), (A38-NDMI: rho=-0.72), vegetation (A49-NDVI: rho = 0.72-0.73), and SAR structure (A25-VH: rho = 0.71). Future research should focus on enhancing the interpretability of embedding-based approaches by incorporating physical knowledge to develop more transparent and trustworthy AI systems for agricultural applications.
  </details>

- **[CARROT: Competitive Adaptive Graph Clustering via Semi-Relaxed Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34012)**  `arXiv:2611.34012`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ching Fan Chiang, Jia-Yu Pan, Wen-Chieh Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep graph clustering (DGC) has emerged as a prominent paradigm for unsupervised attributed graph clustering. However, existing methods often struggle with two practical challenges encountered in real-world applications: the number of clusters $K$ is unknown a priori, and cluster sizes are frequently highly imbalanced. Methods relying on a pre-specified $K$ may fail to recover the underlying clustering structure when $K$ is misspecified, while many approaches implicitly favor balanced partitions, hindering the discovery of minority clusters. We propose CARROT, a Competitive Adaptive gRaph clustering framework via semi-Relaxed Optimal Transport, which introduces semi-relaxed optimal transport into deep graph clustering to jointly address both challenges. CARROT first pre-trains node embeddings to establish a meaningful embedding space, and then jointly optimizes embeddings and cluster assignments through an Adaptive Clustering Mechanism. The mechanism combines semi-relaxed optimal transport to adapt cluster capacities to the underlying data, a competition loss to concentrate mass onto well-supported cluster prototypes, thereby starving degenerate ones, and hard pruning to progressively eliminate them. Extensive experiments on five public benchmarks demonstrate that CARROT achieves state-of-the-art NMI and ARI, accurately recovers the number of clusters without supervision, and faithfully preserves size-imbalanced community structures.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Overgeneralization in RND via Spectral Target Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33850)**  `arXiv:2611.33850`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Minseok Jeong, Yechan Lee, Hyewon Choi, Jeongyong Yang, SooJean Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Random Network Distillation (RND) is a scalable novelty signal for RL based on a fixed random target network and a trained predictor. However, it can overgeneralize: the predictor extrapolates the random target off the data manifold, causing novelty scores to collapse on unfamiliar inputs. This paper analyzes this failure through a kernel-theoretic lens. Under a GP target model and an NTK-regime predictor, we show that the RND novelty score is a cross-kernel residual variance: it is governed jointly by the target covariance kernel and the predictor NTK, rather than by either one alone. This identifies overgeneralization as spectral under-excitation. Motivated by this view, we formulate spectral target design as a principle for shaping RND novelty geometry. As a concrete instance, we replace the implicit random-network target with a bandwidth-controlled Random Fourier Feature (RFF) target, redistributing residual signal toward modes less attenuated by the predictor. Experiments on offline D4RL, online Atari, and Fetch manipulation benchmarks show improved novelty discrimination and downstream RL performance. The code for the RL implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Geometry Is the Memory: Task-Density Routing for Continual LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33487)**  `arXiv:2611.33487`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Alireza Heidari, Thomas Veasey, Mariano Tepper_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Freezing older adapters does not ensure that a continual learner will select and use them properly at inference time. When multiple adapters exist, routing among them and identifying the adapter whose representation space matches the query's answer can be difficult. We propose CONTLORA, which replaces stored examples with compact class statistics for adapter selection and classification. Gaussian class models in a frozen representation select LoRA adapters, while accumulated feature moments exactly recover a class-balanced ridge classifier for fixed training features. Trained without retaining images or feature vectors, CONTLORA reaches 88.55% accuracy on 20-task CIFAR-100 and 86.99% on CUB-200. Across three benchmarks in the matched component study, its mean final accuracy differs from an independently trained stored-feature counterpart by at most 0.07 percentage points. Ablations show that improved task coverage need not improve classification and adapter composition also matters. On held-out classes, competitor-centered likelihood scores improve novelty AUROC over raw likelihood without altering routing ranks. These results support statistical memory as an alternative to example-retention approaches in the evaluated settings, while identifying task ambiguity as an ongoing limitation.
  </details>

- **[One Token, Many Lessons: Sliding-Window Attention within Gated Delta Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33257)**  `arXiv:2611.33257`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuchen Song, Yangbin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear attention compresses historical context into a fixed-size recurrent state, reducing the computational and memory costs of long-sequence modeling. From the perspective of test-time regression (TTR), the state matrix can be viewed as an online-learned regression model, where keys and values serve as inputs and targets respectively, and queries are used to read from the model. From this perspective, the effectiveness of state learning depends not only on the update rule but also on how the key-value learning pairs used for state updates are constructed. While existing work has largely focused on how the state is updated and organized, we investigate how the learning pairs used for state updates are constructed. To this end, we propose Sliding-Window Gated DeltaNet (SWGDN), which replaces short convolutions in Delta-Rule linear attention with sliding-window attention to perform content-dependent local aggregation of projected queries, keys, and values. Our experiments show that SWGDN makes the key-value pairs used in consecutive updates more similar in both GDN2 and KDA. In SWGDN, carrying information from the same token across updates can help the recurrent state reinforce a previously learned association. The local mixing strategy can be incorporated into any Delta-Rule-based linear attention architecture without changing its state update equations or chunkwise parallel algorithms. We apply this strategy to two Delta-Rule variants, GDN2 and KDA, and experimental results demonstrate that SWGDN outperforms the original Delta-Rule baseline across multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[KiRA: Kronecker Input-Gated Rank Adaptation for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of Pretrained Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33236)**  `arXiv:2611.33236`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Akil Ahmad Taki, Shaikh Anowarul Fattah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full fine-tuning of large pretrained models is expensive, and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) is the standard alternative. Most PEFT methods use low-rank updates, whose rank lies far below that of full fine-tuning and is the same for every input. Input-adaptive variants vary the capacity per input but remain within the low-rank bottleneck, and whether a task benefits from input conditioning is not known before training. This paper introduces Kronecker input-gated rank adaptation (KiRA). Instead of the conventional low-rank product, the update $\Delta W$ is the Kronecker product of a frozen random $p\times p$ matrix $A$ and a trainable factor $M(x)$, which overcomes the rank ceiling: because rank is multiplicative under this product, $A$ multiplies the rank of $M(x)$ by $p$ without adding trainable parameters. The factor $M(x)$ is further decomposed as $B_1\,\mathrm{diag}(g(x))\,B_2$, where the input gate $g(x)$, computed once per sample by a network shared across all adapted layers, adjusts the rank of the update to each input in steps of $p$. Where input conditioning is unnecessary, the gate is folded into $B_1$ at deployment. Because the gate changes the rank only in discrete steps, the benefit of input conditioning can be predicted before training from a few hundred labeled inputs. Across six medical imaging and language benchmarks, KiRA attains the highest mean score against five published baselines with 8.7--9.7 times fewer trainable parameters than low-rank adaptation, and the pre-training criterion agrees with the measured benefit of input conditioning on all six tasks.
  </details>

- **[REI: Learning Ideation from Repository Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33183)**  `arXiv:2611.33183`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Muyu Zou, Xiang Feng, Yufei Xu, Shanshan Ye, Zulong Chen, Lingyu Mu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI systems become increasingly capable of executing and evaluating experiments, ideation is emerging as a bottleneck in automated scientific and engineering iteration. In many real-world domains, useful ideas are sparse and their validation is costly, making direct online exploration inefficient. We observe that repository evolution provides a scalable source of reusable technical experience: code changes record concrete technical hypotheses, while subsequent revisions, adoption, and reuse provide signals about their utility. Based on this observation, we propose REI, a Repository Evolution-driven Ideation framework that learns reusable ideation policies from repository evolution, injecting domain-specific technical experience into model parameters and reducing reliance on costly trial-and-error. We further identify a hidden failure mode in quality-driven ideation optimization: semantic diversity collapse. Although generated ideas can remain lexically diverse, they increasingly concentrate in a small number of dominant method families, limiting cross-task generalization and long-tail exploration. To address this problem, we introduce Semantic Prior Optimization and Debiasing (SPOD), a diversity-preserving alignment mechanism that makes method identity explicit, corrects category-level reward bias, and limits redundant accumulation within the same method family while retaining strong representatives. Experiments on public benchmarks and a real-world industrial setting demonstrate improvements in ideation quality, method diversity, cross-task generalization, and long-tail exploration.
  </details>

- **[LEGOSpec: A Composable and Efficient Framework for Speculative Draft Design and Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33004)**  `arXiv:2611.33004`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hanzhen Wang, Jiaming Xu, Guohao Dai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) generation using a lightweight draft. Existing methods evaluate complete recipes under different training configurations, leaving component effects, interactions, and composition benefits unclear. Therefore, we present LEGOSpec, a composable framework for speculative draft design and training. LEGOSpec decomposes draft models into Context, Backbone, Proposal, and Objective components that share a unified training infrastructure. Using LEGOSpec, we systematically vary components and evaluate selected combinations under matched training conditions to isolate their effects and interactions. These experiments reveal three findings: (1) Different workloads favor different backbones, depending on context length and memory budget, with within-block dependency modeling particularly important for long contexts. (2) Proposal design must balance acceptance and latency. (3) The training objective should match token distributions locally and optimize acceptance length across the block. These findings guide workload-aware configurations and DFlux, a balanced general-purpose draft combining a convolution-equipped SWA--GQA hybrid backbone, Residual Corrector, and hierarchical supervision. Across Qwen and Gemma models, DFlux improves acceptance length over the strongest baseline by up to 13.7% on regular benchmarks and 34.7% on long-context workloads. To support efficient training, LEGOSpec integrates metadata-aware scheduling and asynchronous execution, increasing training throughput by up to 79% and accelerating offline supervision preparation by up to $3.11\times$ over SpecForge.
  </details>

- **[Actionwave: A Systematic Benchmark for Waveform-Centric Clinical Prediction Across Care Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31249)**  `arXiv:2611.31249`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Peng, Davis Hobley, Song Wang, Zachary Plotkin, Tianlong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous bedside waveforms record rapid physiological changes that can inform clinical decisions. Existing benchmarks examine selected prediction conditions, but it remains unclear how model advantages depend on signal duration, warning time, and available inputs across care settings. We introduce Actionwave, a benchmark for waveform-centric clinical prediction across intensive-care, operating-room, and emergency-department settings. Actionwave uses a common decision-time prediction interface and groups clinically grounded tasks by what is inferred and when. It varies conditions relevant to clinical use, including the warning required before an intervention. We compare diverse architectures and pretrained biosignal encoders under specified model-size and compute budgets. The protocol requires patient-level splits, strict information cutoffs, and comparisons that isolate individual modeling factors. Across 20 tasks and 1.4M task-labelled windows from source datasets, the experimental grids account for over 87,000 model fits. These comparisons identify task-specific choices and show why matched cohorts, unimodal controls, and explicit timing are necessary when interpreting waveform model performance.
  </details>

- **[Recurrent In-Context Learning for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14998)**  `arXiv:2610.14998`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tejas Vaidhya, Gaurav Sahu, Ishita Sanjay Hiremath, Irina Rish_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models predict unlabelled rows from labelled rows in a single forward pass via in-context learning (ICL). Like language models, these architectures have scaled by stacking transformer blocks, each with its own parameters. However, this approach couples compute with model size: increasing computation requires increasing the parameter count. In this work, we investigate whether tabular ICL requires distinct parameters at every block, or simply sufficient depth. We introduce Tab-Loop, a looped tabular foundation model built on the TabICLv2 architecture. Instead of stacking unique blocks, Tab-Loop takes a small set of blocks with shared parameters and applies them repeatedly in a loop during its ICL stage. We evaluate this design against standard architectures under equal training and inference compute budgets. Our experiments show that depth, rather than unique parameters, drives performance. Across classification and regression tasks on held-out synthetic tables, the TabArena and TALENT benchmarks, Tab-Loop matches the accuracy of a standard model with three times as many ICL blocks while using roughly 60% fewer parameters. Conversely, a non-looped baseline of the same size as Tab-Loop underperforms it, even when trained on 32% more tables. This pattern holds consistently whether we vary the number of distinct blocks from one to twelve or the effective depth. Furthermore, running additional loops at inference improves predictive accuracy without retraining. Training the model with deeper loops extends the range over which these extra inference steps remain beneficial, eventually matching the released TabICLv2 on TabArena at 2.7 times the ICL compute. These results show that tabular foundation models can scale their compute independently of parameter count, pointing to test-time compute scaling as a practical path toward efficient tabular models.
  </details>

- **[Amortized Counterfactual Prediction with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13724)**  `arXiv:2610.13724`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hendrik Stumpf, Francesco Quinzan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual inference asks how an observed outcome might have differed under different circumstances, such as whether a patient would have recovered had they received a different treatment. Such questions arise whenever decisions depend on the outcomes of alternatives that were not observed. Answering counterfactual queries generally requires knowledge of the underlying causal structure, which is often unavailable in practice. A common approach is therefore to first estimate the causal graph from observational data and then perform counterfactual inference. However, errors in the estimated causal graph can propagate to downstream counterfactual predictions. To address this challenge, we introduce the Counterfactual Graph Neural Process (CFGNP), an end-to-end approach that answers counterfactual queries directly from observational data without first estimating a causal graph. CFGNP uses Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to exploit the dual-graph structure by restricting cross-world message passing to corresponding factual--counterfactual variables. CFGNP both offers an approach that does not require knowledge of the causal graph and achieves stronger predictive performance than existing approaches. When trained on counterfactual pairs from the same structural causal model used for testing, CFGNP achieves $1.6-7 \times$ lower MSE than baselines that receive the true causal graph and the same counterfactual supervision, across seven benchmark variants. Furthermore, on synthetic causal graphs, a model trained only on 10-node graphs maintains strong predictive performance on graphs with up to 30 nodes, without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Reification as a Transferable Vocabulary: Zero-Shot Link Prediction with Vanilla GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11347)**  `arXiv:2609.11347`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Camille Pradel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graph foundation models such as ULTRA achieve zero-shot link prediction on unseen graphs through dedicated architectures that hard-code a transfer mechanism. In this work we move that mechanism out of the architecture and into the representation, by \emph{reifying} the input graph: every fact becomes a node, connected to its subject, object, and relation type through a fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations, with relation types as anonymous shared nodes rather than model parameters. On this representation, five textbook GNNs (GAT, GINE with sum and with mean+max aggregation, GraphSAGE, R-GCN), each trained on a single knowledge graph of 4,245 triples for 30 minutes on one NVIDIA A100, transfer zero-shot to 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks. The best of them, an off-the-shelf GAT, matches ULTRA, a dedicated foundation model pretrained on three graphs, across ULTRA's own evaluation suite. The same fixed vocabulary extends to relational databases, a row becoming an entity and a foreign-key column a relation type; a preliminary probe on two unseen databases, with no cell values, schema text or in-context labels, shows a model of this family pretrained on three knowledge graphs ranking foreign-key targets far above random-initialization and degree controls. We release the code, the checkpoints, and the evaluation pipeline for all 40 benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Sample Where You Struggle: Sharpening Base Model Reasoning via Entropy-Guided Power Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09926)**  `arXiv:2606.09926`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hong Guo, Nianhui Guo, Christoph Meinel, Haojin Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sampling from the sequence-level power distribution elicits RL-level reasoning from base language models without any parameter updates, but the standard Metropolis--Hastings (MH), a Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampler, is both expensive and slow-mixing. We trace both to a structural mismatch: the power distribution mainly departs from the base distribution at a sparse, spatially clustered set of high-entropy decision points, yet MH proposes resampling positions uniformly along the prefix---wasting compute on near-degenerate conditionals while under-mixing precisely where modes diverge. We propose Entropy-Guided Power Sampling (EGPS), a training-free and verifier-free approximate sampler that re-derives its proposal from token-level entropy already in the forward pass. EGPS skips deterministic blocks, localizes each MCMC move to a high-entropy neighborhood, and applies Multiple-Try Metropolis at decision points---making sampling cost scale with entropy mass rather than sequence length. EGPS targets significant speedup with accuracy that matches or improves on MH power sampling: across three base models and three benchmarks it attains best or tied-best accuracy on 5 of 9 settings; on Qwen2.5-Math-7B it is best or tied-best on all three (MATH500 $75.8\%$, HumanEval $62.2\%$, GPQA $42.4\%$), with wall-clock speedups over the MH baseline of up to $12.6\times$ across these benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Scalable Heterogeneous Graph Foundation Models for Data-Driven Optimal Power Flow in Smart Grids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23194)**  `arXiv:2605.23194`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Massimiliano Lupo Pasini, Yijiang Li, Kibaek Kim, Teja Kuruganti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast and reliable optimal power flow (OPF) approximation is important for power system operation, yet heterogeneous OPF graph models are often evaluated either with architecture-specific implementations or on limited training corpora. This paper presents a large-scale heterogeneous graph-learning workflow, built on HydraGNN, for data-driven OPF surrogate modeling and graph foundation-model (GFM) development. The workflow preserves buses, generators, loads, shunts, alternating-current (AC) lines, transformers, and device-to-bus relations and provides a common implementation for distributed preprocessing, multi-graphics-processing-unit (GPU) training, hyperparameter optimization (HPO), and downstream adaptation. Using approximately three million heterogeneous graphs from ten Power Grid Library for Benchmarking AC Optimal Power Flow Algorithms (PGLib-OPF) cases spanning 14 to 13,659 buses, we compare six heterogeneous graph neural network (GNN) families under a common HPO campaign on Frontier, resulting in two compact HeteroSAGE and HeteroHEAT models (~1.6-1.7 million parameters) that achieve the lowest observed validation MSE. Strong and weak scaling using up to 1,024 Frontier nodes show that 256 nodes (corresponding to 1,024 AMD MI250X GPUs) provides the best time-to-solution balance for the measured workload. Two downstream tasks evaluate task adaptation on the IEEE 118-bus case: a classification task to distinguish nominal operating conditions from artificially generated extreme-overload conditions and N-1 single-component-outage regression to predict bus voltage magnitude and phase angle. Partial fine-tuning provides the strongest balance between predictive performance and computational requirements.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking LLM-Based Investment Advisor Agents for Asset Allocation in the Chinese ETF Market: Overview of NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22305)**  `arXiv:2603.22305`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Liyuan Chen, Shilong Li, Jiangpeng Yan, Shuoling Liu, Xi Cheng, Haoran Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) convert unstructured financial news into actionable trading strategies, yet standardized end-to-end benchmarks tailored for Chinese market remain scarce. We select common Chinese Exchange-Traded Funds (ETFs) as the investment universe and introduce CN-Buzz2Portfolio, the official benchmark of NLPCC 2026 Shared Task 4. Investment agents take daily trending Chinese financial news and historical ETF price-volume series as inputs to complete news filtering, news-to-ETF mapping and risk-controlled portfolio construction. To facilitate fair comparison, we provide a reproducible Compression--Perception--Allocation baseline compatible with mainstream LLMs. The task contains two ETF allocation tracks: Macro-Asset Allocation and Sector-Rotation Allocation. We adopt chronological data split across 2024--2026 to alleviate overfitting, with final rankings determined by out-of-sample Sharpe ratios on unseen test data. 26 teams registered for the task, and 14 submitted self-designed agent systems. Three core findings emerge: (1) Native LLM reasoning capability cannot translate to superior investment returns, as models show highly differentiated risk-return characteristics; (2) Hybrid frameworks are widely adopted, where LLMs extract textual semantic signals and quantitative modules incorporate price-volume factors and risk constraints; (3) Systems based on identical base LLMs yield distinct results with different signal processing, memory and portfolio construction modules. The project is publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ML-Tool-Bench: Tool-Augmented Planning for ML Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.00672)**  `arXiv:2512.00672`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yaswanth Chittepu, Raghavendra Addanki, Tung Mai, Anup Rao, Branislav Kveton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The development of autonomous machine learning (ML) agents capable of end-to-end data science workflows represents a significant frontier in artificial intelligence. These agents must orchestrate complex sequences of data analysis, feature engineering, model selection, and hyperparameter optimization, tasks that require sophisticated planning and iteration. While recent work on building ML agents has explored using large language models (LLMs) for direct code generation, tool-augmented approaches offer greater modularity and reliability. However, existing tool-use benchmarks focus primarily on task-specific tool selection or argument extraction for tool invocation, failing to evaluate the sophisticated planning capabilities required for ML Agents. In this work, we introduce a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating tool-augmented ML agents using a curated set of 61 specialized tools and 15 tabular ML challenges from Kaggle. Our benchmark goes beyond traditional tool-use evaluation by incorporating an in-memory named object management, allowing agents to flexibly name, save, and retrieve intermediate results throughout the workflows. We demonstrate that standard ReAct-style approaches struggle to generate valid tool sequences for complex ML pipelines, and that tree search methods with LLM-based evaluation underperform due to inconsistent state scoring. To address these limitations, we propose two simple approaches: 1) using shaped deterministic rewards with structured textual feedback, and 2) decomposing the original problem into a sequence of sub-tasks, which significantly improves trajectory validity and task performance. Using GPT-4o, our approach improves over ReAct by 16.52 percentile positions, taking the median across all Kaggle challenges. We believe our work provides a foundation for developing more capable tool-augmented planning ML agents.
  </details>

- **[A gene informed benchmark for BioScoring of pathology foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34930)**  `arXiv:2611.34930`  `cs.LG`  
  _Paul Vilars, Vincent Quoc-Huy Trinh, Maria Vakalopoulou, Dimitris Samaras, Stergios Christodoulidis, Mahdi S. Hosseini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pathology foundation models are usually compared by their performance on downstream tasks such as classification and retrieval. While such benchmarks measure how useful a model is, they provide no insight into the biology it represents. We introduce BioScore, a benchmark that quantifies how well an image encoder captures cell identities, with spatial transcriptomics of the same tissue as the reference. A sparse autoencoder splits the encoder's patch embeddings into sparse features, which we cluster into a fixed number of groups. BioScore then measures, for each of 52 cell identities defined by marker genes, how specifically one group follows the identity's expression over the tissue. Because the autoencoder training is fully unsupervised, a match shows that the model represents the cell identity from the image alone. We score 8 pathology foundation models and 3 natural-image models. Every pathology model scores above every natural-image model (0.299--0.371 \vs 0.214--0.232), and paired tests separate three tiers among the pathology models. Unlike a supervised probe, BioScore needs no labels, builds its reference from the data and scores even from few sections, yet it ranks the encoders as a probe does. Its per-identity breakdown indicates which cell identities each encoder captures or misses.
  </details>

- **[Put the World Back: Timestamps Matter in Time Series Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34848)**  `arXiv:2611.34848`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tingrui Huang, Yuanyuan Deng, Qingren Yao, Joaquin Vanschoren, Mykola Pechenizkiy, Songgaojun Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to time series reasoning. However, current evaluations often give models events in chronological order and already matched to the time series. This makes it unclear whether models use timestamps to connect events with observations or simply take shortcuts. To expose this ambiguity, we reformulate time series reasoning from a world-to-series projection perspective, viewing timestamps as explicit indices that connect numerical observations and external events to shared world states. We identify three structures that timestamps can make explicit: temporal causal structure, time semantics, and event-series alignment. Building on this perspective, we develop a diagnostic protocol that distinguishes timestamp-grounded reasoning from correlational shortcuts, a distinction that accuracy-based benchmarks cannot make. Controlled interventions on 310 questions from two tasks show that high accuracy can persist when timestamps are replaced by relative offsets or removed, while breaking event-series alignment induces systematic failures. When context is pre-aligned, models barely need timestamps. When order is missing, they use dates to recall world knowledge. Without dates, they rely on correlational shortcuts and rarely notice incorrect timestamps. These findings show that high accuracy on pre-aligned, ordered inputs does not establish that a model uses timestamps to synchronize observations with the world. We release our code, model outputs, and trajectory labels atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Routeability Before Routing: Routeability Audit Protocol (RAP) and RouteabilityBench for Audited LLM Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34825)**  `arXiv:2611.34825`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yihang Lu, Denica Kjorvezir, Ana Gjorgjevikj, Carola Doerr, Tome Eftimov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM routing aims to choose, for each prompt, which model in a portfolio is most likely to answer correctly. This only makes sense when the benchmark contains enough prompt-specific variation for different models to be best on different inputs. On coarse-scored LLM benchmarks, however, many models often tie for the best score, and the single best model may already leave little room for improvement. We therefore propose a routeability-first audit, instantiated as RAP and RouteabilityBench, a runnable claim-verification package. The audit is retrospective: it runs once the benchmark-portfolio score matrix exists and before router comparisons are interpreted as general evidence, rather than certifying a portfolio that has not yet been evaluated. Across four benchmarks and 66 LLMs, generic routing enhancements such as larger embedding sets, dimensionality reduction, selective abstention, clustering, and stronger regressors do not consistently outperform the single best model. The clearest positive evidence is concentrated in portfolio-dependent Omni-MATH settings, while GPQA, MMLU-Pro, and IFEval show different ways in which routing can remain hard or predictor-sensitive. Our practical recommendation is that routing gains should be reported together with routeability diagnostics, tied-best analysis, portfolio construction details, and family-corrected statistical tests.
  </details>

- **[Robust Recourse under Causal Graph Ambiguity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34723)**  `arXiv:2611.34723`  `cs.LG`  
  _Muyan Anna Li, Juliette Sinnott, Mohammad Kohandel, Amir-Hossein Karimi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal algorithmic recourse recommends actions to reverse unfavorable decisions by modeling how interventions change an individual's features. Existing methods typically assume one causal graph that describes the system and account for uncertainty in the causal mechanisms. However, there usually exist several graphs that fit the same data but can predict different effects for the same action. Certain actions may achieve recourse across multiple graphs, but as the set of potential graphs grows, a single best action may become infeasible.We propose Worst-Case Recourse and Graph-Weighted Recourse to find a low-cost action that succeeds across all candidate graphs or a specified fraction of graph weight, respectively. Assuming a set of plausible graphs, for each graph we fit Gaussian process structural equations and assess success using the posterior mean--standard-deviation criterion. We use gradient-based search to determine minimal cost actions and check the validity of the actions on fresh posterior samples. In synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks, relaxing robustness, the requirement that an action succeed under every candidate graph, can increase the number of valid recommendations for recourse and lower costs of actions for both methods. In the 20-node GP benchmark, relaxation increases the number of factual instances offered recourse (coverage) from 341 to 436 out of 1,000, while the number of true successes, actions that flip the decision under the generating model, falls from 251 to 184, illustrating the coverage--validity trade-off. Accounting for graph ambiguity can help decision-makers recommend actions that remain effective even when the causal mechanisms of the system are not fully known.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Looped MoE Language Models under Matched Budgets: Information Flow and Parameter Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34276)**  `arXiv:2611.34276`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shipeng Bai, Jiayu Qin, Hong Liu, Xili Wang, Jianchao Tan, Jiacheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers decouple computational depth from parameter count, and prior studies have demonstrated promising performance gains with increased computation. However, systematic design principles for configuring these models under strict iso-parameter and iso-FLOP constraints remain scarce. We contribute to this direction by improving information flow in looped models and systematically studying loop parameter configurations. Specifically, we refine this information flow via gated inner-loop state updates and a cross-stage residual connection. Building on this design, controlled studies identify a configuration combining a single-pass Core depth of 25%-35% of the total unrolled depth with reallocating saved parameters into extra routed experts per layer, improving performance at zero extra compute while preserving compatibility with existing training infrastructure. Evaluated after 1T pre-training tokens, our 69B MLA and 51B KDA Mixture-of-Experts models outperform budget-matched non-recurrent baselines by an average of 1.16 and 2.29 percentage points across four benchmarks, respectively. Further reproduction experiments on a common backbone with a 200B-token training budget show that our method achieves lower perplexity than other looped methods. Code for these reproduction experiments is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Agent-as-a-Verifier: From Scores to Evidence for General Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34132)**  `arXiv:2611.34132`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Sun, Chen, Nagarajan Natarajan, Sushrut Karmalkar, Niranjani Prasad, Amit Sharma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verification is increasingly critical across various inference and learning paradigms, such as test-time scaling, recursive intelligence and automated research, where feedback can improve efficiency and optimization. Common score-based verifiers, or discriminative reward models, predict a scalar reward for an output. While useful for filtering, rewarding, and ranking, such scores provide no explicit, auditable evidentiary basis for the judgment. In parallel, although existing generative process reward models offer text feedback, most designs do not fully exploit agentic capabilities, such as tool use and iterative reasoning, which are essential for fact-checking and fine-grained claim verification. In this paper, we address these limitations by introducing Agent-as-a-Verifier, an agentic, certificate-producing verification system that places role-separated LLM agents inside a formal-methods-inspired claim-obligation-certificate protocol. Given a generation candidate, Agent-as-a-Verifier produces a certificate by extracting a verdict-blind dependency graph of claims, independently compiling the task's acceptance contract, and deriving checkable obligations. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, Agent-as-a-Verifier achieves higher accuracy than the state-of-the-art approach. Across different downstream applications, the augmented agent also outperforms the plain agent on FrontierMath and CyberGym. More importantly, Agent-as-a-Verifier is able to identify reward-hacking solutions that deceive the benchmark evaluator, highlighting its broader potential for iterative and recursive agent optimization. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[The Reference Matters: Displacement Probing for Weight-Space Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33998)**  `arXiv:2611.33998`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haechan Shin, Hoonki Lee, Jungwoon Kang, Wooyeol Baek, Sangyeob Kim, Jongyoo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space learning predicts a model's attributes directly from its parameters, and probing-based methods do so efficiently by reading a model's responses to learned probes. Prior work shows that probing succeeds within a Model Tree -- models fine-tuned from a shared pretrained origin -- but not why. We trace this to the shared origin itself: since a tree's leaves start from the same weights, what distinguishes them is their displacement from that origin. This motivates probing the displacement rather than the raw weight, an intervention we call the DiSplacement Probe (DSProbe). We develop this idea along two axes: extending it to second-order Gram responses, whose displacement, to first order, tracks the feature changes induced by fine-tuning; and treating the reference as a design choice, learning a shared component to subtract beyond the pretrained origin. Across four architectures on the Model Jungle benchmark, the resulting Refined-DSProbe (R-DSProbe) sets a new state of the art, matches prior accuracy with substantially fewer probes, and supports a single encoder shared across Model Trees, reducing metanetwork parameters by 3.61 times. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Flipping the Coins: Training Physical Simulators through Hard Switches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33716)**  `arXiv:2611.33716`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianrun Gao, Ruiqi Feng, Haomin Wen, Tailin Wu, Liangqiong Qu, Xiaomeng Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid simulators embed learnable components such as neural networks in physical solvers and train them on reference trajectories. Many scientific solvers feature hard switches, such as a tripping power line, an igniting grid cell, or depolarizing brain tissue, where the loss exhibits discrete jumps across switch flips, leaving standard automatic differentiation to capture only its smooth part. Existing training paradigms face a fundamentally challenging trade-off, as gradient-based relaxations smooth the switches and introduce bias across jumps, whereas derivative-free methods treat the solver as a black box and discard path gradients of the continuous dynamics. Here we introduce EventFlip, which flips a near-threshold switch from a stored state, replays the original solver, and measures the loss difference between the two runs, in effect a finite difference across the switch. By perturbing every threshold with small Gaussian noise, we cast each switch as a stochastic binary decision and derive an unbiased gradient estimator that combines the continuous path gradient with a jump term constructed from these counterfactual rollouts. EventFlip-Rand optimizes this noise-averaged objective, whereas EventFlip-Hard operates on the deterministic hard-switch simulator used at deployment and provably targets the complete discontinuous loss. Under an identical forward evaluation budget, we benchmark EventFlip against twelve baselines across a power-grid cascade, a detonation wave, and cortical spreading depolarization, achieving the lowest error on every task and reducing the strongest baseline's error by factors of 1.5, 1.4, and 1.4, respectively.
  </details>

- **[PRIOR: Pre-Call Reasoning in SFT and Its Influence on Tool-Use Retention under RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33648)**  `arXiv:2611.33648`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiwen Hu, Hyunin Lee, Joseph Suh, Serina Chang, Shuai Shao, Yong Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool use learned during supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is not always retained during reinforcement learning (RL). We study how properties of SFT data may affect this retention by constructing Low, Medium, and High SFT datasets with approximately matched token budgets but different tool-call depths. Under the same RL recipe, the three initializations reach similar RL training accuracy yet exhibit markedly different tool-use dynamics: tool initiation increases in Low, declines in Medium, and collapses in High. We observe that these datasets also differ substantially in the amount of reasoning preceding each tool call. In particular, High trajectories contain considerably less pre-call reasoning, motivating the hypothesis that pre-call reasoning may affect how well tool use is calibrated and, consequently, whether the corresponding behaviors are retained during RL. An idealized theoretical analysis provides a selective-retention view of this process: advantages in overall and tool-conditioned accuracy can be preserved under outcome-only optimization, while tool initiation increases only when tool-using trajectories are more successful than the tool-free alternative. Guided by this analysis, we rank the original High trajectories by average pre-call reasoning length and retain the highest-ranked examples while approximately matching cumulative SFT token exposure. This simple filtering strategy improves post-RL accuracy and tool-conditioned final-answer success without consistently increasing tool initiation across multiple benchmarks. These results suggest that pre-call reasoning is a useful dimension of SFT supervision for understanding and improving tool-use retention under downstream RL.
  </details>

- **[Pretraining for Forecasting in Relational Databases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33645)**  `arXiv:2611.33645`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziyue Lin, Sebastian Regalado Lozano, Michael Guerzhoy, Ricardo Baptista, George Stein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world forecasting increasingly relies on asynchronous transaction logs in relational databases rather than uniformly sampled arrays. While relational deep learning (RDL) models can forecast from these complex histories, they are typically trained separately for each specific target and root entity type. We introduce Query-Conditioned Relational pretraining (QCRP), a self-supervised framework that enables a standard RDL backbone to act as a universal forecaster across a database. QCRP generates diverse forecasting objectives using a structured temporal query grammar and interleaves them through a shared conditional predictor. This produces a robust, frozen encoder reusable for downstream tasks via lightweight predictors or a zero-shot interface. Evaluated on large-scale RelBench databases, QCRP learns frozen representations that support competitive forecasting across tasks and entity types. Furthermore, a single unified QCRP model checkpoint is competitive with isolated, entity-specific specialists. We demonstrate promising results for reusing the learned representations from standard RDL backbones for forecasting tasks in relational databases.
  </details>

- **[Deletion You Can Check: Auditing LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33525)**  `arXiv:2611.33525`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mayank Gulati, Gerhard Wunder_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A common indication that a fine-tuned model has forgotten a deleted record is a membership-inference score near chance (ROC-AUC $\approx 0.5$). This reading is sound only if the comparison population represents how the record should behave after a valid deletion---a counterfactual that is rarely validated. We test this premise in a controlled $2\times2$ study pairing two membership-inference benchmarks, ArxivMIA and BookMIA, with two language models, Llama-3.2-1B and Qwen2.5-1.5B. Exact retraining validates the membership reference: it lies within the prespecified chance band on ArxivMIA but reaches $0.122$ on BookMIA, causing the same metric to reject an empirical deletion reference. A post-hoc BookMIA/Qwen diagnostic replaces benchmark label-0 records with same-pool, never-fine-tuned records and moves the reference from $0.257$ to $0.523$, supporting population mismatch in that cell. Each approximate-unlearning checkpoint is judged jointly on membership removal, deletion-targeted shift beyond non-deletion variation, and utility. On the admitted ArxivMIA/Llama axis, no tested configuration of gradient ascent with retain descent (GA+GD), negative-preference optimization with retain training (NPO-RT), or teacher--student unlearning (SCRUB) passes. A score near chance is not evidence of forgetting until a reference establishes what chance means.
  </details>

- **[Yet Another Concept Drift Benchmark? A Principled Framework for Evaluating Drift Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33424)**  `arXiv:2611.33424`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lorenzo Iovine, Giacomo Ziffer, Emanuele Della Valle_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept drift detection is a fundamental component of data stream learning, yet its empirical evaluation remains based on loosely defined protocols, ambiguous alarm semantics, and inconsistent metrics. We propose a principled benchmark grounded in an explicit formulation of drift observability and alarm semantics. Our framework introduces finite detection windows and repeated alarms, enabling a clear separation between detection accuracy, timeliness, and stability, and employs a reproducible observer to provide a controlled and interpretable performance signal. Building on this formulation, we define normalized evaluation metrics and a composite score addressing limitations of existing measures. We validate the framework through a large-scale stress-testing evaluation of 10 detectors across 9 learner configurations and 16 datasets.
  </details>

- **[FlowersBench: A FluidFlower-Inspired Dataset for Benchmarking Carbon Storage Surrogates Across Geological Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33335)**  `arXiv:2611.33335`  `cs.LG`  
  _Farah Rabie, Hannah Menke, Florian Doster, Amy Gough, Ahmed H. Elsheikh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning surrogates are emerging as a computationally efficient tool for uncertainty quantification in geological carbon storage (GCS). Geological structure constitutes a major source of uncertainty; however, existing benchmark simulation datasets predominantly vary petrophysical and operational parameters within a fixed geological setting. Generalisation to unseen geological structures therefore remains largely unexplored. We introduce FlowersBench, a benchmark dataset of 5,000 two-dimensional, reservoir-scale CO$_2$ storage simulations across 244 structural geometries. The geometries are sampled from an in-house structural generator inspired by the FluidFlower experiments and Case B of the 11th Society of Petroleum Engineers Comparative Solution Project (SPE11B). Each simulation is run with Open Porous Media (OPM) Flow over a 50-year injection period and a 500-year post-injection monitoring period. Test geometries are withheld from training to assess generalisation to unseen structural geometries. Two additional subsets support evaluation under structural shift. The first extends the parameter ranges of the structural generator beyond those used for training, and the second introduces an unseen topology of multiple parallel faults. A baseline U-Net surrogate is trained to predict CO$_2$ gas saturation. In representative test cases, the surrogate recovers the main migration pathways, including lateral spreading beneath low-permeability layers and upward flow along fault damage zones, with errors concentrated near the plume boundary. Under both structural shifts, prediction error exceeds that on the held-out geometries, and the increase in relative error is greater for the unseen fault topology. FlowersBench is publicly available and provides a common basis for evaluating GCS surrogates across geological structures.
  </details>

- **[Typical Angina, Reversed: A Long-Standing Documentation Error in the UCI Heart Disease Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33248)**  `arXiv:2611.33248`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tejas Tovinkere Pattabhi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The UCI Heart Disease data are a widely used machine-learning benchmark. We show that their chest-pain variable is stored in reverse of its documentation. At all four sites, the code documented as "asymptomatic" (4) marks patients with typical angina, and the code documented as "typical angina" (1) marks patients with almost none of the classical angina features (at Cleveland, no chest pain).Two complementary checks establish this: (i) the chest-pain table and regression coefficients of the original 1986 Cleveland study are reproduced only under the corrected reading; (ii) in the Budapest, Switzerland and Long Beach files, 93-96% of patients coded 4 have all three classical angina features. A 1990 word-labeled Cleveland file already uses the documented labels, and Statlog (Heart), a Cleveland subset, stores the same codes. OpenML, PMLB and two highly voted Kaggle copies that name the categories use the same documented labels (about 400,000 downloads combined).We audited a seeded random sample of 150 records from a pre-specified Scholar query for SHAP studies of these data mentioning asymptomatic chest pain (124 readable). Of 32 papers that state a direction for a named category, 25 (78%; 95% CI 61-89%) state one inconsistent with the corrected category identities; 22 (69%; 51-82%; post hoc) are attributable to the documentation error, and nine of these explain it clinically. None of the 124 readable papers identifies the reversal.Conventional models are essentially unaffected, one reason the error went unnoticed. Interpretations that attach the documented names to model effects are reversed, and zero-shot language-model classifiers that read the labels are misled: with the documented labels, three open-weight models lose 0.12-0.33 in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) given age, sex and chest-pain type. We give the corrected mapping, evidence and code.
  </details>

- **[Escaping Latent Traps by Remembering: Training-Free Episodic Kernel Repulsion for World-Model Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22470)**  `arXiv:2611.22470`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yican Zhao, Bolin Yang, Ruizhe Wang, Haiyang Guo, Fuji Yang, Bingo Wing-Kuen Ling, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models plan towards a goal image by minimising the distance between predicted and goal latents. Because this distance is not geodesic, it creates latent traps---local minima or flat basins behind walls, at corners and in dead ends---where memoryless receding-horizon replanning keeps revisiting states far from the goal. We propose Episodic Kernel Repulsion (EKR), a training-free, test-time cost term that penalises candidate plans via a top-$k$ Gaussian kernel to a novelty-gated memory of the latents the agent has actually visited, normalised by the current batch base cost so a single strength transfers across models. Across two JEPA world models and four benchmarks, EKR improves success in all eight settings without changing the model, planner or search budget, matching or exceeding $4\times$ samples at $0.8$--$1.5\times$ compute in seven of eight settings while adding $\sim 25$ ms ($<1\%$) per replan. Controlled trap-fork interventions confirm that the memory causes escape, and an untuned configuration transfers directly to DINO-WM with CEM (e.g. $44.7\%\to 100.0\%$ on long PointMaze, $+19.3$ points on long Wall) and to JEPA-WM on Metaworld mw-reach-wall ($25.3\%\to 40.7\%$).
  </details>

- **[Best Policy Tracking in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34501)**  `arXiv:2610.34501`  `cs.LG`  
  _Judith Echevarrieta, Etor Arza, Aritz Pérez, Josu Ceberio_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning commonly exhibits non-monotonic performance dynamics in single-task settings. During learning, a sequence of policies is generated whose true performance is unknown and may oscillate significantly across iterations. Despite this well-known phenomenon, standard experimental practice typically reports either the final visited policy or the best among periodically evaluated ones, implicitly assuming that these selection criteria reliably approximate the best-performing policy visited throughout the entire sequence.In this work, we present an empirical study on the implications of non-monotonicity for policy selection in single-task policy-based reinforcement learning. We introduce post-hoc diagnostic metrics to characterize performance degradation and assess the accuracy and efficiency of different selection criteria. These metrics allow us to evaluate commonly used selection strategies across standard continuous-control benchmarks using a well-established on-policy actor-critic method. Our results show that, in this representative experimental setting, widely adopted selection practices can deviate considerably from the best policy encountered during learning, leading to underperforming estimates and suboptimal use of computational resources. As an alternative, we recommend a validation-based policy selection strategy constrained by computational cost, which provides a more consistent and accurate approximation of the best visited policy while improving selection efficiency. Overall, our findings highlight policy selection as an underexplored factor in the evaluation protocols of reinforcement learning and motivate further study on explicit and standardized validation-based selection procedures.
  </details>

- **[SLIM: Simplex-Lattice Interpolation Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01037)**  `arXiv:2610.01037`  `cs.LG`  
  _Seongcheol Jeong, Masahiro Suzuki, Yutaka Matsuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing merging coefficients for large language models can require many costly benchmark evaluations. We propose Simplex-Lattice Interpolation Merging (SLIM), which constructs a quadratic surrogate of aggregate performance on the coefficient simplex using a classical mixture design. Evaluations of individual experts and equal-weight pairs determine the surrogate with the minimum number of measurements needed to identify a general quadratic on this domain. SLIM then optimizes the surrogate without further target-metric evaluations. Experiments on two model architectures demonstrate accurate prediction of unseen multi-expert mixtures and competitive merge performance under limited evaluation budgets. Matched-budget comparisons show that structured evaluation points improve prediction fidelity over random designs, including those using regularized fitting.
  </details>

- **[Tabby: An Open Pretraining Recipe for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13956)**  `arXiv:2609.13956`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shifeng Xie, Bahaeddine Abdessalem, Zehao Xiao, Youssef Attia El Hili, Ambroise Odonnat, Zhiwei Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this report, we release Tabby, a long context probabilistic time series foundation model, together with a complete and open recipe of how it was built. Tabby adopts an encoder-only patch Transformer architecture and concentrates the contributions on the data and the training procedure. The pretraining corpus combines an extended real-world collection, GIFT-Eval-Pretrain+ and BLAST, with synthetic data from KernelSynth and CauKerV2, an online generator that composes temporal dynamics through randomly sampled structural causal models. Training couples a progressive convergence schedule, which yields reusable intermediate checkpoints, with a deep quantile supervision objective for intermediate layers. The resulting 145M parameter backbone supports contexts of up to 8,192 observations and serves forecasting, classification, and anomaly detection, while a prompt-tuning module further improves in-distribution forecasting performance with the pretrained weights frozen. Tabby achieves competitive zero-shot forecasting performance on GIFT-Eval and the out-of-distribution TIME benchmark, while the same pretrained backbone also supports classification on the UCR Archive and zero-shot anomaly detection on TSB-AD-U. We release training pipeline and model as open source at huawei-noah/trustworthyAI.
  </details>

- **[CrevasseSeg: A Label-Efficient UAV Crevasse Segmentation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15790)**  `arXiv:2608.15790`  `cs.LG`  
  _Steven Wallace, William D. Harcourt, Richard Hann, Aiden Durrant, Somayajulu Sripada, Georgios Leontidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Crevasse mapping from uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) imagery supports glaciological research and field safety, but pixel-level annotation requires costly expert effort. We introduce CrevasseSeg, a binary segmentation evaluation framework from the terminus of Borebreen, Svalbard, comprising 2,244 unlabelled UAV orthomosaic tiles for self-supervised/unsupervised fine-tuning and 200 labelled tiles (24 validation, 176 test). We benchmark five self-supervised objectives (BYOL, Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD), Barlow Twins, VICReg and a combined BYOL-JSD) across O-Net, O-Net++ and a DINOv3-initialised O-Net. Frozen features are read out with a linear probe and an XGBoost classifier, both fit only on the 24 validation tiles. At the final decoder block, the two readouts invert. DINOv3 models are mostly the weakest under linear probing, often collapsing to a constant prediction, yet they are the strongest under XGBoost. This pattern does not hold at earlier decoder blocks. UMAP suggests why: DINOv3 fragments pixels into many small, locally interleaved clusters, whereas the convolutional models embed them on a single class-sorted manifold. Satellite-pretrained DINOv3 beats natural-image initialisation for every objective under XGBoost (+9.7 mDSC on average), but not consistently under linear probing. Our pipeline, DINOv3-ViT-L-Sat-O-Net-BYOL-JSD, reaches 74.75 mDSC / 61.16 mIoU (77.04 / 64.28 with a decoder-block ensemble), on par with BYOL alone, and outperforms RGB-pixel ML baselines. However, a frozen DINOv3-Sat encoder with an XGBoost readout alone reaches 77.18 / 64.17. It matches or exceeds every SSL variant and comes within 0.71 mDSC of a fully supervised DINOv3 U-Net, highlighting satellite foundation models as a strong baseline. We release CrevasseSeg as a benchmark for label-efficient segmentation in remote sensing.
  </details>

- **[ED-DiT: Physics-Guided Diffusion Pretraining for Transferable Molecular Representations from Electron Density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03260)**  `arXiv:2608.03260`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shuang Liang, Haocheng Wang, Jiayi Song, Shuquan Ye, Ben Fei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretraining has shown strong potential for learning transferable representations, yet it remains underexplored for electron-density-based molecular learning. Electron density provides a continuous three-dimensional description of molecular electronic structure, capturing both local spatial patterns and global physical quantities. This raises a key question: can electron-density fields be used for self-supervised pretraining to learn a shared representation that transfers across diverse electronic-structure-related tasks? We propose ED-DiT, a physics-guided Diffusion Transformer for self-supervised pretraining on electron-density point clouds. ED-DiT learns reusable representations by reconstructing corrupted and partially masked log-density fields across diffusion noise levels. An electron-number consistency constraint is further introduced to preserve the total electronic mass. The pretrained encoder can be adapted to property prediction, open-/closed-shell classification, molecule-electron-density retrieval, and molecule-conditioned electron-density prediction. Experiments on six EDBench tasks show that ED-DiT consistently outperforms the same architecture trained from scratch, especially under limited supervision. For molecule-conditioned electron-density prediction, it reduces RMSE from 2.2474 to 1.3753 and surpasses the available baseline. With only 10% labels, it improves orbital energy prediction RMSE from 0.0293 to 0.0138. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of physics-guided electron-density pretraining for learning transferable molecular representations.
  </details>

- **[EEG Foundation Models Need Channel Adaptation, but Fixed Channel Mappings Often Suffice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23091)**  `arXiv:2604.23091`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kuntal Kokate, Dung Truong, Pierre Guetschel, Bruno Aristimunha, Arnaud Delorme_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling EEG foundation models requires pooling data across heterogeneous montages, so every model needs a channel-adaptation step mapping an arbitrary source montage onto its expected input. Whether this step must be learned, and whether it confounds cross-model comparison, is untested. We compare five channel-adaptation routes (a learned Conv1d projection, spherical spline interpolation, source-space decomposition, Riemannian re-centering, and each model's native handling) across five pretrained models (5M--157M parameters), five downstream tasks, two training regimes, and up to 15 random seeds. The answer is architecture-dependent rather than adapter-universal: fixed or native mappings are best in 18 of 25 model--task cells, but regime-specific wins are distributed across Native/Conv1d/SSI/OmnEEG/Riemannian. Rigid fixed-input BENDR is the main case where a learned bridge is needed; the strongest model, CBraMod, reaches its best results without adding a learned channel adapter. Because a learned adapter adds a montage-dependent task parameter budget, we recommend reporting frozen-probe benchmarks under a fixed/native route, and treating learned adapters in full fine-tuning as part of the model being evaluated. Source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SVDP: Training-Free Contextual Sparsity Predictors for Fast LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14110)**  `arXiv:2603.14110`  `cs.LG`  
  _Georgii Serbin, Kirill Koshkin, Zhongao Sun, Anastasiya Bistrigova, C.C. Korikov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contextual sparsity is one of the approaches used to reduce computational complexity in the inference process of large language models (LLMs). Existing techniques for efficient LLM inference acceleration based on contextual sparsity with minimal accuracy degradation require training sparse pattern predictors. This paper presents a framework for accelerating inference of ReGLU-based feed-forward networks (FFNs) within LLMs. The proposed framework provides a fast, training-free method for building sparse pattern predictors using truncation-aware singular value decomposition (SVD) of the gate projection matrix, along with a threshold calibration algorithm, and inference executors supporting conditional computation on CUDA and CANN devices. Experiments on three sparse LLMs with an average activation sparsity level of 90% in the FFNs demonstrate up to a 1.8x reduction in end-to-end decoding time while maintaining less than 1% degradation in benchmark scores on tasks involving complex math and code generation. This work advances the deployment of LLMs on edge devices.
  </details>

- **[ELBO$_{\mathrm{TDS}}$: Temporal Distribution Shift Generalization for Industry-Scale Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21032)**  `arXiv:2511.21032`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Zhu, Cong Fu, Yabo Ni, Anxiang Zeng, Yuan Fang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Temporal distribution shift (TDS) degrades the generalization of recommender systems: models trained on past interactions face systematically different user behavior in future time windows. While incremental training on recent data is widely used in industrial production, it can over-adapt to transient patterns and exacerbate instability. Existing approaches such as invariant learning and self-supervised learning offer partial solutions but often suffer from unstable temporal generalization, representation collapse, or inefficient data utilization. To address these limitations, we propose ELBO$_{\mathrm{TDS}}$, a probabilistic framework for temporally robust recommendation. First, we identify key shifting factors in real-world production data and design a simple yet effective data augmentation strategy that simulates realistic time-varying factors and expands the training support. Second, we derive a self-supervised variational objective, ELBO$_{\mathrm{TDS}}$, to harness the benefits of this extended distribution while preventing representation collapse. Extensive experiments on both a public benchmark and a large-scale industrial dataset demonstrate that ELBO$_{\mathrm{TDS}}$ consistently improves next-period ranking performance over strong baselines. It achieves a 2.33\% uplift in GMV per user in online experiments and has been successfully deployed in production. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Analysis of Variational Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22994)**  `arXiv:2509.22994`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zachary Baker, Yuxiao Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Variational sparse autoencoders (vSAEs) replace a sparse autoencoder's deterministic Top-K mechanism with a sampled Gaussian posterior hypothesized to produce a more robust feature dictionary. To test this, we build permutation comparisons over training seeds for custom analyses, and score two popular benchmarks on one pair of existing checkpoints. When fixing the unit variance and ablating sampling on the vSAE, we find that the gap between the non-sampled vSAE and a Top-K SAE with an L2 activation penalty can be decomposed completely into a set of five implementation details. When we enable sampling and allow the network to learn its own variance, we find that the discrete selection process of Top-K fails when in conjunction with stochastic sampling methods. At the noise scale these models are trained upon, vastly different Top-K feature sets (Jaccard 0.075-0.29, chance 0.067) are found on equal model activations due only to the sampling noise, in two forward passes of one trained model on the same token. Soft methods such as JumpReLU fail to hold their sparsity target as well due to sampling, though under different failure modes. Noise moves its achieved sparsity, and at matched sparsity reconstruction fails to track selection stability (r = -0.55). Ablating the KL loss term recovers only 3.9 percent of the lost reconstruction. The rest is due to sampling.
  </details>

- **[Out-of-Distribution Detection on Graphs: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08105)**  `arXiv:2502.08105`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tingyi Cai, Yunliang Jiang, Yixin Liu, Zhonglong Zheng, Changqin Huang, Ming Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph learning has advanced prediction and discovery across a wide range of relational data applications. In evolving environments, new categories and changes in attributes or relationships can produce out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs on which models make confident but unreliable predictions. Graph out-of-distribution (GOOD) detection identifies such nodes or graphs to support rejection and further assessment. This paper presents the first systematic survey dedicated to GOOD detection. We clarify the problem settings and organize existing methods into six categories according to their primary OOD discrimination mechanisms. The review examines their technical connections, supervision and reference requirements, and conditions of use, alongside representative datasets, OOD constructions, benchmarks, and evaluation metrics. Controlled synthetic comparisons deepen the understanding of detection settings by illustrating how representative methods respond to different distribution shifts and graph conditions. We discuss open challenges and outline future research directions, aiming to provide a common foundation for advancing GOOD detection.
  </details>

- **[Caravan MultiMet: Extending Caravan with Multiple Weather Nowcasts and Forecasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.09459)**  `arXiv:2411.09459`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guy Shalev, Frederik Kratzert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Caravan large-sample hydrology dataset (Kratzert et al., 2023) was created to standardize and harmonize streamflow data from various regional datasets, combined with globally available meteorological forcing and catchment attributes. This community-driven project also allows researchers to conveniently extend the dataset for additional basins, as done 6 times to date (seethis https URL). We present a novel extension to Caravan, focusing on enriching the meteorological forcing data. Our extension adds three precipitation nowcast products (CPC, IMERG v07 Early, and CHIRPS) and three weather forecast products (ECMWF IFS HRES, GraphCast, and CHIRPS-GEFS) to the existing ERA5-Land reanalysis data. The inclusion of diverse data sources, particularly weather forecasts, enables more robust evaluation and benchmarking of hydrological models, especially for real-time forecasting scenarios. To the best of our knowledge, this extension makes Caravan the first large-sample hydrology dataset to incorporate weather forecast data, significantly enhancing its capabilities and fostering advancements in hydrological research, benchmarking, and real-time hydrologic forecasting.
