- **[DocDUR-Net: Degradation-Aware Unified Document Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34860)**  `arXiv:2611.34860`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haowei Xu, Peirong Zhang, Xuhan Zheng, Lianwen Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Captured document images often suffer from diverse degradations caused by uncontrolled imaging conditions, which reduce readability and obscure fine text structures. Single-degradation restoration methods are usually trained and deployed for individual degradation types, and therefore require model selection before restoration. Unified restoration models alleviate the need to maintain multiple restoration networks by sharing one network across tasks, but representative unified document image restoration methods still depend on external task selection. This dependency limits automatic document image restoration. We propose DocDUR-Net, a degradation-aware unified restoration network that derives restoration guidance directly from the input image. It introduces a Degradation Perception Module (DPM) to extract degradation representations and predict degradation weights, and Degradation Prior-guided Adaptive Feature Fusion (DP-AFF) to combine learnable degradation priors with degradation features extracted from the input. The resulting guidance is injected into a shared restoration backbone, enabling adaptive restoration without external task selection. DocDUR-Net also supports a manual mode with explicit task selection. Experiments on document image restoration benchmarks show that DocDUR-Net achieves the best average PSNR of 29.405 dB and SSIM of 0.960 among unified methods while remaining competitive with single-degradation methods.
  </details>

- **[Diffusion Steering via Adaptive Reward Estimation and Resampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34839)**  `arXiv:2611.34839`  `cs.CV`  
  _Abinitha Gourabathina, Sana Tonekaboni, Marzyeh Ghassemi, Akbar Rafiey_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image diffusion models can generate images that satisfy complex prompts, yet often produce images that omit requested objects, attributes, or relationships. We ask if models can more reliably generate text-aligned images by redistributing probability toward their more successful outcomes. We propose TrailMix, a training-free method that uses the base model's distribution of possible outcomes to adaptively guide generation toward better text alignment. During denoising, TrailMix generates inexpensive lookahead images from parallel trajectories (trails) and scores their alignment with the prompt using an external judge. The resulting distribution of reward scores allows us to estimate how strongly to steer generation toward a desired alignment level. We use these estimates to reweight and resample trajectories, favoring more promising outcomes. Across three text-to-image benchmarks, TrailMix outperforms existing baselines, with particularly strong gains on rare multi-object compositions. We further extend TrailMix to targeted image editing and introduce SEAM-Bench (Semantic Editing and Attribute Maintenance), a systematic benchmark evaluating whether semantic edits preserve other image content. Experiments on SEAM-Bench and existing benchmark PIE-Bench demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach for controlled image editing. Our results show how a model's own possible outcomes can inform reliable and controllable generation.
  </details>

- **[Reading, Not Adapting: Unlocking Frozen Visual Knowledge for AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34719)**  `arXiv:2611.34719`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuncheng Guo, Ying Dong, Xiaodong Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalization to unseen image generators has driven efforts to adapt pretrained visual representations for detection. Yet recent findings show that vision foundation models already encode transferable detection knowledge, while further adaptation can impair generalization. This motivates a shift from backbone adaptation to knowledge readout. Conventional linear probing offers a direct way to read this knowledge but is limited to final-layer RGB features. Multi-cue detectors broaden the evidence base, but their designs introduce other tradeoffs: adaptation may compromise pretrained transferability, heterogeneous encoders complicate feature integration, and late fusion precludes intermediate cross-view interaction. To overcome these limitations, we propose ReMoT, a Mixture-of-Transformers-inspired framework that reads complementary knowledge from spatially aligned RGB and wavelet views within a fully frozen visual foundation. ReMoT employs staged joint attention to enable controlled local and global cross-view interaction, while learnable fusion queries progressively read complementary evidence across Transformer depth without writeback. ReMoT achieves 91.2% average accuracy across eight benchmark groups with 3,074 trainable parameters, attaining the highest overall average among the evaluated methods. These results support structured knowledge readout as a promising alternative to backbone adaptation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Seeing the Right Context: Grounded On-Policy Distillation for Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34700)**  `arXiv:2611.34700`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yichang Luo, Hourun Zhu, Shixiong Liu, Kan Wei, Shi Liang, Yidan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote sensing scene understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to interpret queried targets in relation to nearby objects and surrounding structures, which serve as spatial references or define the scope of an answer. However, standard answers and bounding boxes often leave these contextual relationships implicit. Thus, we construct GeoContext-5K to make them explicit in 5,394 training pairs for visual question answering (VQA), with spatial cues, visual facts, and supporting regions. GeOPD uses these annotations for on-policy distillation, guiding a frozen teacher with region marks on the full image and a visual fact while preserving surrounding spatial references. It prioritizes teacher corrections that agree with the shift in the student's next token distribution when evidence is restored. A complementary objective weights the contrast between the student's scores on the two views by how much masking lowers the teacher's log probability for the same sampled token. Experiments on five remote sensing VQA benchmarks show that GeOPD outperforms the evaluated open-source MLLMs and matched post-training baselines, improving over the 4B and 9B base models by 5.81 and 2.97 percentage points on average. The dataset and code will be publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Counting Beyond Frames: Codec Motion Tells Frozen Diffusion VLMs How Many](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34657)**  `arXiv:2611.34657`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuoqin Li, Jiayu Ding, Ge Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counting how many times something happens is among the simplest questions about a video and among the hardest for video-language models, autoregressive and diffusion alike. The difficulty lies between the frames: a model samples only a few of them, while each repetition unfolds as movement in between. Video codecs already record this movement densely as block displacements, and codec-based video models use it to shape their visual input. We are the first to show that this motion provides direct evidence for the count: a candidate count is a testable motion hypothesis, and unwinding the cycles it implies measures how strongly the observed directions support it. We present CoDiCount, a unified training-free framework that turns this test into a count potential and applies it while a frozen diffusion vision-language model (dVLM) denoises its answer. At every denoising step, structured count messages map each option letter, or each grammar-valid edit of an open integer, to the count it expresses and reweight the model's own probabilities within that set. Consistent gains over the official decoders on MotionBench, TOMATO, and SVCBench demonstrate the broad generalization of CoDiCount across diffusion models, datasets, and diverse counting tasks. With CoDiCount, a 7B dVLM outperforms GPT-5.1 and Gemini-2.5-Pro on MotionBench, surpasses GPT-4o and much larger open models on TOMATO, and matches GPT-5.4 on SVCBench.
  </details>

- **[Commit2Drive: Perception-Grounded Language Decisions as Commitments for Trajectory Planning in Vision-Language-Action Driving Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34614)**  `arXiv:2611.34614`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zikai Zhang, Hubert P. Shum, Toby Breckon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models introduce language reasoning into end-to-end autonomous driving. They output a language decision together with a trajectory, which makes the planner more interpretable. However, in existing methods the language decision is either an independent output or an index that selects among similar candidate trajectories, and its annotations are not verified against the trajectory. As a result, the language decision and the trajectory are often inconsistent in closed-loop driving. To address this, we propose Commit2Drive, a framework that imposes the language decision as a consistency constraint on trajectory planning. The decision is a commitment, a maneuver class with a continuous parameter that one deterministic rule reads from any trajectory, and it is grounded in facts reported by the perception of the model itself. A Maneuver Bank maps the commitment to a code with a kinematic meaning, and consistency losses train the planner to execute every admissible decision of a scene, so that the trajectory follows the decision. An automatic data pipeline supplies the supervision. Decisions are labeled from the future motion of the expert, counterfactual executions train the decision and the trajectory together, and rule-based functions with a Vision-Language Model (VLM) generate, review and refine the language annotations. The code is a lookup that involves no hidden state of the language model, so Commit2Drive is extensible to rule-based decisions, which the same planner executes without retraining. Commit2Drive uses a 0.5B language model and runs closed-loop inference online on a single consumer graphics card. On Bench2Drive, Commit2Drive reaches a Driving Score (DS) of 78.50 and a Success Rate (SR) of 55.91% with a lightweight model, which is competitive with the state of the art, and greatly reduces the language-action inconsistency. Code will be released soon.
  </details>

- **[DuCo: Exploiting Dual Cross-Layer Consistency for Sparse Geometric Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34608)**  `arXiv:2611.34608`  `cs.CV`  
  _Liangyu Xiang, Junyu Gao, Xiaofeng Wang, Changsheng Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense global attention is crucial for capturing cross-view geometry in visual geometry foundation models, yet its quadratic complexity limits efficient inference on long image sequences. Existing sparse attention methods primarily select entries of the global attention matrix based on attention probabilities and construct masks independently across layers, overlooking value redundancy and cross-layer information reuse. By analyzing attention distributions and clustering value representations across layers, we reveal two forms of \emph{cross-layer consistency}, characterized by highly similar attention probability distributions and localized changes in value redundancy structures. Building on these observations, we propose \textbf{DuCo}, a training-free sparse attention framework that exploits historical attention information and value redundancy for efficient geometric inference. Specifically, DuCo leverages historical attention information to estimate current-layer dense attention probabilities and introduces value clustering to exploit redundancy, with clusters efficiently refined through local updates across layers. Both factors are integrated through probability-weighted value distortion to jointly optimize four cluster representation states under an attention budget for sparse mask construction. Experiments across three visual geometry foundation models and five benchmarks demonstrate favorable accuracy--efficiency trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[You Only Look Multiple Times: YoloMT, a Real-Time Streaming Model on a T4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34601)**  `arXiv:2611.34601`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nessim Ben Abbes, Duc Han Le, Sabri Mtibaa, Van-Tam Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a new model for real-time streaming captioning on a single GPU. YoloMT is a 449.8M-parameter streaming captioner that provides Pareto-frontier performance among generative vision models in its class. Distilled from a 27B teacher by SFT and GRPO, it leads every model under 1B parameters on OVO-Bench, MVBench, StreamingBench and Video-MME, answered from its captions, and beats LFM2.5-VL-1.6B and SmolVLM2-2.2B. YoloMT is designed to read more frames cheaply and make them informative, encoding each frame once and caching it as 16 tokens. This allows the model to incorporate and infer from past frames without additional vision passes, making it efficient and effective. We also provide a detailed recipe for training YoloMT, with its stages and data, as well as ablations that demonstrate the contribution of each component.
  </details>

- **[SEAM: A Self-Evolving and Adaptive Probing Framework for Automatic MLLM Hallucination Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34598)**  `arXiv:2611.34598`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shiyu Zhang, Zengyi Wo, Yuming Liao, Ran He, Caifeng Shan, Yanbo Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The severe susceptibility of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to hallucinations poses a critical threat to their reliability, making a rigorous evaluation framework indispensable. However, existing evaluation pipelines largely rely on predefined test items, suffering from high construction costs, rapid obsolescence as MLLMs evolve, and ineffectiveness in probing model-specific weaknesses. To address these limitations, we propose SEAM, a self-evolving and adaptive probing framework for automatic MLLM hallucination evaluation. Specifically, SEAM introduces a composable Hallucination Probe Construction Library comprising ten hallucination-probing skills, three elicitation wrappers, and five question formats. Building on this library, SEAM establishes a dual-loop framework. In the inner loop (adaptive probing), skill-guided visual analysis, question composition, and hierarchical quality verification are coordinated with verifier and target-response feedback to refine test items and uncover model-specific hallucinations. In the outer loop (skill evolution), a skill updater distills historical probing trajectories into localized, reusable rule updates, which undergo held-out validation and are frozen before testing. Driven by these innovative structural designs, SEAM establishes an automated and self-evolving evaluation paradigm. Experiments across four target MLLMs and ten hallucination types demonstrate that SEAM substantially outperforms representative static benchmarks, achieving an 89.2\% relative improvement in mean valid hallucination discovery rate across 12 matched comparisons.
  </details>

- **[ADClaw: A Programmable Visual Agent for Industrial Anomaly Detection and Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34591)**  `arXiv:2611.34591`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinyi Wu, Rongxin Gao, YuHui Lin, Yonglin Hong, Chu Li, Wei Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial anomaly detection (IAD), the task of identifying and localizing deviations from normal product conditions, is essential to automated quality inspection in manufacturing. Many vision-language models for IAD rely on large domain-specific datasets for fine-tuning and make predictions in a single pass, leaving no opportunity to re-examine ambiguous regions. Although tool-using agents can revisit these regions, predefined tool interfaces limit how they can combine intermediate results in a follow-up check. When such checks are insufficient, models may miss subtle defects or misidentify the defect type. To address this limitation, we introduce ADClaw, an inspection agent that uses executable code to test anomaly hypotheses without task-specific fine-tuning. For each hypothesis, a Structured Normality Prior identifies the applicable rules and retrieves normal visual references for the inspection target. Guided by this prior, the agent writes and executes code in a persistent Inspection Workbench to combine masks, anomaly maps, and measurements from earlier steps into a new test of the suspected defect. After each check, Hypothesis-Guided Evidence Reasoning assesses whether the evidence supports the suspected defect and its location, using gaps or inconsistencies in that evidence to guide the next code action. ADClaw achieves competitive results in detection and localization across 2D and 3D industrial anomaly detection benchmarks and in anomaly understanding on MMAD.
  </details>

- **[Feed-Forward Adaptive Gaussian Splatting for Interactive Medical Volume Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34478)**  `arXiv:2611.34478`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhongpai Gao, Benjamin Planche, Meng Zheng, Anwesa Choudhuri, Chaoyi Zhou, Terrence Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Path-traced rendering shows CT and MR volumes with realistic lighting, but it restarts after every change of view, clipping plane or transfer function (TF), the map from voxel intensity to colour and opacity, which limits the frame rate. Feed-forward Gaussian splatting models such as RenderFM predict the Gaussians directly from the volume instead of fitting them to each scan, and are fast. RenderFM, however, handles only CT, runs its network again after every TF edit, has no clipping operator, and places its Gaussians on a uniform 3-mm grid that blurs organ boundaries and cut faces. We present RenderFM-Next, a feed-forward model that addresses these limits. One network handles both CT and MR. Its Gaussians are predicted once per volume: a new TF needs no pass of this network, and a clipping plane cuts them exactly, using operators from prior work. The model predicts coarse 3-mm Gaussians for the whole volume and refines them to 1.5 mm only where refinement improves the image. A small score head ranks the coarse Gaussians by how much refining them would reduce the rendering error; at render time the user chooses how many are refined, and those next to a clipping plane are always refined, which sharpens cut faces. We build a benchmark of about 2,000 CT and MR scans with path-traced references under clinical presets, TF edits and clipping. Our RenderFM-Next improves over RenderFM retrained on this benchmark by 2.8 dB on CT and 2.4 dB on MR and renders a 1600x1600 view in 4-6 ms. Refinement adds 0.30 / 0.69 dB over the same model trained without it (0.51 / 1.17 dB on cut faces), and refining a learned 10% of the coarse Gaussians matches a random 50% with 2.4x fewer Gaussians. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Evidence-Aligned Benchmarking and Learning for Medical Video Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34465)**  `arXiv:2611.34465`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhongpai Gao, Anwesa Choudhuri, Meng Zheng, Benjamin Planche, Terrence Chen, Ziyan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A medical video language model should answer from the visual evidence its question asks about. We find three places where this breaks down and address each with an approach we call MedEvidU. First, benchmarks reward answers that ignore the video: on one dataset, the position of a clip within its recording predicts the surgical step in 37% of questions, and a constant answer beats every trained model on a standard safety-assessment metric. We build MedVidBench-v2, 34 tasks from 18 surgical and clinical video datasets, in which prompts and metrics are audited with answers given without video, and which tests unseen hospitals and windows cut blindly from whole recordings. Second, common video encoders merge consecutive frames into shared tokens, so a question about one frame is answered from a blend of two. Query-slot inputs give each queried frame its own tokens at almost no extra cost. Third, a fine-tuned model has memorized its training questions, which leaves little to learn from. Label-matched question resampling (LQR) adds unseen questions about the same recordings while keeping the answer distribution fixed, in a final supervised epoch and in reinforcement learning. Together, these raise the grounding IoU of a 4B model from 30.4 to 44.4, above a supervised 27B model, and instrument grounding at an unseen hospital from 33.9 to 72.5. On blindly cut windows, event-presence accuracy improves by 5.5 points and localization F1 by 8.1 points. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SceneDesigner++: A Unified 9-DoF Pose Control Framework for T2I and T2V Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34461)**  `arXiv:2611.34461`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xincheng Shuai, Zhenyuan Qin, Henghui Ding, Dacheng Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise 9-DoF pose control is essential for visual content generation, enabling users to specify object location, size, and orientation in 3D space. However, existing methods typically focus on either text-to-image (T2I) or text-to-video (T2V) generation, with limitations in pose controllability and temporal coherence. We present SceneDesigner++, a unified framework for 9-DoF pose control across both T2I and T2V generation. We construct ObjectPose9D++, a dataset containing high-quality images and videos with accurate pose annotations. Trained on ObjectPose9D++, SceneDesigner++ combines absolute and relative pose encoding strategies to decouple frame-wise spatial grounding from inter-frame motion modeling. Specifically, absolute pose encoding leverages CNOCS map sequences to provide explicit, frame-wise 9-DoF pose guidance. Meanwhile, our relative pose encoding, termed VPRoPE, incorporates relative pose information into rotary position embeddings to explicitly model inter-frame pose transitions, thereby promoting temporally coherent object motion. A learnable orthogonal transformation is further introduced for each attention head, allowing different heads to capture complementary geometric relationships while preserving input feature norms to promote training stability. We further adopt a two-stage training strategy to learn the absolute and relative pose encoding mechanisms separately. Benefiting from this lightweight and decoupled design, SceneDesigner++ also supports integration with an independently trained camera-control module, enabling separate or joint control over object and camera motion. Finally, we introduce T2IVPoseEval, a benchmark for evaluating diverse object poses and motion patterns in both T2I and T2V tasks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SceneDesigner++ consistently outperforms same-backbone baselines in pose fidelity and motion quality.
  </details>

- **[PreserveReward: Reliable Consistency Reward Modeling for Image Editing via Dual-Teacher On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34446)**  `arXiv:2611.34446`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xueheng Li, Yong Liu, Wen Xue, Shi-Yu Tian, Zhuofan Xia, Chengjun Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-guided image editing has a wide range of applications, yet existing models may inadvertently alter content that should remain unchanged. Reliable consistency evaluation is therefore essential for assessing content preservation and providing reward signals for Reinforcement Learning (RL). However, existing reward models may overlook subtle visual changes or fail to distinguish necessary modifications from unintended alterations, limiting the reliability of consistency assessment. To address this challenge, we propose PreserveReward, a consistency reward model for image editing that integrates visual On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD) to enhance fine-grained visual perception and consistency assessment. PreserveReward learns from reference preservation scores through a regression-based scoring loss and employs two complementary teacher views for self-distillation. The local teacher uses matched crops to identify subtle visual changes, while the joint teacher combines local details with global context to determine whether these changes align with the editing intent. To improve the reliability of distillation supervision, we calibrate teacher score distributions via minimum-KL projection under reference-score error constraints and adaptively select teacher process supervision based on gradient alignment with the scoring objective, mitigating scoring bias and optimization conflicts. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that PreserveReward achieves strong alignment with human preference on non-edited content preservation and generalizes well across diverse editing scenarios.
  </details>

- **[SPGSR: A Small Pixel-Space Generator for One-Step Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34418)**  `arXiv:2611.34418`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yun Yue, Luming Liang, Yiqi Zhong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In one-step super-resolution (SR), vision-only models extract semantic cues directly from the low-resolution (LR) image, keeping those cues spatially aligned with the structures they help restore. Recent models achieve high restoration quality with generators of up to 1.4 billion parameters. Given how much structure the LR image already provides, could a much smaller generator achieve comparable quality? We introduce SPGSR, a vision-only framework that adds a learned RGB correction to the LR image. A frozen DINOv2 guides prediction, and a lightweight block refines the correction locally. SPGSR achieves higher PSNR/SSIM and lower LPIPS/DISTS than VOSR, a leading vision-only SR method, across three benchmarks. Its $31.8\mathrm{M}$-parameter generator is just $6.5\%$ the size of VOSR-0.5B's. SPGSR also achieves competitive quality against T2I-based SR methods. SPGSR's generator uses only $0.60\%$ of the HR pixels used to train and distill VOSR's generator. For $512\times512$ outputs, SPGSR achieves $2.80\times$ and $3.35\times$ GPU-pipeline speedups over VOSR-0.5B and VOSR-1.4B, respectively.
  </details>

- **[AIRIS: Benchmarking Social Embodied Intelligence in Egocentric Streaming Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34347)**  `arXiv:2611.34347`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Zhou, Hongsheng Wu, Jiayu Tang, Yunxuan Mao, Hanjing Ye, Shenghao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents operating around people must not only recognize social cues, but also decide when the available evidence is sufficient, whether to intervene, and how to respond without violating social norms. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate these abilities in isolation or with access to completed videos, obscuring where an online social decision fails. We introduce AIRIS, a causal streaming benchmark that formulates social embodied intelligence as a time-anchored chain of Attention, Intention, Reaction, and Interaction (A-I-R-I). AIRIS contains 8,000 expert-curated questions grounded in 357 egocentric clips across 11 context categories and 36 capability dimensions. At each query, models receive only the observation prefix available at that time; decision-aware metrics reward calibrated waiting and complete reactions while penalizing premature commitment. Evaluating 45 proprietary and open-source general-purpose, embodied, streaming-video, and omni-modal models reveals fragmented and temporally miscalibrated competence. General-purpose VLMs excel at passive cue grounding and intent recognition, whereas embodied specialists lead on isolated reaction sub-decisions; yet across all families, models frequently guess intents before sufficient evidence emerges and fail to jointly align intervention timing, action domain, and concrete execution within the same episode. AIRIS thus provides a diagnostic foundation for bridging the gap between passive social perception and reliable embodied interaction.
  </details>

- **[Detector Operating Behavior Inference via Data-Space Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34252)**  `arXiv:2611.34252`  `cs.CV`  
  _Stefan Becker, Simon Weiss, Wolfgang Hübner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A detector scores candidate detections, but the recall and false alarms at a given confidence threshold depend on the input distribution. We introduce Detector Operating Behavior Inference (DOBI), a second-level inference task: estimating a fixed detector's threshold-dependent, distribution-level operating response from the internal evidence of a single unlabeled image. DOBI treats the detector's threshold response, i.e. recall, false positives, and precision across thresholds, as a latent behavioral state under the current data condition. Controlled data-space sampling probes this state space offline, and a lightweight monitor learns the inverse map from one image to this state, whose single functional output supports multiple operating policies. In a controlled proof of concept with a fixed YOLOv10-Nano person detector and COCO-derived degradations, predicted PR behavior reduces mean absolute deviation from 0.128 for an image-independent mean-behavior prior to 0.054, and predicted functional distances track empirical regime distances (Spearman 0.96). From one unlabeled image, the inferred behavior selects operating points as well as a non-deployable lookup that knows the true regime (macro-regime $F_1$ 0.6604 for both, versus 0.6407 under pristine calibration; oracle 0.6620). On this discrete benchmark, regime-supervised curve lookups are strong controls. Pre-specified continuous and compositional probing shows where functional inference matters: on unseen combinations, the functional decoder has a lower mean curve error than a template lookup, increasingly with distance from the training conditions, whereas the lookup is more accurate for interpolation. These results establish the feasibility of single-image inference of distribution-level detector behavior over the probed conditions. Source code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Analemma $\infty$: A Temporal Benchmark for Geospatial Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34240)**  `arXiv:2611.34240`  `cs.CV`  
  _Alex López-Cifuentes, Laura Martínez-Ferrer, Juan Ignacio Bravo Pérez-Villar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Satellite time series capture how land surfaces change over time. Pixel-wise temporal foundation models read a year of satellite observations per pixel, yet public benchmarks lack a temporal axis or subsample it to a few dates. We introduce Analemma, a temporal benchmark of six datasets that carry every 10 and 20 m Sentinel-2 band and a time series spanning a growing season, completed where their releases omit geometry, dates, or cloud masks. One frozen-encoder protocol evaluates all models across label ratios and three seeds, with two baselines without pretraining. The TESSERA model ranks first overall. A from-scratch model on monthly composites outranks every foundation model except OlmoEarth v1.3-Base, suggesting that temporal input explains most of the performance. Three evaluation choices left unstated by the source publications each shift TESSERA v1 by 7.6 to 18.6 mIoU, against top-two gaps of 0.6 to 2.8. Data, harness and leaderboard will be publicly released soon.
  </details>

- **[VibeVideoAgent: Closing the Perceptual Loop for Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34237)**  `arXiv:2611.34237`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuanyu Zheng, Luoyuan Zhang, Yancheng Long, Haoyang Xu, Muxi Diao, Yuzhe Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Code-driven video generation asks a coding agent to write and run rendering programs. This gives exact control over typography, data visualizations, synchronized audio, and multi-shot scenes, and it leaves an editable project behind. A program that exits cleanly can still render overlapping labels, a camera aimed away from its subject, broken object interactions, or narration that drifts out of sync, and none of these raise a runtime error. Feeding rendered frames and audio back into the coding session bloats its long-horizon context, while critiquing only the finished full-resolution render turns early design mistakes into costly rewrites. We introduce VibeVideoAgent, a training-free framework that lets coding agents inspect their drafts throughout production. Multimodal perception runs in short-lived contexts kept apart from the coder and returns compact, grounded Findings. Seven primitives operate on a unified multi-clock representation of assets and drafts: they explore media from coarse to fine, verify quoted requirements with evidence from every required modality, compare a draft with a reference or an earlier revision on separate clocks, and measure stream signals locally. A lightweight staged protocol has the agent test its riskiest decision on a minimal preview first. On all 239 VibeVideoBench tasks, with matched agent runtimes and a fixed perception model, VibeVideoAgent improves holistic audiovisual quality (L3) for Claude Opus 5.5 (4.03 -> 4.43, while L2 rubric compliance rises from 98.87% to 99.35%), GPT-6 Astra (4.03 -> 4.27), and Claude Sonnet 5.5 (3.97 -> 4.10), with the largest gains in audio-visual synchronization (up to +0.56). Controlled ablations show that each component contributes, and trajectory audits show that the granularity of code edits governs the trade-off between rubric adherence and perceptual quality.
  </details>

- **[OS-Omni: Benchmarking Generalist Computer-Using Agents on Long-Horizon Workflows Across Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34232)**  `arXiv:2611.34232`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hui Shen, Yunta Hsieh, Jianing Ma, Zhuoran Yin, Zhuhan Bao, Ziyuan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-using agents are increasingly expected to complete realistic user goals rather than isolated GUI actions. However, existing benchmarks typically isolate web, mobile, or desktop environments, leaving it unclear whether a single generalist agent can execute long-horizon workflows that span multiple operating systems and persistent application states. We introduce OS-Omni, a live cross-platform benchmark and execution environment for evaluating computer-using agents across Windows, Ubuntu, macOS, iOS, Android, and Web environments. OS-Omni is released in versioned waves of 300 tasks each; this paper focuses on the first release, v1, which contains 48 tasks per platform and 12 cross-platform workflows that pair a desktop with an Android phone. OS-Omni provides reproducible initialization, platform-specific adapters, trajectory logging, and executable final-state evaluators, and includes controlled service-backed applications for workflows involving account, cart, budget, calendar, message, and other persistent state. Human studies show that OS-Omni tasks require 67.54 interaction steps, substantially exceeding OSWorld references of 15.0 steps. On v1, the strongest evaluated agent, Claude Opus 5.5, reaches 68.5% average success, trailing the 77.3% human reference by 8.8 points; the gap is uneven across platforms, with frontier models exceeding humans on Linux but trailing them by more than 25 points on iOS and macOS. These results indicate that current agents remain far from platform-robust, long-horizon computer use, especially when success requires sustained state tracking and durable final-state changes.
  </details>

- **[EgoMyo: Physical Hand State Estimation from Egocentric Video and Wrist Electromyography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34224)**  `arXiv:2611.34224`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guanglong Sun, Kanglei Zhou, Ziteng Wang, Nana Wang, Hengkai Tan, Xiaolong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical hand interaction depends on configuration, contact, and force, yet nearly identical appearances can conceal different contact states and exerted forces. We therefore formulate physical hand state estimation from egocentric video and wrist surface electromyography (sEMG), combining visual geometry with neuromuscular evidence to jointly recover these coupled components. To support this task, we introduce EgoMyo, a benchmark spanning 5.75M video frames, 50 participants, 34 contact configurations, and repeated donnings, synchronizing these inputs with 3D hand pose and with contact and normal force registered onto the hand surface from bare-hand pressure. Controlled analysis reveals that vision resolves spatial structure while sEMG better estimates force magnitude, that sEMG's gains concentrate in regions where vision is least reliable, and that contact localization limits force accuracy. Guided by these findings, we propose MyoBridge, which makes the hand region the unit of vision-sEMG fusion, lets each region gate its own share of sEMG, and distributes regional force magnitudes only over predicted contact vertices at inference. On held-out trials, MyoBridge reduces pose and force error by 39.8% and 18.5% over the strongest baseline and achieves the lowest pose error on unseen sessions and subjects.
  </details>

- **[Variation-aware Temporal Correspondence for Self-Supervised Video Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34220)**  `arXiv:2611.34220`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yang Liu, Qianqian Xu, Peisong Wen, Siran Dai, Qingming Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised video representation learning has achieved remarkable progress by leveraging large-scale unlabeled video data. Recent methods commonly extend Masked Image Modeling (MIM) to Masked Video Modeling (MVM), where video representations are learned by capturing semantic information from temporal contexts during masked content recovery. Unlike static images, videos exhibit inherent temporal variation, as visual objects continuously change their positions, appearances, and motion states across frames. Therefore, MVM needs to preserve semantic consistency while capturing temporal changes of corresponding regions, which raises two central challenges: 1) how to establish reliable temporal correspondence across frames, and 2) how to formulate an appropriate representation learning objective for modeling both semantic consistency and temporal variation. To address these challenges, we propose Variation-aware Temporal Correspondence for video Representation learning (VT-CoRe) framework. For challenge 1), VT-CoRe introduces a sandwich sampling strategy to construct two-sided temporal references and a Patch Registration Module (PRM) to establish reliable patch-level correspondence. For challenge 2), VT-CoRe imposes latent-space semantic alignment on registered representations through a self-distillation architecture and further proposes temporal variation modeling to constrain representation trajectories over time. Experiments on 8 downstream tasks across 16 benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and applicability of VT-CoRe across pixel-level, frame-level, video-level, and vision-based robotic learning scenarios. Complementary representation analysis shows that VT-CoRe learns a more structured latent space for variation-aware temporal correspondence learning.
  </details>

- **[Admoab-DETR: Resolution-Tiered Fusion for Infrared-Visible Object Detection with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34163)**  `arXiv:2611.34163`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenchao Li, Haoran Jia, Shuyuan Wen, Keyan Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Infrared and visible images offer complementary cues for all-weather object detection, yet real-world sensor dropouts can leave one stream unavailable and degrade paired-input detectors. Meanwhile, existing modality-adaptive methods apply heavy cross-modal attention uniformly across scales, incurring prohibitive token overhead on high-resolution maps. We propose Admoab-DETR, a unified real-time detector supporting infrared-only, visible-only, and paired inputs with a single set of weights. Specifically, \emph{Resolution-Tiered Fusion} (RTF) allocates fusion capacity by spatial token density and feature granularity: a lightweight Spatial Modality Gate governs fine local fusion at the highest-resolution scale with zero-cost single-view pass-through, while channel-compressed deformable Admoab necks capture cross-modal correspondence at coarser semantic scales. Furthermore, \emph{Mode-Matched Feature Distillation} (MMFD) aligns multi-scale encoder features with an availability-synchronized full-fusion teacher, avoiding ill-posed cross-modal hallucination while cutting parameters by 18.1\% and paired latency by 39.6\% at zero inference overhead. We also construct Admoab, a strictly registered 20,000-pair infrared--visible benchmark across diverse scenes. Experiments show Admoab-DETR achieves state-of-the-art accuracy--efficiency trade-offs across missing-modality and paired benchmarks. Our code will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HSTTA: Hierarchical Selective Test-Time Adaptation for Label-Free Cross-Subject EEG-to-Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34153)**  `arXiv:2611.34153`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongzhou Chen, Yuhao Wei, Lianghua He, Yihang Liu, Zengqiang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-subject EEG-to-image retrieval suffers from substantial performance degradation on new subjects due to subject shift. Test-time adaptation (TTA) can mitigate this shift by adapting pretrained models with unlabeled target-subject EEG. However, most existing TTA methods struggle to match supervision granularity to each query's prediction confidence, leaving many queries without reliable pseudo-labels. To address this, we propose a novel framework named Hierarchical Selective Test-Time Adaptation (HSTTA). HSTTA builds a hierarchy over gallery image embeddings, uses hierarchical selective prediction to assign each EEG query a supervision granularity matching its current prediction confidence, and constructs adaptation objectives accordingly. We further propose Cross-Session Consistency Calibration (CSCC), an unsupervised method that calibrates the global confidence threshold to target a predefined accuracy level for hierarchical predictions. Moreover, we introduce Reciprocal Hierarchical Backoff (RHB), which moves queries lacking reverse-matching support to coarser nodes to mitigate error reinforcement. Experiments on two public benchmarks and ablation studies validate the effectiveness of HSTTA and its key components. On THINGS-EEG2, HSTTA outperforms the strongest EEG-specific TTA baseline by 17.9 (13.4) and 8.9 (3.7) percentage points in Top-1/Top-5 accuracy, respectively, under cosine (CSLS) ranking. Further analyses show that HSTTA yields more accurate pseudo-labels during adaptation, supporting more reliable model updates.
  </details>

- **[A Calibration-Referenced Protocol for Measuring Decision Stability under Reference Bank Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34134)**  `arXiv:2611.34134`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiwen Chen, Shengming Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference banks used for industrial image inspection can be compressed to fit a deployment budget, but the resulting anomaly decisions depend on both the compressed scores and the operating threshold. We present a four-step protocol that compares compression methods at matched deployment sizes, establishes a reference distribution from 200 recalibrations, evaluates disagreement against two calibration benchmarks, and measures changes by distance from the full-bank threshold. With WideResNet-50, budget-matched product quantization (PQ) at the 10% budget has the lowest decision disagreement among five matched methods, including int8 scalar quantization, on VisA and MVTec AD at both operating levels. Int8 scalar quantization gives lower disagreement than PQ at the other four budgets. On MVTec AD at the lower operating level, PQ meets the calibration median in 34/39 cells and the upper quantile in 39/39 cells. Category-equally weighted image-level AUROC loss at this budget ranges from 0.0003 to 0.0037 across the evaluated configurations. On VisA at the 1% budget and a 1% operating level, disagreement reaches 28.12% (95% CI 23.25--33.58%) among near-threshold anomalies and 1.30% (0.79--2.14%) across the normal holdout. The 10% budget-matched PQ setting retains the lowest disagreement among four compared methods with ResNet-18, with clean reference banks, and when thresholds are estimated from normal-directory probes. The protocol connects deployment size to a calibration-referenced decision measure and identifies the evaluation populations most informative for inspection.
  </details>

- **[Detectable by Design: Learning a Continuous 3D Saliency Field from 2D Detector Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34108)**  `arXiv:2611.34108`  `cs.CV`  
  _Han Sun, Jun Li, Christy Jie Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The 3D points kept in a map decide whether a camera can be localized against it, and this choice matters more as the map becomes sparser. Existing 3D keypoint detectors usually define saliency by semantics, repeatability, reconstruction or descriptor matchability. None of these definitions asks whether a point will actually be detected in an image, and as a result most of the points they select are not detected again in images.In this paper, we define 3D saliency as image detectability: the response of a 2D interest-point detector at a surface point, aggregated over all views in which the point is visible. We propose DetField, a continuous field over 3D space that takes a point cloud as input and predicts detectability at any location around it. The supervision is the response of a 2D detector, back-projected from images onto the point cloud. Multi-scale curvature invariants are passed to the decoder at every query point, bypassing the voxel pooling of the encoder.In a localization benchmark that isolates the choice of 3D points, DetField outperforms Harris-3D and five learned 3D keypoint methods on objects and on real indoor scenes. On unseen rooms, it improves localization success by 7.4 points over Harris-3D, the strongest baseline. On objects, it also leads under a 2D detector not used in training. On sparse maps, it stays ahead of Harris-3D on both objects and real scenes.
  </details>

- **[What Makes a Gavagai? Learning Visual Concepts from One Positive and Few Negatives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34105)**  `arXiv:2611.34105`  `cs.CV`  
  _Katsuya Ogata, Kazuya Nishimura, Yuta Nakashima_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans can recognize novel concepts from only a few observations by identifying defining visual attributes and eliminating alternative interpretations. However, a single observation is consistent with multiple possible concepts, and accurate visual perception alone is insufficient to determine the intended interpretation. Negative evidence can reduce this ambiguity by eliminating competing hypotheses. Inspired by Quine's gavagai problem, we introduce the Gavagai Benchmark to evaluate whether vision-language models can identify the attributes defining a novel concept from positive and negative examples. The benchmark consists of synthetic images depicting novel combinations of visual attributes and real images of newly described species. Using these datasets, we examine how models use negative examples to identify novel concepts and analyze the respective roles of visual perception and reasoning. Several models struggle even when correct attributes are provided, while others show large gaps when ground-truth attributes are replaced with model predictions. These diagnostic comparisons highlight challenges in both attribute perception and inference from limited evidence.
  </details>

- **[TaCo: Rethinking Correlation for Visual Tracking on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34084)**  `arXiv:2611.34084`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qingmao Wei, Fagui Liu, Quan Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lightweight trackers have replaced cross-correlation with attention, and on low-power edge hardware they pay for it, since memory-bound softmax, normalization and reshaping operators of attention cost far more time than their arithmetic suggests. Benchmarking efficient trackers on an edge Neural Processing Unit (NPU) and an Arm CPU under one numerically validated protocol, we find that correlation trackers are the fast ones, but they lag in accuracy unless they adopt a ViT backbone, which brings attention back. We revisit what correlation lacks. Template--search interaction serves two roles: conditioning the search features on the target and matching them against it spatially. Attention does both, whereas correlation only matches, and does so on features extracted without knowledge of the target. We present TaCo (Target-aligned Correlation), which supplies the missing conditioning without attention by letting the template enter the per-frame network only as operands computed once per video. A descriptor of the target scales and shifts the search features channel by channel inside the backbone, and kernels aligned with the target box, each with a weight predicted from the template, correlate with them in one matrix product. With the backbone fixed, our correlation remains essential while incurring negligible NPU overhead, offering a better speed-accuracy trade-off than the attention-based interactions we tested. TaCo-T achieves 62.3% LaSOT AUC at 184 FPS on the Rockchip RV1126B NPU and 96 FPS on a Raspberry Pi 5 CPU, 3.0x and 3.5x the NPU speed of HiT-Tiny and AsymTrack-T at higher accuracy. TaCo-B reaches 65.6% at 50 FPS on the NPU, more than twice the speed of HiT-Base, AsymTrack-B and LightFC-vit, again at higher accuracy. Code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When to Respond, Where to Ground: Streaming 3D Affordance Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34067)**  `arXiv:2611.34067`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenting Luan, Weilong Yan, Siqi Lu, Ming Li, Beibei Lin, Yongbin Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a continuous RGB-D stream and a language request, streaming 3D affordance grounding requires an agent to decide when to respond and where to locate the requested functional part using only observations available so far. Existing 3D affordance grounding methods typically assume complete scene observations, while streaming vision-language models lack fine-grained 3D functional grounding. Our goal is to jointly determine response timing and functional-part localization during continuous scene observation. We propose StreamAff, an evidence-guided response strategy that makes both decisions from the grounding model's own visual evidence. The model scores the visual patches of the frames it reads: their combined support, weighed against a wait option, decides when to respond, and the highest-scoring patch selects the frame to ground in. A conditional look-back policy lets the model answer from an earlier observation at the moment of the request or wait for new evidence, and zoom-and-refine grounding segments the part named in the answer on the 3D points of the selected frame. We further introduce StreamFun3D, a benchmark derived from real-world 3D scenes that evaluates response timing and grounding across four evidence settings: Current, History, Future, and Unobserved, together with StreamIoU, a metric that credits a mask only when the response is timely and grounded in a valid source frame. We adapt offline 3D affordance grounding methods and build external-decider baselines that pair vision-language models with our mask decoder. StreamAff achieves 30.14% StreamIoU on 15-second streams, a 40.6% relative improvement over the strongest of these baselines.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Diffusion Acceleration: Methods, Trade-Offs, and Open Questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34056)**  `arXiv:2611.34056`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaofeng Mao, Fu-yun Wang, Le Zhuo, Zhengkai Jiang, Shaohao Rui, Weijie Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and flow-based generative models support high-quality image, audio, and video synthesis. Sampling can require dozens to hundreds of denoiser evaluations, with each pass becoming more expensive as resolution, video length, or model size increases. This cost affects latency and energy use in interactive and edge applications. We examine four approaches to accelerating inference: distillation, consistency training, sampling methods, and denoiser design. We express training objectives, transition maps, and numerical updates in a common notation to compare changes to the learning target, the sampling trajectory, and the work performed within each denoiser pass. Published image and video benchmarks illustrate the quality--cost trade-offs of one- and few-step generation, the interaction between solvers and timestep schedules, the effect of feature caching, and the difference between first-frame latency and sustained streaming throughput. We close with open problems in visual-native architectures and post-compression distillation, solver-aware few-step training, and semantic trajectory alignment with factorized distribution matching.
  </details>

- **[FasterGRN: Reducing the Refinement Redundancy for Accelerating Generative Refinement Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33982)**  `arXiv:2611.33982`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziqian Zhang, Pengcheng Wang, Yulong Chen, Yaxing Wang, Senmao Li, Kai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Departing from the paradigm of diffusion and autoregressive models, Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) enables high-quality image generation through iterative refinements. However, this repeated processing over the entire visual representations results in substantial computation overhead. Furthermore, the binary representations and global refinement mechanism in GRN disable the direct application of acceleration strategies developed for other generative models. To accelerate GRN model, we first observe several key properties by analyzing the entire generative process. That includes the stage characteristic, dual branch similarity and more importantly, the $\textbf{refinement redundancy}$. Motivated by the observations, we present $\textbf{FasterGRN}$, a training-free acceleration method for the Generative Refinement Network. $\textbf{FasterGRN}$ first divides the GRN into the semantic establishment stage and detail refinement stage according to its semantic establishment. For alleviating the refinement redundancy mainly resulting from the detail refinement stage, we propose Adaptive Retention Expansion ($\textbf{ARE}$) and Selective Token Freezing ($\textbf{STF}$) to shrink refinement steps and reduce token computations, respectively. In the experiments on GenEval and DPG-Bench benchmarks, our method $\textbf{FasterGRN}$ achieves a $2.03\times$ sampling speedup while largely preserving image generation performance. Our evaluation on COCO datasets additionally demonstrates that $\textbf{FasterGRN}$ even outperforms the original GRN in image fidelity.
  </details>

- **[Where to Approximate: Understanding and Accelerating Looped Multi-View Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33948)**  `arXiv:2611.33948`  `cs.CV`  
  _Siyuan Zhang, Yuhan Chen, Yangyang Ning, Nan Wang, Jun Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped multi-view reconstruction refines scene features by repeatedly applying a shared transformer block. Making these updates cheaper requires knowing how an approximation at one step affects the rest of the refinement. We study this with paired interventions in Déjà View and stacked reconstruction transformers, separating the error an approximation injects, how that error propagates, and how it affects the output. In Déjà View, approximation errors are attenuated more strongly than matched random perturbations under a scale-adjusted state-error measure, and a local Jacobian decomposition separates the contribution of channel gating from the response to spatial error structure. Spatial approximations made early benefit from subsequent refinement, whereas cross-step reuse depends on which quantity is cached and how reuse steps are spaced. At equal final norm, approximation errors change the predicted rotations 1.55 times as much as random errors. These findings guide our proposed method, which reduces spatial resolution and pools keys and values early in refinement, then interleaves attention-output reuse with full attention, and is adapted through lightweight self-distillation. Across five reconstruction benchmarks, the balanced configuration runs $1.63\times$ faster than the official 16-step Déjà View model with a 1.86-point reduction in average pose AUC@$3^\circ$. At matched latency, it exceeds the interpolated curve of a trained baseline using full refinement blocks by 4.73 points in AUC@$3^\circ$ and improves the corresponding geometry metrics.
  </details>

- **[TAT-Det: A Dataset for Testicular Appendage Torsion Detection and Segmentation in Ultrasound Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33940)**  `arXiv:2611.33940`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ying Jiang, Juntao Jiang, Linxuan Fan, Lingling Li, Yaqi Wang, Xiuzhen Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Testicular appendage torsion (TAT) is the leading cause of acute scrotal pain in prepubertal children. Yet, its diagnosis remains challenging due to nonspecific clinical manifestations and the absence of standardized datasets for pediatric acute scrotal emergencies.Results: We present TAT-Det, an expert-annotated ultrasound dataset specifically designed for TAT. TAT-Det comprises 2,600 ultrasound images from 1,203 patients collected at the Children's Hospital, Zhejiang University School of Medicine. Each TAT-positive image is accompanied by lesion-level bounding boxes for detection and pixel-level segmentation masks for fine-grained delineation. Annotations were produced through a multi-stage workflow involving independent annotation by board-certified pediatric sonographers, consensus review, and refinement. Baseline benchmarks for object detection and semantic segmentation using representative deep learning models are further provided, with YOLO11m achieving the best detection performance and RWKV-UNet the best segmentation performance.Conclusions: TAT-Det addresses the lack of publicly available resources for pediatric emergency ultrasound and establishes a foundation for developing and evaluating artificial intelligence methods for automated TAT analysis.
  </details>

- **[Event-WAM: Learning to Act with Event-Derived Futures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33917)**  `arXiv:2611.33917`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jongseong Bae, Hyunwoo Kim, Yeongin Lee, Eubin So, Ha Young Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Action Models (WAMs) leverage future visual modeling for robotic action learning, with recent studies exploring alternative or complementary objectives beyond conventional RGB prediction. We introduce Event-WAM, which incorporates event-derived visual changes as auxiliary future supervision alongside RGB. Event-WAM constructs temporally structured event representations from RGB demonstrations and encodes them using a frozen pretrained RGB video VAE, without training an event-specific VAE. Joint RGB--Event latent flow matching provides appearance- and change-oriented supervision without requiring event observations or explicit future generation at inference. Across simulated benchmarks, Event-WAM maintains comparable performance under standard conditions, while robustness gains under perturbations are benchmark-dependent, including improvements on LIBERO-Plus. Real-world evaluations further demonstrate improved manipulation success under both standard and unseen perturbation conditions. Further analyses show that policy improvements are not consistently accompanied by higher future prediction fidelity, suggesting the potential of change-oriented supervision for action learning.
  </details>

- **[RelPRM: Grounding Visual Process Reward Models in Relational Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33903)**  `arXiv:2611.33903`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaoxing Zhang, Ziqiu Gui, Cheng Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual process reward models (PRMs) can detect calculation errors yet assign high scores to steps that misread visual relations. Supervision based on subsequent answer success does not directly establish whether a step's relational claim holds. To strengthen visual-relation verification, we first build an automated pipeline that programmatically verifies each claim and identifies the required objects, relations, and image regions. It produces RelProcessTrain and RelProcessBench (RPB), which tests whether scores respond appropriately to relational evidence through controlled image edits and step rephrasings. Building on this pipeline, we further introduce RelPRM, a visual-relation-oriented method for process reward modeling with two training mechanisms. Relation-evidence self-distillation constructs views preserving necessary objects, relations, and dependencies, reducing the burden of finding decisive evidence in complex scenes and transferring reliable evidence-assisted judgments to full images. Relation-evidence-driven consistency training encourages appropriate score changes when relation edits reverse a step's truth, stability under irrelevant edits, and similar score changes across paraphrases. At inference, RelPRM directly scores the current step using only the original image and reasoning context. RelPRM achieves 66.2% on VisualProcessBench (VPB) and 68.4% on RPB, gains of 4.2 and 9.9 percentage points over VisualPRM-8B. These results show that training for visual relations improves both relational verification and step assessment across broader multimodal reasoning tasks.
  </details>

- **[LayoutRecall: Preserving Appearance in Multi-Round 3D Layout-Controlled Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33885)**  `arXiv:2611.33885`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yankuan Chi, Xin Lin, Baihan Yang, Yuheng Liu, Jason Li, Truong Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D layout-controlled image editing gives users explicit control over object placement and camera viewpoint. In a sequence of edits, however, the latest image can lose the appearance of an object when it is occluded or leaves view. The target layout specifies where the object should reappear, while its appearance must be recovered from earlier observations. We introduce LayoutRecall, a multi-round editor that stores and reuses appearance in the local coordinate systems of 3D boxes. Box-Surface Latent Memory retains image latents on box faces and retrieves them along rays in the target view. Geometry-Aligned Memory Conditioning supplies the retrieved tokens to a FLUX.2 editor using target-view positions, shared box-local embeddings, and object-wise attention. A correspondence loss encourages the editor to attend to matching observations during denoising. We also introduce LayoutLoop, a multi-round 3D layout-controlled image editing benchmark with calibrated layouts and designed revisits. We conduct extensive experiments on LayoutLoop and real-scene data to verify the ability of our method. The project page is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[X-Matte: VAE-Free Pixel-Space Flow Matching for Single-Step Image Matting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33874)**  `arXiv:2611.33874`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zanyi Wang, Jiaang Li, Dengyang Jiang, Ziru Wang, Chengzu Li, Davit Abrahamyan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained generative models offer rich visual priors for image matting, but their adaptation often relies on variational autoencoders (VAEs), whose spatial compression can degrade fine alpha details and introduce additional inference overhead. To address these limitations, we propose X-Matte, a VAE-free pixel-space flow matching framework for single-step image matting. By eliminating latent compression, X-Matte formulates the RGB-to-alpha transition as a continuous flow directly in pixel space. We adopt trajectory-wide x-prediction with transition-aware alpha supervision, enabling the model to learn from intermediate states while directly predicting the target alpha matte in a single forward pass. This design avoids both VAE encoding and decoding and iterative sampling. Extensive experiments demonstrate performance competitive with state-of-the-art trimap-free matting methods on P3M benchmarks, together with promising zero-shot generalization to RWP-636 and AIM-500. Moreover, X-Matte achieves up to a 9.4x inference speedup over a constructed single-step latent counterpart at high resolution, demonstrating the effectiveness of VAE-free pixel-space modeling for accurate and efficient image matting.
  </details>

- **[MAFNet-UR$^3$: Mask-Aware Fusion Network for Unified Raindrop and Reflection Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33866)**  `arXiv:2611.33866`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yeongjin Jeong, Hyunjin Cho, Minsang Kim, Jiwon Lee, Jae Woong Soh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Raindrops and reflections frequently co-occur on windshields and jointly obscure scene content, making their unified removal, referred to as UR$^3$, a highly ill-posed problem. Existing approaches are mainly based on diffusion models and thus can produce visually plausible restorations. However, they may generate scene-inconsistent structures in severely corrupted regions. To address this limitation, we propose MAFNet-UR$^3$, which adopts a three-stage framework to exploit multiple observations for scene-consistent unified raindrop and reflection removal. Specifically, Stage I suppresses raindrops while preserving the reflection layer, and Stage II performs prior-guided reflection removal followed by image-space refinement. Stage III estimates degradation masks from intermediate restoration results to provide degradation cues and performs mask-aware multi-scale fusion across observations to preserve scene structure and fine details. As a result, our proposed MAFNet-UR$^3$-L achieves state-of-the-art performance on the RDRF-testing benchmark, outperforming DiffUR$^3$ by 2.33 dB in PSNR and 2.7634 in the challenge score. Our method ranked third in the LoViF 2026 UR$^3$ Challenge, achieving a final score of 41.1416.
  </details>

- **[OmniMeld: Enhancing Audio-Visual Understanding via Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33861)**  `arXiv:2611.33861`  `cs.CV`  
  _Daili Hua, Yifan Dai, Bohan Zeng, Yuran Wang, Zihao Wu, Chengzhuo Tong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio-visual understanding requires perceptual grounding, temporal understanding, and semantic inference. Multi-teacher distillation can consolidate these capabilities, but direct probability matching does not explicitly capture teacher prediction changes across audio-visual conditions. We introduce OmniMeld, a multi-teacher on-policy distillation method that integrates domain expertise through modality-response-guided distribution matching. We train four teachers covering grounding and correspondence, temporal dynamics and event structure, causal and inferential reasoning, and contextual and holistic understanding. Each sample is routed to its corresponding teacher for supervision conditioned on student-generated answer prefixes. We derive transport costs from teacher prediction changes over shared candidate tokens across audio-visual conditions, using optimal transport to match student predictions to those of the teacher under complete audio-visual input. We also construct OmniMeld-20K for domain-specialized teacher training. The dataset spans four capability domains, with responses structured into evidence, reasoning, and answer sections and verified through consistency checks and media-based review. Experiments on audio-visual and general video understanding benchmarks demonstrate competitive teacher performance among similarly sized open-source models. The student improves over its base model across all evaluated benchmarks and surpasses the teachers' base model on several benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Structured Noise Diffusion for Echocardiography Haze Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33860)**  `arXiv:2611.33860`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zike Luo, Hao Li, Mengxiao Wang, Qingya Li, Ching-Hui Sia, Lei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Echocardiographic images are frequently degraded by spatially structured haze artifacts that obscure anatomical boundaries and hinder clinical analysis. Conventional diffusion models typically employ pixel-independent Gaussian noise, limiting their ability to represent localized ultrasound haze. In this study, we propose a structure-informed diffusion framework that explicitly models ultrasound haze as an accumulation of localized Gaussian components constrained by the ultrasound fan geometry. The forward process generates spatially correlated corruption by sampling component locations and scales, providing a formulation that better reflects the spatial characteristics of real echo artifacts. During reverse restoration, a dual-head network predicts an anchor probability map and Gaussian scale parameters to progressively remove structured artifacts while reducing the center bias associated with direct coordinate regression. We evaluated the framework on the public DehazingEcho2025 benchmark and two private clinical datasets, without retraining or fine-tuning on either private cohort. Our method achieved the highest mean generalized contrast-to-noise ratio across all three datasets, reaching $0.715 \pm 0.090$ on the public benchmark while preserving tissue edges.
  </details>

- **[MMotion: Collaborative Cross-Scale Memory for Text-to-Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33819)**  `arXiv:2611.33819`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaoxin Fan, Qingchen Yu, Kejian Wu, Jian Yang, Zeming Liu, wenjun wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-motion generation aims to synthesize realistic human motions from natural-language descriptions, enabling applications in animation, virtual avatars, and embodied agents. Despite recent progress, existing methods either retrieve motion exemplars or retain long-range context, but rarely exploit memory to jointly reason about semantics, dynamics, and generation history. In this paper, we propose MMotion, a collaborative multi-scale memory framework that unifies scale-conditioned semantic retrieval, latent dynamics modeling, and causal working memory. Specifically, semantic memory provides motion priors at different generation scales, dynamics memory captures latent state transitions, and working memory preserves causal context from previously generated motions. A collaborative fusion mechanism integrates these complementary memories to determine what motion to generate, how it should evolve, and how it should remain consistent with its history. MMotion is architecture-agnostic and can be readily integrated into both diffusion-based and next scale autoregressive models. Extensive experiments across standard benchmarks and diverse backbones demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines.
  </details>

- **[EvoVAU: Tool Evolution for Video Anomaly Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33808)**  `arXiv:2611.33808`  `cs.CV`  
  _Benfeng Wang, Chao Huang, Yudong Cheng, Chengliang Liu, Wei Wang, Wenqi Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video anomaly understanding (VAU) requires reasoning about event progression, interactions, and the contextual conditions that distinguish abnormal behavior from common activity across diverse scenes. Although video agents enable multi-round analysis, the predefined analytical operations may not adequately satisfy these diverse reasoning demands. In this paper, we present EvoVAU, a framework that evolves a reusable tool library for a frozen video solver. Guided by task outcomes and execution trajectories, an external proposer identifies potential limitations in the library and refines it, retaining task experience as analytical operations that can be reused across questions. Specifically, Cross-Version Contrastive Feedback guides tool refinement by combining current errors with paired execution trajectories from historical corrections and regressions. Adoption-Aware Selection accepts a candidate library only when overall development-set accuracy improves and corrections outnumber regressions among questions on which the solver invokes new or modified tools. Using a small development set, EvoVAU alternates tool refinement with candidate evaluation and selection. At test time, the evolved library remains fixed, with no further involvement from the proposer. Experiments on several VAU benchmarks demonstrate strong performance and the flexibility of the evolved library across anomaly domains.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Transferability and Discriminability via Positive-Unlabeled Domain Adaptation for Building Damage Mapping Across Disaster Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33790)**  `arXiv:2611.33790`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qirui Wang, Hengwei Zhao, Xiaoshu Yang, Jingtao Li, Bo Li, Sorin Popescu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mapping the damage to buildings following natural and human-caused disasters is essential for disaster response and humanitarian assistance. However, differences in hazard types, geographic environments, building characteristics, and imaging conditions cause cross-event distribution shifts that limit the generalization of deep learning models to new disaster events, even for geospatial foundation models (GFMs) pretrained on extensive Earth observation data. Unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) can mitigate these shifts, but adaptation remains challenging when damage-proportion and damage-appearance shifts coexist and interact. We identify enhancing target-domain discriminability as key to addressing this challenge. To this end, inspired by positive-unlabeled (PU) learning, we introduce positive-unlabeled domain adaptation, a paradigm that incorporates PU weak supervision to enhance target-domain discriminability, requiring only a few labeled damaged buildings in the target domain. Building upon this straightforward intuition, we develop Positive-ANchored Domain Adaptation (PANDA), comprising two complementary and mutually reinforcing components: Pairwise Alignment with Intra-class Robustness (PAIR) for discriminative representation learning and Refine Gate and Verifier (RAV) for supervision refinement. On a global-scale multi-disaster benchmark, PANDA generally outperforms representative domain adaptation and PU learning methods. Results on the 2025 Los Angeles wildfires show its effectiveness in new disaster scenarios with few labeled damaged buildings. Further experiments demonstrate the potential of integrating PANDA with GFMs for cross-event adaptation. These findings highlight PANDA's practical potential for rapid, reliable, and cross-event building damage mapping.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Immediate and Prospective Credit Assignment for Medical Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33777)**  `arXiv:2611.33777`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuqi Cheng, Rui Meng, Yijin Li, Chandra Sekhar Reddy Edula, Xiuwei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical visual question answering (VQA) requires acquiring useful evidence and deciding where to look next. Yet final-answer rewards do not reveal which observations support answering or guide subsequent inspection. We introduce MedPACT, an agentic reinforcement learning framework that learns selective observation policies through immediate answer support and prospective guidance. Our content-controlled estimator compares next actions induced by real and matched alternative observations under a shared real-observation history, isolating how observed content improves subsequent decisions. We combine both forms of credit within GRPO, assigning observation credit to the corresponding tool-call tokens alongside trajectory-level outcome supervision. MedPACT uses a single vision-language model and image-question-answer supervision, with credit estimation confined to training. Experiments with 4B and 8B backbones on six medical VQA benchmarks demonstrate improved accuracy, strong out-of-distribution generalization, and substantially lower inference costs. Case studies further illustrate how MedPACT avoids repeated observations and uses acquired evidence to guide subsequent inspection, demonstrating real-world practice potential.
  </details>

- **[VAPOR: Improving Multimodal Reasoning with Sparse Visual Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33751)**  `arXiv:2611.33751`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingyu Su, Wei Wu, Erle Zhu, Chengxing Xie, Deyao Hong, Hongning Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in visual reasoning, but extended generation over large visual contexts remains computationally expensive. During autoregressive decoding, visual attention is recomputed for every generated token, even when consecutive tokens rely on similar visual evidence. We introduce VAPOR (Visual Attention Persistence with On-demand Refresh), a training-free framework that reduces this redundancy by reusing visual attention outputs across decoding steps and refreshing them when needed. VAPOR caches the visual attention output while continuing to update textual attention at every step, helping reduce visual grounding drift during extended reasoning. To decide when to reuse cached outputs, VAPOR estimates visual attention mass using a low-rank approximation. It also uses prefill-based attention consistency and cache age to make independent Persist and Refresh decisions for each attention head. VAPOR retains the full visual key-value (KV) cache, so all visual tokens remain available when new evidence is needed. Experiments on eight image and four video reasoning benchmarks across two MLLM families show that VAPOR improves accuracy over dense attention on nearly all benchmarks while achieving 1.11-1.21x decoding speedups. Compared with the strongest state-of-the-art acceleration baseline in each setting, VAPOR further improves accuracy by 0.35-2.15 percentage points while achieving comparable or lower decoding latency. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VTK-Bench: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Distinguishing Language Priors and Text Modal Bias in Visual Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33725)**  `arXiv:2611.33725`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yao Lu, Yuhang Zheng, Yaxin Gao, Zhe Tang, Zhaowei Zhu, Jiaheng Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual language models (VLMs) have demonstrated exceptional capabilities across various multimodal tasks, but they remain susceptible to hallucinations, undermining their reliability. Existing hallucination benchmarks primarily focus on identifying hallucinations and describing their manifestations, often neglecting their underlying causes. Specifically, multimodal hallucinations can stem from two distinct failure modes: text modality bias (TMB), reflecting an over-reliance on misleading text input; and language prior (LP), reflecting a reliance on previously learned knowledge. To differentiate these failure modes, we propose VTK-Bench, a benchmark that explicitly separates three information sources: visual information (V), textual information (T), and prior knowledge (K).
