By establishing mutually exclusive answers corresponding to V, T, and K, VTK-Bench enables fine-grained attribution analysis of model responses. Furthermore, we introduce discrete attribution and soft attribution metrics to characterize information source preferences. Extensive experiments on 11 representative open-source VLMs show that LP is more prevalent than TMB in most evaluated models. Notably, TMB decreases substantially with model scaling, whereas LP exhibits relatively limited improvement and remains the dominant failure mode. These findings highlight the importance of different information source preferences for a deeper understanding of multimodal hallucinations.
  </details>

- **[MOVR-SAR: A Multi-resolution SAR Registration Dataset under Orbit-crossing and View-variant Imaging Differences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33673)**  `arXiv:2611.33673`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchi Pu, Bosong Qi, Pengcheng Shi, Jiayuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) image registration under varying acquisition geometries is challenging because orbit direction, look side, and incidence angle affect backscatter, terrain-induced distortion, and feature visibility. However, large-scale geometry-aware benchmarks remain scarce. This paper presents MOVR-SAR, a multi-resolution dataset of 450,845 registered and quality-screened tile pairs derived from 2774 scene pairs from Capella, Umbra, Sentinel-1, and NISAR. It covers X, C, and L bands, ground sampling distances of 0.25-20 m, and diverse regions and land-cover types. A unified framework defines four imaging-geometry pair categories from orbit direction and look side, plus viewing-angle-separation labels for Capella and Umbra, enabling stratified evaluation under same- and opposite-direction illumination. Systematic evaluation of representative matching methods shows that official and cross-modal pretrained weights perform poorly on high-resolution X-band imagery. Fine-tuning on MOVR-SAR improves accuracy and reduces disparities across platforms, imaging-geometry pair categories, and viewing-angle separations. For example, fine-tuning increases FlexGlue AUC@5 from 3.06% to 89.79% on Capella and from 1.79% to 89.03% on Umbra, reaching 93.41% and 94.77% on Sentinel-1 and NISAR, respectively. Fine-tuning substantially reduces the official-weight same-orbit, same-look-side advantage and reverses the three-method average category ranking on Capella, suggesting that this bias reflects training-data coverage rather than an invariant limitation of cross-geometry matching. Land-cover effects remain platform- and method-dependent. MOVR-SAR provides a training resource and geometry-aware benchmark for robust SAR-SAR registration. The dataset and model weights will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recall4D: Forgetting, Not Misalignment, Limits Long-Horizon Feed-Forward 4D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33655)**  `arXiv:2611.33655`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tianle Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward 4D trackers recover camera motion, depth, and point trajectories from short video clips, but chaining windows over long videos degrades their accuracy. We find that replacing inter-window alignment with ground-truth transforms leaves tracking error essentially unchanged. Static-point error instead grows with time since last observation: repeatedly predicting a static point from windows that no longer observe it corrupts previously recovered geometry. We call this failure forgetting. Merely retaining the last observed positions of unseen points reduces error by 26% on PointOdyssey without modifying the tracker. We introduce Recall4D, a training-free framework that augments a frozen tracker with a persistent world state and sparse motion anchors. Static points retain their world-space positions outside the camera view and are refined by averaging observations decoded at sparse keyframes while in view. For dynamic objects, the tracker decodes sparse representative anchors, while geometric skinning propagates their displacements to dense queries. Across two long-horizon benchmarks, Recall4D improves the accuracy of its backbone while running 7.6-9.0$\times$ faster, and its accuracy gains carry over to five further backbones and three other long-sequence tasks, demonstrating its generality.
  </details>

- **[CariesBench: A Hierarchical Multi-view Benchmark for Dental Caries Screening and Diagnostic Reasoning from 3D Intraoral Scans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33643)**  `arXiv:2611.33643`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuefen Liu, Yanxiong, Xinquan Yang, Fangqian Zhang, Qinyi Lu, Yuning Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dental caries assessment is a structured clinical process requiring lesion localization, identification of the affected tooth and surface, and severity grading rather than simple image-level recognition. 3D intraoral scans (IOS) preserve detailed dental geometry and surface morphology and support standardized rendering from complementary viewpoints, enabling fine-grained visual assessment while introducing a distinct need for anatomically grounded multi-view reasoning. Despite recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and dental vision-language benchmarks, existing evaluations do not jointly assess hierarchical arch-to-tooth-to-surface reasoning, cross-view evidence integration, and surface-level caries assessment. To address this gap, we introduce CariesBench, a hierarchical benchmark constructed from 5,932 3D IOS, comprising 85,455 standardized multi-view renderings and 133,994 clinically grounded visual question answering instances with expert quality control. CariesBench comprises three complementary task families: Arch-level Clinical Screening (ACS), Tooth-level Diagnostic Reasoning (TDR), and Structured Clinical Decision-Making (SCD). Using the CariesBench training set, we further fine-tune Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct to obtain IntraoralGPT. Extensive evaluation of 24 representative baseline MLLMs reveals a substantial benchmark-level performance disparity between arch-level screening and tooth-level diagnostic reasoning, with higher accuracy on ACS than on TDR. CariesBench-specific supervision substantially improves the Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct baseline, increasing the 19-task macro-average clinical accuracy from 52.7% to 69.5% and TDR Overall from 34.4% to 44.7%.
  </details>

- **[AVERT: Anomalous Value Erasure with Response Testing against Visual Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33532)**  `arXiv:2611.33532`  `cs.CV`  
  _Newt Hue-Nam K. Nguyen, Jui-Yu Ho, Wei-Han Chen, Pin-Yen Ting, Yangqiu Song, Shang-Tse Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision Language Models (VLMs) are vulnerable to visual prompt injection, in which an adversarially perturbed image forces the model to respond with the attacker's chosen content, such as malicious tool calls, website links, or advertisements, sometimes regardless of the user's question. Existing defenses, such as diffusion-based purification and image-token masking, mitigate these attacks at a substantial cost to the model's utility on benign inputs. To address this challenge, we propose AVERT, which removes adversarial feature directions only when the model's answer depends on them. Our key procedures involve identifying anomalous value subspaces and detecting answer dependence. To identify the anomalous value subspace, we compare the statistics of each input's attention-head outputs with those of clean images and extract the directions along which the input deviates the most. We then project out these directions to remove the injected content. Because the same directions also carry benign visual information, we apply the removal only to answers that depend on them, namely answers whose likelihood collapses once the directions are removed or that are as likely under unrelated questions as under the user's question. Against white-box attacks on four VLMs (Qwen2-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, LLaVA, InternVL2.5) and four injection benchmarks, AVERT lowers average attack success from 86-90% to at most 0.6% while retaining at least 96% of undefended utility averaged over thirteen benchmarks. Its defense-utility F-score ($F_{DU}$), the harmonic mean of attacks stopped and utility retained, is 98.0-99.1 and the highest among all compared defenses on every model.
  </details>

- **[Separating Environment and Sensor Shift from Acquisition Protocol in Physical Vision Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33507)**  `arXiv:2611.33507`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuyang Yuan, Jason Wu, Mohammadali Khodabandehlou, Jianpeng Yao, Hang Qiu, Mani Srivastava_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In real-world deployments, vision systems must operate under varying environmental conditions, such as illumination changes. Recent work has explored adaptive sensing strategies that select camera parameters according to the current environment and downstream task to improve task performance. Reliable physical benchmarks are therefore essential for evaluating such strategies. We study ImageNet-ES-Diverse, a physical benchmark that evaluates the adaptive exposure control strategy Lens by recapturing printed ImageNet images under varying illumination conditions. We construct four controlled acquisition pipelines that vary printed-image scale and camera zoom, and conduct a pilot study with directly captured physical objects. We find that the acquisition choices substantially change benchmark difficulty on all downstream baselines. Moreover, the average classification-accuracy advantage of Lens over auto exposure observed on the original benchmark does not persist in either our collected pipelines or real-object pilot study. Oracle exposure selection nevertheless retains substantial gains, indicating remaining potential for sensor control. These findings highlight acquisition protocol as an important factor in physical benchmark evaluation and motivate explicit protocol reporting and evaluation across multiple acquisition setups when assessing adaptive-sensing benefits.
  </details>

- **[EvOrderBench: When do sampled frames establish event order?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33492)**  `arXiv:2611.33492`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoqin Feng, Yuqi Li, Xinyuan Li, Neil Rodrigues, Xianglong Wang, Kuiye Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are often asked which of two actions in a video happened first, given only a few sampled frames. Such frames can show that both actions occurred without showing which came first, so a reliable model should state an order only when the frames establish it. Forced-choice temporal benchmarks treat every input as answerable and reward lucky guesses, while answerability benchmarks do not separate frames that miss an action from frames that show both actions but not their order. We introduce EvOrderBench, a real-video benchmark of 579 event families. Each family fixes an episode and a question and varies only the frames: of two four-frame inputs, only one establishes the order, and two six-frame inputs extend the other one with either the frames that resolve the order or frames that do not. Every input is labeled for whether its frames establish each action and their order. Across sixteen open-weight configurations and three closed-source references, the best open models order nearly as accurately as the references when the frames establish the order, but withhold correctly on at most 83% of the inputs where they do not, against at least 90% for every reference; forced-choice accuracy cannot reveal this gap. Open models withhold mainly when the frames show neither action; once the frames show one or both, most of them commit to an order. Thinking narrows the gap, even when it is switched on within a single checkpoint, mainly by separating sufficient from insufficient evidence rather than by making models more cautious overall. Accurate ordering thus does not imply knowing when the frames fall short, and benchmarks should reward withholding alongside ordering.
  </details>

- **[Streaming Tax: Can Streaming VLMs Replace Their Offline Parents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33474)**  `arXiv:2611.33474`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhi Yang, Junzhou Chen, Jindong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most vision-language models (VLMs) specialize in offline video understanding; in contrast, their post-trained versions, streaming VLMs, can perform live interactions. We investigate whether streaming VLMs can replace their offline parent models on offline tasks. Such replacement could reduce deployment costs and improve efficiency. We propose Streaming Tax to systematically evaluate streaming VLMs on offline tasks. Our exploration spans three core dimensions: understanding, trustworthiness, and generalization across 15 benchmarks. Our findings reveal a critical gap between offline capability preservation and practical replaceability. This gap emerges at two stages: streaming adaptation can alter trustworthy behavior even when offline understanding is largely preserved, while the native streaming protocol can introduce further performance losses. Our analyses show that some of these losses arise from restricted access to historical visual evidence and failures to deliver usable responses. Importantly, regarding safety, streaming models often leave both harmful and legitimate requests unanswered rather than genuinely refusing unsafe ones. We then propose Stream++, a training-free plugin to improve streaming VLMs on offline tasks.
  </details>

- **[Playfulness as Feedback for Agentic Game Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33457)**  `arXiv:2611.33457`  `cs.CV`  
  _Weijia Mao, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Zhen Fang, Danze Chen, Dongxing Mao, Mike Zheng Shou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents can generate runnable games from natural-language descriptions. However, a runnable game does not guarantee an excellent gameplay experience for players. In our view, a playful game should provide 1) reliable core functionality, 2) appealing visual design and assets, and 3) rich gameplay that supports exploration and progression. How agents can improve these aspects of playfulness remains underexplored. In our work, we propose Play2Evolve, a recursive game development framework with three stages: Initialization, Refinement, and Evolution. Using playfulness as feedback, agents playtest the game and diagnose issues in its functionality, visual presentation, and gameplay. Their feedback guides bug fixes and asset improvements. It also helps agents propose new gameplay and aesthetic goals for continued game evolution. To enrich game materials, we combine a video generation model with code to create appealing static and dynamic assets and integrate them into interactive gameplay. Finally, Play2Evolve achieves state-of-the-art performance among the evaluated methods, scoring 60.05 on GameCraft-Bench compared with 50.81 after Game Initialization and 52.46 for Play2Code. Human overall-quality ratings reach 3.67, compared with 2.38 and 2.43, respectively. Our code will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recovering What Matters, Transferring Only What Is Reliable: Toward Precise Reference-Based Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33455)**  `arXiv:2611.33455`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sihan Ji, Wenxin Chen, Yue Shi, Jiezhang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-based super-resolution (RefSR) aims to recover missing details from high-quality references. However, existing methods face two fundamental challenges. First, perceptual plausibility does not guarantee content fidelity. Existing methods often produce visually convincing details without faithfully recovering reference-specific information, such as text, codes, and identity cues. Second, faithful reference reading does not guarantee reliable information transfer. Increasing reference utilization also exposes the restoration process to misleading content, as global semantic relevance does not necessarily imply local correspondence consistency. To address these challenges, we propose PreciseRef, a reference-aware generative framework for faithful and reliable RefSR. At its core, Asymmetric Twin-Attention (ATA) allows target and reference images to participate in a unified attention space while the reference keeps its own shape. By letting their image tokens interact directly through joint attention, ATA facilitates fine-grained reference information retrieval without explicit image alignment. To ensure reliable information transfer, we introduce Dual-Scale Reference Gating (DRG), which combines Semantic Relevance Gating (SRG) to filter globally irrelevant reference context and Bidirectional Consistency Gating (BCG) to suppress unreliable local correspondences. Furthermore, we establish VeriRef, a benchmark evaluating exact reference-dependent content recovery and robustness to mismatched references. Extensive experiments show that our method achieves 85.6% character accuracy and 76.3% exact code decoding, compared with 17.4% and 0% for existing methods, while effectively suppressing erroneous reference transfer. It also achieves state-of-the-art perceptual quality on CUFED5 and WR-SR.
  </details>

- **[ArtKit: Motion-Driven Re-Segmentation and Link Re-Generation for Articulated Objects from RGB-D Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33403)**  `arXiv:2611.33403`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sylvia Yuan, Dan Wang, Vivian Yu, Ravi Ramamoorthi, Xinrui Cui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An RGB-D camera that films an object being moved, often from one viewpoint, sees one side of it and how its parts move. We present ArtKit, which takes such a video as input to create an articulated model with complete parts, a kinematic tree, and typed joints. Our central idea is generating the hidden geometry and verifying it against what the camera sees. A generative part prior proposes complete, part-decomposed geometry based on the first frame. Point tracks, grouped into rigid trajectories, split and merge the generated parts into links. We then fit revolute and prismatic joints, with a visibility-aware energy ensuring that the articulated model explains the depth observed across the video. A rule checks against the video to choose between synchronous group fits and per-frame chain fits, so that one pipeline serves a diverse range of articulated objects, such as household items and robotic hands. Finally, we regenerate each link of the chosen fit as one part and keep it wherever it agrees better with the observations. We evaluate on Articulate-100 and on HArt, a new benchmark of 60 PartNet-Mobility objects and 8 robotic hands and grippers rendered as RGB-D videos with ground-truth parts and joints. ArtKit reduces the axis and part motion errors of the strongest baseline by more than half on the objects, as well as the axis error on the hands. ArtKit also runs on real recordings and generated videos.
  </details>

- **[Synchronizing Cameras via 3D Object Relationships](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33387)**  `arXiv:2611.33387`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sujong Shin, Sang-Il Choi, Kwang Moo Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we synchronize multiple videos of the same dynamic event through object relationships. Driven by dynamic reconstruction and novel view synthesis, many recent synchronization methods rely on pixel-level evidence, such as point correspondences across views or the photometric losses of a reconstructed scene. In dynamic scenes, however, fast motion and occlusions make the moving regions that carry much of the timing information hard to match across views. We thus propose to bring back the traditional wisdom that objects are reliable cues for global reasoning about cameras. Measured in 3D, the relationships between objects, such as the distance between two people, are stable across all views up to scale; they can serve as cues for alignment. To compare these relationships across cameras, we only need the identity of each object in every view, as the cues are relational. We obtain these identities by giving a vision-language model a few frames of every camera and prompting it to identify the objects in the scene. We then segment and track each identified object through every video with an open-vocabulary segmentor. To obtain the 3D locations of objects, we use a pretrained depth estimator. Where depth or segmentation fails, we complement these 3D cues with 2D motion from point tracks. Finally, we synchronize the cameras by finding the temporal offsets at which the cues of different cameras agree best, first for each camera pair and then for all cameras jointly with robust optimization. We evaluate our method on four standard benchmarks, outperforming the state of the art.
  </details>

- **[DreamTour: Layout Steering for Training-Free World Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33378)**  `arXiv:2611.33378`  `cs.CV`  
  _Srikumar Sastry, Meher Gitika Karumuri, Chenqi Li, Sarosij Bose, Mellon Zhang, Allen Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video diffusion and world models generate plausible videos but do not reliably match the requested camera direction and travel distance. We present DreamTour, a training-free framework that generates camera tours from a single image with more accurate control over camera motion. DreamTour first uses a frozen image inpainting prior to extend the photograph beyond its original field of view while preserving the input pixels. Depth-based rendering of this extended scene along the requested camera path produces approximate target views that serve as spatial guidance. We introduce Layout Steering, which aligns early video latents with these views through decaying low-frequency corrections, leaving later denoising to refine appearance and dynamics. For longer tours, we autoregressively generate the video segments, repeating guidance construction from each segment's final frame to maintain camera control across the full trajectory. DreamTour improves directional accuracy and travel calibration on VBench while maintaining competitive video quality. On WorldScore, it strengthens camera control and scene consistency across chained movements. Together, these results demonstrate that training-free spatial guidance enables more accurate and consistent camera control in pretrained video diffusion and world models.
  </details>

- **[Searching Ultra-Long Videos with Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33368)**  `arXiv:2611.33368`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ce Zhang, Baiqi Li, Seongsu Ha, Gedas Bertasius_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding ultra-long videos requires retrieving sparse, query-relevant evidence from extensive video histories. Existing similarity-based retrieval methods are efficient, but they encode the question and the video content independently and thus often miss evidence whose relevance requires reasoning over both. Generative large language models (LLMs) can perform such reasoning, but they typically generate text over multiple rounds of search, which makes each query slow and costly. In this work, we introduce DeLoVi (Decision Models for Long Video), a framework that leverages pretrained decision models to retrieve evidence from ultra-long videos. Like an LLM, a decision model reads the question together with the candidate evidence, so it can reason over both. Unlike an LLM, it directly outputs a relevance score without generating text, which makes it fast and inexpensive. We first present DeLoVi-S, a simple method that generates captions for short video clips and uses a decision model to rank those captions by their relevance to a question. A generative LLM then answers the question using the top-ranked captions. Despite its simplicity, DeLoVi-S achieves state-of-the-art performance on three ultra-long video understanding benchmarks, EgoLifeQA, EgoMemReason, and MM-Lifelong, and generalizes effectively to long-video understanding benchmarks, VideoMME-L and LVBench. However, DeLoVi-S scores every caption for every question, so its cost grows linearly with video length. To reduce this cost, we propose an automated policy evolution framework that iteratively refines the memory construction and retrieval policies around a fixed pretrained decision model. The resulting method, DeLoVi-E, maintains comparable performance while reducing input tokens by 34x on average compared with DeLoVi-S. It also runs over 3x faster than the best prior method on EgoLifeQA while achieving higher accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CriticalScenes: Scalable Photorealistic Critical-Scenario Generation for Closed-Loop Driving Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33255)**  `arXiv:2611.33255`  `cs.CV`  
  _Alexandros Theocharous, Holger Caesar, Ziqi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety-critical interactions are rare in recorded driving data, leaving end-to-end driving planners with limited exposure to evasive behaviour during training. This lack of critical supervision can lead to unsafe behaviour and, in rare instances, collisions. To address this gap, we introduce CriticalScenes, a reconstruction-based framework that transforms ordinary recorded driving scenes into photorealistic safety-critical interactions with corresponding collision-avoidance supervision. Across 53 reconstructed source scenes, 979 of 1,060 attempted interactions yield feasible threat trajectories (92.4\%), while the inserted threat actors remain detectable in more than 95\% of non-occluded observations within 5--35\,m. Experiments with two structurally different end-to-end planners show that the generated supervision improves critical-scenario planning, while closed-loop transfer varies across planner architectures. SparseDrive achieves a 46.5\% relative reduction in collision rate on an independent photorealistic closed-loop benchmark, whereas UniAD shows only a modest closed-loop improvement despite substantial open-loop gains. The results further highlight the importance of closed-loop evaluation, as improvements in open-loop trajectory prediction do not necessarily translate into equivalent safety gains.
  </details>

- **[Confidence-Based Adaptive Retrieval Fails Where It Matters Most: A Paradox in Biomedical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33210)**  `arXiv:2611.33210`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chengying She, Siyu Deng, Haiyan Ge, Lizhuang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for biomedical question answering, but plausible incorrect answers remain a safety risk in clinical settings. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can ground responses in medical literature, while confidence-based adaptive RAG retrieves only when the model appears uncertain. We evaluate this routing strategy on three biomedical QA benchmarks of increasing difficulty and compare models across a broad range of parameter scales. The results reveal a paradox: confidence is well calibrated (AUROC 0.78--0.89) on standard medical exam questions where retrieval adds little value (+2.4\% to -1.4\%), yet becomes \emph{near-random} (AUROC 0.506) on specialist-level questions where retrieval helps most (+11.2\%). Five uncertainty signals---first-token probability, margin, entropy, consistency-based sampling, and verbalized confidence---show the same failure pattern. Five post-hoc calibration correction strategies, including density-ratio reweighting and rank-preserving transformation, do not recover a useful routing signal on hard questions (best AUROC: 0.507). Cross-model comparison shows that larger parameter scale and higher base accuracy do not guarantee reliable confidence-based routing under specialist-domain difficulty shift. These findings challenge the premise of confidence-based adaptive RAG: in high-stakes biomedical settings, defaulting to always-retrieve is safer and more accurate than confidence-based routing when calibration on the target domain is unknown.
  </details>

- **[Decision-Native Multimodal Clinical Modeling via Anatomical Grounding and Confidence Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32993)**  `arXiv:2611.32993`  `cs.CV`  
  _Juntao Jiang, Wenqi Zhong, Guangyu Guo, Dexia Chen, Yilong Hu, Yunhao Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured clinical decisions from volumetric computed tomography (CT) require anatomically grounded predictions and calibrated confidence estimates. However, existing 3D medical multimodal models often suffer from uncalibrated autoregressive decoding and shortcut reliance on irrelevant anatomical regions. We introduce Radev, a decision-native multimodal model that integrates anatomy-guided visual compression, direct candidate prediction, and counterfactual confidence supervision. To bridge global context with fine-grained slice details, a frozen 3D encoder extracts reusable organ-level features, which are dynamically selected by the query to attend to 2D slice features within predicted anatomical bounds. The resulting compact visual tokens enable the language backbone to estimate candidate probabilities in a single forward pass without autoregressive decoding. We further propose Counterfactual-Calibrated Training (CCT) to compare predictions under intact and intervened organ representations. CCT encourages confidence to decrease when removing target-organ evidence degrades decision quality, while penalizing excessive confidence drops under unrelated-organ removal. To facilitate rigorous evaluation, we construct CT-Verdict, a comprehensive benchmark spanning binary finding presence, multiple-choice questions, and ordinal severity assessment across both in-distribution and external cohorts. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Radev significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both decision accuracy and confidence calibration, while generalizing robustly under distribution shifts. Project page atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What to Change, What to Keep: Experience-Evolving Agents for Video Recasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32196)**  `arXiv:2611.32196`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shiying Zhang, Yuxia Zhu, Huaze Xue, Shengqi Dang, Yizhen Wu, Yijie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video recasting aims to transform the entities and environments of existing videos into new visual worlds while preserving their essential actions and interactions. However, achieving coherent transformations remains challenging, as coordinated edits can disrupt the original event structure. In this paper, we introduce RecastAgent, an experience-guided agentic framework for source-conditioned video recasting that operates under frozen model weights. Our framework employs an Experience-Guided Planner to choose edit prompts and input video states under qualitative visual critique, allowing the agent to build upon partial progress or backtrack to preserve source structure. Across tasks, an Experience Accumulation module distills completed trajectories into conditional, evidence-linked principles that record both an editing strategy and its scope of validity. We additionally construct a benchmark of 67 source videos with 134 paired-specification tasks across six creative categories for evaluation. Experiments demonstrate that RecastAgent outperforms single-pass and agentic rewriting baselines across Bernini and Ditto. Furthermore, accumulated experience improves performance on held-out test videos and transfers zero-shot to external benchmarks like FiVE-Bench and VE-Bench.
  </details>

- **[MediaStormBench-CameraLog: Can Multimodal Models Identify a Camera's Log Curve?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32172)**  `arXiv:2611.32172`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ming Ma, Wenhao Hu, Qichao Wang, Yuhao Wang, Junpeng Zhao, Qiyi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  MediaStormBench is a multimodal benchmark for real-world film and television production, with each subset drawn from MediaStorm's day-to-day work. We release its first subset, MediaStormBench-CameraLog (CameraLogBench), on a common foundational task in footage ingest and initial color grading: identifying the Log curve of a clip without metadata. Cameras record Log footage by compressing a wide range of scene light into code values with a vendor-defined logarithmic curve, and color can be restored correctly only once that curve is known. The curve, however, is recorded only in file metadata, easily lost in transcoding and team hand-offs. A colorist then has to try candidate curves one by one against a waveform scope, and a wrong choice shifts the tones and colors of the whole clip. Recovering the curve from the image alone is challenging, because vendor curves cluster tightly in code value and are coupled with unknown exposure. CameraLogBench is built on professionally shot footage and tests whether multimodal models can identify the curve, with a probe that removes the correct option to expose guessing from priors. Because every candidate curve is strictly defined by a published vendor formula, we derive four controls from one implementation of these formulas: graded negatives, synthetic frames of identical scenes, in-context specification digests, and a training-free formula baseline, which together decouple semantic priors from photometric measurement. Across 11 models, real-footage accuracy barely exceeds always guessing the most frequent curve; when the correct option is removed, models almost never abstain, revealing heavy reliance on scene and brand priors. Supplying the vendor specification sharply improves recognition on calibrated charts but hardly helps on natural images without a photometric reference, exposing a gap between reading a specification and measuring pixels.
  </details>

- **[EditText: Structure-Aware Generation and Selective Visual Transport for Fine-Grained Text Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32134)**  `arXiv:2611.32134`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaobin Chu, Guandong Li, Xin Lv, Zhiwei Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained text editing aims to accurately replace text in a specified region while preserving the source text's font, color, and texture, as well as visual consistency in unedited regions. We present EditText, a unified framework for fine-grained text editing that combines target character generation with visual transport from the source image. The model uses dual-condition Fill inputs: a masked image and a source text style reference, both sharing a spatial reference with the target image. The masked image hides the original text and retains unedited content as a source canvas; the style reference supplies appearance cues that guide new character generation together with the target text. We introduce an Attention Transport framework in which Source Weight describes the attention allocated to a condition and the Spatial Aggregation Kernel describes its internal spatial attention distribution. Accurate transfer of source details depends on attention strength, the retention of details in condition features, and spatially accurate aggregation. For selected non-edit queries, we therefore preserve the total attention weight assigned to the masked image while redistributing its attention toward spatially corresponding neighborhoods, enabling selective visual transport. Multi-stage training, combined with a Glyph-aware Structural Loss on local target character structure, teaches the model to generate accurate text, preserve source text appearance, and retain unedited details. We also introduce TBench, a fine-grained text editing benchmark covering Alphabetic, Logographic, and Arabic numerals, to evaluate character updates, appearance preservation, and the preservation of unedited details. On TBench, EditText achieves state-of-the-art non-edit-region SSIM (0.9769) and full-image LPIPS (0.0460).
  </details>

- **[CineAct: Interaction-State Agents for Playable, Branching Film Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30613)**  `arXiv:2611.30613`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyan Lin, Jianqi Ma, Shujie Duan, Hanxin Zhu, Zhihao Hu, Jinming Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open video generators such as MiniMax-H3 produce short clips of only several to a dozen seconds per run. To synthesize longer sequences, existing methods either rely on agentic systems following preset scripts, or roll out autoregressively from the previous clip's last frame, losing off-screen states and accumulating hallucinations across turns. Consequently, users still cannot take an explicit goal, choose how to accomplish it, and see that choice persist across subsequent clips. We study goal-grounded interactive video generation, where one goal admits multiple valid approaches, the user's chosen approach must be executed on screen, and its consequences must persist across clips. To this end, we propose CineAct, a state-first agentic framework in which language-model agents handle reasoning while frozen image and video generators serve purely as renderers. Before interaction, the Architect initializes the world as a spatial layout with cameras, an asset library, and a scene graph; at each turn, the system deduces the planned state change, generates a target end frame with an image generator and rigorously verifies it, and synthesizes intermediate motion with an FL2V model. We further introduce a multi-space benchmark covering two suites: long-horizon step execution and open-intent branching admissibility. Experiments show that explicit world-state modeling boosts multi-step rollout consistency over the pixel-only baseline (+11.4 automated, +23.1 human points) and raises paired branching success toward a shared goal by +30.8 points.
  </details>

- **[PinGround: Pinning Queries to Points for Parallel Grounding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30304)**  `arXiv:2611.30304`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Zhilin Zhao, Xiao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can localize through their text interface, grounding open-vocabulary objects, complex referring expressions, text lines and layout regions with strong semantic understanding. They write the boxes one after another as a single sequence, although the instances of a query, unlike the words of a sentence, form an unordered set with no sequential dependence. The number of decoding steps therefore grows with the number of instances, which makes dense scenes the most expensive. Decoding the instances in parallel avoids this growth, but streams that cannot see each other duplicate some instances and miss others. We propose PinGround, which writes a single point into each query as a spatial anchor and asks for the matching instance nearest to that point within a local radius, or None. Given the image, the anchored queries are independent, so they are decoded in parallel as leaves of a prefix tree that shares the image and the query, and the model is trained on the same tree. Coarse-to-fine anchors refine only the regions where instances are found, duplicate suppression keeps one box per instance, and set-level reinforcement learning optimizes the set F1. Only the prompt and the attention mask change, not the architecture. On three different VLMs and 12 benchmarks covering detection, OCR, layout and referring, PinGround is at most 5.5 F1 below sequential decoding trained on the same data, while its speedup grows with instance density and reaches 21.9$\times$ on dense scenes.
  </details>

- **[RVLM: Fewer Tokens, Better Resolution Scaling, Fine-Grained Spatial Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29146)**  `arXiv:2611.29146`  `cs.CV`  
  _Savya Khosla, Chris Dongjoo Kim, Alex Schwing, Derek Hoiem, Christopher Clark, Sangho Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) tokenize images using fixed spatial grids, so visual token count grows with pixel area rather than scene content. We propose to group neighboring patches based on their visual appearance, allowing tokens to represent coherent image regions rather than fixed spatial windows. We introduce RVLM, a VLM trained to use regions rather than patches as its visual units. Analyzing region construction for general-purpose VLMs, we find that regions must cover the full image, stay spatially connected during end-to-end training, and preserve fine structure such as small text, while semantic grouping adds little. RVLM therefore partitions images using training-free Felzenszwalb-Huttenlocher segmentation, assigns each ViT patch to a region, and pools patches within each region into a variable-length sequence of visual tokens. The patch-to-region assignment is retained to support spatially precise prediction through coarse-to-fine decoding. Across tasks spanning VQA, pointing, counting, segmentation, and depth estimation, RVLM matches or improves example-weighted task performance while using 21.5% fewer visual tokens than an identically trained patch baseline. The advantage grows with image resolution, reaching a 5.3-point gain while using 51.8% fewer visual tokens at the highest-resolution setting. The gains are largest on spatially demanding tasks, where prior token-reduction methods degrade most sharply. RVLM adds no model parameters and improves inference throughput by up to 35.9%. On unseen pathology and satellite images and on video benchmarks without any video training, RVLM performs on par with the patch baseline while using 2-3$\times$ fewer visual tokens. These results establish regions as a scalable alternative to patch-based visual representations while preserving the spatial precision required for grounded prediction.
  </details>

- **[EgoCaptioner: Towards Comprehensive and Fine-Grained Egocentric Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26380)**  `arXiv:2611.26380`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuqi Tang, Yang Shi, Yuran Wang, Tengfei Liu, Rundong Wang, Zongyang Qiu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Egocentric video captioning requires fine-grained detail and accurate timing, yet current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often miss brief actions, confuse their order, or misinterpret the wearer's motion. Existing caption formats also offer limited support for combining detailed narration with precise timing. We present EgoCaptioner, which pairs a video-level overview of the setting, participants, and the wearer's overall activity with timed steps whose narration adapts to varying perceptual demands and marks key moments at specific timestamps. We develop a coarse-to-fine annotation pipeline that establishes event structure before expanding step descriptions, yielding 50,000 training examples from eight egocentric video sources. Complementary rewards assess factual accuracy and coverage, measure step-level temporal alignment, and penalize fragmented or redundant narration, guiding caption refinement through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). To improve the reliability of factual supervision, we supplement caption references with pose-derived motion facts when sensor data are available. Across five egocentric benchmarks, EgoCaptioner achieves leading performance among evaluated open-source models in caption quality, caption-conditioned question answering, and temporal alignment. It also surpasses Gemini 3.1 Pro on PhysCapBench and all evaluated proprietary baselines on Minerva-Ego under the caption-to-QA protocol. Ablations support the complementary roles of factual and temporal rewards and show that coarse-to-fine annotation provides more effective supervision than single-pass annotation.
  </details>

- **[State-Message Interaction Adapter for Cross-Domain Few-Shot Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21085)**  `arXiv:2611.21085`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hang Xing, Yan Xia, Yongjiang Liu, Siqi Zhang, Xueming Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-domain few-shot segmentation adapts natural-image pretrained encoders to medical or industrial domains from a few pixel-annotated support masks. Within frozen DINOv3, intermediate per-head attention messages already carry target-background distinctions that baseline final-layer similarities and predictions do not consistently reflect. We propose the State-Message Interaction Adapter (SMIA) to learn how per-head messages should update each receiving token representation while keeping pretrained attention and FFN parameters frozen. Inserted right after per-head aggregation, SMIA conditions the transformation of each head's aggregated output (message) on each patch's pre-attention feature (state), allowing similar contextual messages to produce distinct or opposing updates depending on the receiving position. Because scarce support masks cover only limited state-message combinations, a shared low-rank bilinear map confines the learned interactions to low-dimensional state, message, and output subspaces. Added before each adapted block's frozen FFN, these updates yield adapted features that are refined by frozen FFNs and deeper blocks for linear readout. With only 0.51M trainable parameters and single-pass, support-free inference, SMIA achieves six-dataset mean mIoUs of 77.40% at 5 shots and 80.99% at 10 shots across 20 medical and industrial tasks, surpassing the strongest prior methods by +3.61 and +3.93 percentage points. In controlled 10-shot comparisons across three benchmarks on the same DINOv3 backbone, SMIA also outperforms parameter-matched AdaptFormer, LoRA, and ParaX with a better precision-recall balance.
  </details>

- **[HEmIF-Bench: Benchmarking H&E-to-mIF Models Beyond Virtual-Stain Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19905)**  `arXiv:2611.19905`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fuxian Zhao, Hyokeun Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hematoxylin and eosin (H&E)-to-multiplex immunofluorescence (mIF) generators predict spatial protein expression from routine histology, yet pixel-level marker reconstruction does not establish downstream utility. We present HEmIF-Bench, evaluating five frozen generators across eight datasets spanning molecular, cellular, tissue, and patient scales. HEmIF-Bench decouples internal encoder features from generated outputs, benchmarking both against the pathology foundation reference UNI2. Mirroring the clinical workflow where pathologists rely on mIF to define cell phenotypes, delineate tertiary lymphoid structures (TLSs) and germinal centers (GCs), and stratify patient risk, we evaluate external CD3 recovery, single-cell classification, dense nuclear segmentation, TLS/GC segmentation, and patient survival prediction. Across the benchmark, foundation-model-based MIPHEI-ViT leads among the generators, while non-foundation models show task-specific strengths. Comparing the two routes reveals that internal encoder features generally outperform generated outputs, and larger output marker panels do not guarantee stronger downstream utility across datasets. Moreover, neither internal encoder features nor generated outputs consistently surpass frozen UNI2. These findings show that pixel-level stain fidelity does not equate to downstream utility, and that generators trained at current paired-data scales have yet to surpass large-scale pure-H&E foundation models. With the expansion of paired translation cohorts and the advancement of foundation-based generative architectures, HEmIF-Bench establishes a definitive benchmark for tracking when H&E-to-mIF generation crosses the threshold to deliver genuine value beyond routine histology for tumor microenvironment analysis and precision oncology.
  </details>

- **[MotionCore: A Latent Neural Simulator for Video Generation with Rigid-Body Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08904)**  `arXiv:2611.08904`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zheng Wang, Zhengwei Gong, Yuanzhi Liang, Haibin Huang, Chi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models can produce highly realistic videos while generating physically inconsistent object motion. This limitation suggests that visual modeling alone may not provide a transferable representation of physical dynamics. We introduce MotionCore, a framework for learning physical dynamics via a learnable latent neural operator which focuses on rigid-body video generation. We introduce a latent neural simulator learning to predict the evolution of object-level physical states from observed motion. Its predictions are then converted into spatially aligned latent controls for a controllable video diffusion model. The generator serves as a visual interface: it provides appearance and scene detail, while the learned dynamics model specifies the physical trajectory. We construct our training dataset comprising semi-realistic synthetic multi-object videos with rich annotations. We evaluate MotionCore on public physical video generation benchmarks and conduct controlled ablations to assess the contribution of learned dynamics relative to other priors. Results indicate that MotionCore improves physical consistency and trajectory obedience in the evaluated rigid-body settings, while maintaining comparable visual quality. MotionCore outperforms larger general and physics-aware video generators while using substantially fewer parameters.
  </details>

- **[OVM-Occ: Open-Vocabulary Monocular Occupancy Prediction via Geometry-Aware Vision-Language Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35312)**  `arXiv:2610.35312`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuyang Yu, Haibo Qin, Shaohan Hu, Wei Shan, Xiaoqing Guan, You Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular occupancy prediction in indoor scenes aims to recover a scene's 3D geometry and semantics from a single RGB image. Existing methods are restricted to a closed set of predefined categories, limiting their ability to generalize to unseen categories. This limitation is particularly critical in indoor scenes, where objects exhibit highly diverse and fine-grained semantic categories. We study open-vocabulary monocular occupancy prediction for indoor scenes and present OVM-Occ, a Gaussian-based occupancy framework with an instance-aware vision-language teacher. Specifically, we adopt Language-Embedded Gaussians as the intermediate scene representation, propose a geometry-aware deformable attention module to refine Gaussian semantic features, and construct instance-level vision-language embeddings to provide reliable semantic supervision. We further introduce geometry-aware decoupled semantic distillation to mitigate the interference between geometry learning and semantic learning. To evaluate open-vocabulary occupancy prediction performance, we introduce OpenOcc-ScanNet and OpenOcc-Replica, two extended benchmarks with more diverse and fine-grained semantic categories. Experiments show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in open-vocabulary monocular occupancy prediction for indoor scenes, outperforming the previous state-of-the-art approach by 12.2 mIoU and 0.6 IoU on the Occ-ScanNet dataset, with consistent gains under new open-vocabulary evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[HumanPhys: A Human-Centric Physical-World Perception Benchmark for Real-World Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14679)**  `arXiv:2610.14679`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiming Zhang, Enzhi Zhou, Junan Lin, Dulan Zhou, Weiqiang Wang, Yuge Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce HumanPhys, a human-centric benchmark for real-world physical perception, and a training corpus emitted by the same pipeline. Each question targets a physically grounded quantity related to people, including depth, inter-person distance, gaze angle, displacement, velocity, heading-step trajectory, blink count, and contact-deformation strength. The rendered probes additionally test illumination inference from body and face cues and camera ego-motion, both with and without a person in the frame. All labels are derived programmatically from sensor calibration, source metadata, or renderer state, rather than written by annotators or produced by estimators on the benchmark frames. HumanPhys systematically probes six dimensions of human-centric physical perception: spatial state, kinematics, blink events, deformation, illumination, and camera pose. To the best of our knowledge, no existing MLLM benchmark-and-training corpus jointly supervises this set of human-centered physical quantities under direct metadata- or renderer-derived labels. The six dimensions appear only partially dissociable across the tested MLLMs. We benchmark a range of open- and closed-source MLLMs on HumanPhys and find that all six capability dimensions remain challenging for current models. We hope HumanPhys will support future research on multimodal systems that perceive and reason about people in the physical world.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Visual Token Compression for Video Large Language Models: A Simple Yet Strong Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35394)**  `arXiv:2609.35394`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiao Zhang, Wang Zeng, Sheng Jin, Yunzhe Liu, Wentao Liu, Chen Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video large language models (Video LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress in video understanding, but their inference efficiency is constrained by the large number of visual tokens produced by long videos. Recent video token compression methods increasingly rely on complex, hand-crafted heuristics for token pruning or merging. This raises a fundamental question: how much of compression performance can be obtained by simply preserving the structure encoded in the visual representations? We investigate this question with SimpleComp, a simple and training-free baseline that performs position-aware cross-frame clustering in the visual feature space and represents each cluster using the mean of its original visual features. Extensive experiments across five video understanding benchmarks and three representative Video LLMs show that SimpleComp outperforms recent compression methods at most visual token retention ratios, with particularly strong robustness under extremely low retention rates. To understand this behavior, we analyze the feature space preserved by different compression methods in terms of local approximation fidelity and global coverage. The results show that stronger downstream performance is consistently associated with better preservation of the original visual feature distribution, especially its global coverage. These findings highlight feature-space preservation as an important consideration for video token compression under highly constrained token budgets. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where Compute Matters: Heterogeneous Attention for Efficient Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31050)**  `arXiv:2609.31050`  `cs.CV`  
  _Olga Zatsarynna, Denis Korzhenkov, Juergen Gall, Amir Habibian, Mohsen Ghafoorian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient video generation requires reducing the quadratic cost of self-attention over long spatiotemporal token sequences. Denoising difficulty varies across video regions and evolves throughout generation, motivating an adaptive allocation of global context. We introduce HetA-DiT, a heterogeneous attention mechanism that routes uncertain tokens through global attention and processes more reliable tokens with local attention. A lightweight uncertainty branch is trained with an uncertainty-aware distribution-matching objective. At inference, its preceding-step predictions are shared across attention heads and reused across blocks, without an additional Transformer evaluation. We evaluate DMD-distilled Wan2.2-5B, Wan2.1-1.3B and Wan2.1-14B models. VBench across all three models, together with VBench2.0 and human preference evaluations on 5B, shows that HetA achieves a favorable quality-efficiency trade-off while routing only approximately 20\% of tokens through global attention. Across Wan2.1-1.3B and Wan2.2-5B/14B benchmarks on A100, HetA achieves up to 3.78x self-attention and 2.23x Transformer-block speedups over dense FlashAttention. Complete-denoising speedups grow with model size and sampling schedule, with composed runtime measurements projecting up to 2.06x for a 14B expert with 50-step sampling.
  </details>

- **[STEREOFLOW: Progressive Stereo Matching with StereoDiT and Transition Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19986)**  `arXiv:2607.19986`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Wang, Haoran Geng, Xiaotong Yang, Jing Tang, Songlin Wei, Linlong Lang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stereo matching is a fundamental task in 3D reconstruction. Despite remarkable advances, the prevailing paradigms formulate stereo matching as a deterministic regression problem, collapsing the multimodal distribution modeling into a single-point estimation. This formulation suffers from a regression-to-mean bias, frequently struggling with ambiguous regions. In contrast, we introduce a prior-guided generative framework that integrates deterministic matching regression and generative distribution modeling within a complementary formulation. Built upon this formulation, we introduce StereoFlow through three key components: (i) a two-stage progressive cascade matching network that progressively produces multi-resolution stereo conditions with complementary matching cues; (ii) a pixel diffusion transformer (termed StereoDiT) with a frequency-decoupled architecture for modeling correspondence ambiguity; (iii) a few-step flow matching objective (termed Transition Flow Matching) for efficient optimization. In summary, \textsc{\textbf{StereoFlow}} achieves strong geometric consistency and rich fine-grained details in ill-posed, discontinuous regions and under zero-shot generalization. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed StereoFlow establishes multiple state-of-the-art results across benchmarks, including Scene Flow, KITTI, ETH3D, and Middlebury.
  </details>

- **[AE-UAV: An Air-to-Air Event Camera Dataset for UAV Detection and Tracking with Continuous-Time Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14726)**  `arXiv:2607.14726`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zixin Jiang, Bing He, Chaoran Xiong, Zhenzhen Wang, Xin Zhao, Ling Pei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Perceiving unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) from another UAV is essential for aerial avoidance, interception, and counter-UAV operations. Event cameras suit this task because of their microsecond temporal resolution and high dynamic range. However, existing event-based UAV datasets were mostly recorded by static or ground-based observers, and their targets are annotated only at fixed frame rates. We present AE-UAV, an air-to-air event camera dataset collected from a flying UAV for detection and tracking. It contains 178 sequences of event data at 1280x720 resolution covering daytime and nighttime flights under three motion geometries. A continuous-time annotation framework based on cubic B-spline interpolation provides ground-truth boxes at arbitrary timestamps and a label for every event. Hold-out experiments confirm a median interpolation error of 2.19 pixels, reducing the piecewise-constant baseline error by 74\%. We benchmark six detectors and six trackers across illumination and motion conditions. The results show that nighttime and head-on scenarios are the hardest cases for detectors. A cross-rate generalization study further shows that learned trackers lose their accuracy when the test aggregation rate differs from the training rate. The dataset will be publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Anatomically Plausible Human Image Generation via Synthetic Localized Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25759)**  `arXiv:2605.25759`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bao Li, Yuliang Xiu, Zhen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale text-to-image foundation models have achieved remarkable visual realism, yet generating human images with correct anatomical structures remains challenging. Existing approaches enforce anatomical constraints through part-specific modules or localized loss weighting during supervised fine-tuning on high-quality human photos, but such datasets are limited and often provide ambiguous optimization signals due to confounding factors such as lighting, pose, and background. Preference-based alignment offers an alternative, but standard Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) treats all pixels equally and therefore fails to exploit the localized nature of anatomical artifacts. To address this, we propose the framework of Alignment via Synthetic Anatomical Preference (ASAP), which constructs controlled preference pairs through a localized degradation mechanism applied to high-fidelity human images. This mechanism performs a controlled experiment on images by introducing explicit anatomical errors in targeted regions while preserving the remaining content. With this mechanism, we create the Human Anatomical Preference (HAP) dataset with over 10K curated pairs for effective anatomical alignment of text-to-image human image generative models. To better leverage the locality of these controlled preference pairs, we introduce a localized and margin-bounded variant of DPO that prioritizes optimization in targeted anatomical regions while enforcing a finite preference margin to prevent over-optimization and preserve global semantics. We further introduce HAF-Bench, a benchmark for systematic evaluation of anatomical fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ASAP consistently reduces anatomical errors across multiple foundation models while maintaining overall image quality.
  </details>

- **[ArtMesh: Part-Aware Articulated Mesh Fields with Motion-Consistent Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16582)**  `arXiv:2605.16582`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sylvia Yuan, Dan Wang, Ravi Ramamoorthi, Xinrui Cui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present ArtMesh, a mesh-native method for reconstructing articulated objects explicitly as connected triangle meshes with per-part rigid motion from multi-view observations in start and end states. Existing 3D Gaussian Splatting pipelines for articulated reconstruction inherit the unstructured point-based geometry of their splatting base, which provides no surface topology for reasoning about part boundaries or enforcing motion consistency along the object's connectivity. ArtMesh instead builds on a mesh-based differentiable rendering backbone, enabling part-aware dynamics to act directly on the structured topology. To make the topology compatible with articulation, we introduce part-aware restricted Delaunay remeshing, producing connected submeshes whose triangles do not cross semantic part boundaries. The dynamic mesh field then optimizes articulation using bidirectional Vertex-wise Motion Consistency on transported mesh vertices and Pixel-wise Motion Consistency on rendered images. We introduce Articulate-100, a new benchmark of 100 articulated objects spanning 16 PartNet-Mobility categories. On this benchmark, given ground-truth part masks, ArtMesh achieves the best aggregate joint-axis estimation and part-level geometric reconstruction among prior 3DGS-based pipelines, with the largest gains on objects with many movable parts.
  </details>

- **[PoseBridge: Bridging the Skeletonization Gap for Zero-Shot Skeleton-Based Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11497)**  `arXiv:2605.11497`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sanghyeon Lee, Jinwoo Kim, Jong Taek Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-shot skeleton-based action recognition (ZSSAR) is typically treated as a skeleton-text alignment problem: encode joint-coordinate sequences, align them with language, and classify unseen actions. We argue that this alignment is often too late. Skeletons are not complete action observations, but compressed outputs of human pose estimation (HPE); by the time alignment begins, human-object interactions and pose-relative visual cues may no longer be explicit. We call this upstream semantic loss. To address it, we propose PoseBridge, an HPE-aware ZSSAR framework that bridges intermediate HPE representations to skeleton-text alignment. Rather than adding an RGB action branch or object detector, PoseBridge extracts pose-anchored semantic cues from the same HPE process that produces skeletons, then transfers them through skeleton-conditioned bridging and semantic prototype adaptation. Across NTU-RGB+D 60/120, PKU-MMD, and Kinetics-200/400, PoseBridge improves ZSSAR performance under the evaluated protocols. On the Kinetics-200/400 PURLS benchmark, which contains in-the-wild videos with diverse scenes and action contexts, PoseBridge achieves substantial gains over prior ZSSAR methods, outperforming the strongest compared baseline by 13.3-17.4 points across all eight splits. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Anatomy: Interpreting Graph-Based ASD Classification Across ABIDE Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02518)**  `arXiv:2603.02518`  `cs.CV`  
  _Syeda Hareem Madani, Noureen Bibi, Adam Rafiq Jeraj, Sumra Khan, Anas Zafar, Rizwan Qureshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph neural networks can classify resting-state functional MRI connectomes, but a high score on one configuration leaves open which representation choices matter and whether the result supports broader clinical use. We examine this problem through two ABIDE-I analyses. On a balanced 400-subject subset, an AAL-based graph convolutional network (73.3%), an MSDL-based configuration (84.0%), and a five-model graph attention ensemble (95.0%) report high nominal performance. However, we show that this 95.0% result, which substantially exceeds typical multi-site ABIDE benchmarks (65-70%), stems from a single site-stratified test split of 60 subjects and does not reflect out-of-distribution generalization to unseen acquisition centers. In a separate 1,035-subject analysis with different preprocessing and parcellation, BrainGAT reaches 58.26% mean accuracy, below an SVM at 69.37%. We interpret these as comparisons of complete pipelines and retain both the favorable ensemble result and the negative broader-cohort result. Analysis of the existing summaries shows that atlas substitution changes the number of possible region pairs approximately ninefold, that accuracy on 60 test subjects changes by 1.67 percentage points per prediction, and that the best accuracy and AUC belong to different broader-cohort classifiers. Regional attribution on the baseline GCN (not the 95% GAT ensemble) highlights default-mode-network features but reflects classifier reliance rather than validated clinical biomarkers. Unresolved metric aggregation and diagnosis-aware harmonization limit the findings to exploratory classification evidence.
  </details>

- **[Saliency-Aware Multi-Route Thinking: Grounding and Reasoning on Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16702)**  `arXiv:2602.16702`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingjia Shi, Yinhan He, Yaochen Zhu, Cassie Dong, Jundong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language (VL) agents increasingly combine extended reasoning with ex ternal visual tools. Yet long traces can drift from the image as generated text accumulates, while directly inserting tool outputs can turn one incorrect detection into a persistent premise. We ask how inference-time computation can improve reasoning without surrendering control to the tool. We propose Saliency-Aware Principle Selection (SAP), a training-free method that evolves compact reason ing principles rather than lengthening a single token trajectory. Each principle generates independent routes from the original image and prompt. A frozen ground ing tool scores whether objects cited along these routes are visually supported; agreement, diversity, and uncertainty further control drift, collapse, and overcon fidence. The tool supplies selection evidence but never writes the reasoning or answer. Across object-hallucination benchmarks, SAP consistently improves vi sually grounded prediction under a unified model and decoding budget. Broader evaluations spanning reasoning, knowledge, OCR, documents, diagrams, and real world imagery show that this gain does not come at the expense of general VL capability. Cross-architecture and model-scale experiments further demonstrate that the principle-selection mechanism transfers beyond a single backbone, while controlled ablations confirm that both multi-route exploration and evolutionary se lection are necessary. Because routes run in parallel, SAP also reduces serial latency relative to an equally long chain. Together, these results show that scaling over governed reasoning behaviors can preserve visual evidence more effectively than simply extending the chain.
  </details>

- **[Assemble the Puzzle: Cross-Agent Utility-Aware Feature Scheduling for Bandwidth-Limited Cooperative Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17843)**  `arXiv:2511.17843`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenyi Wang, Muhammad Waqar, Wujie Wen, Ming F. Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative perception (CP) lets connected vehicles and roadside units detect objects beyond the inherent range limitation and occlusion of single-agent systems, yet its practicality is constrained by limited Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) communication bandwidth. Existing methods improve bandwidth efficiency by letting collaborating agents send only selected feature cells driven by local detection confidence, serving a centralized ego agent's request. They overlook realistic communication scheduling and neglect cross-agent redundancy. We present JigsawComm, a decentralized utility-aware feature scheduling framework that learns to ``assemble the puzzle'' of multi-agent features. Choosing who sends each region requires a utility that measures what each agent's features contribute to the collaboration. JigsawComm incorporates a lightweight feature utility estimator, learned without utility labels from the cooperative model's own leave-one-agent-out counterfactuals, which aligns utilities onto a single comparable scale across agents. From one broadcast of compact utility maps, every agent ranks the observers of each cell and sends the cells it observes best to maximize coverage and, budget permitting, second best for accuracy refinement. Each agent also broadcasts its onboard detector's lightweight bounding box list, enabling an intermediate-late hybrid fusion with our feature-anchored box consensus, which synergistically enhances the final perception results with all available observations. With every transmitted byte counted, JigsawComm at 0.45 KB per vehicle outperforms all baselines on the real-world V2X-Real benchmark, and under measured C-V2X traces on CooperScene it retains 74.5 of its 79.8 AP@0.5, at least 15.5 points above every prior art baseline.
  </details>

- **[LongScape: Advancing Long-Horizon Embodied World Models with Context-Aware MoE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21790)**  `arXiv:2509.21790`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lei Jin, Yu Shang, Yiding Ma, Xin Zhang, Chen Gao, Wei Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video-based world models hold significant potential for generating high-quality embodied manipulation data. However, current video generation methods struggle to achieve stable long-horizon generation: classical diffusion-based approaches often suffer from temporal inconsistency and visual drift over multiple rollouts, while autoregressive methods tend to compromise on visual detail. To solve this, we introduce LongScape, a hybrid framework that adaptively combines intra-chunk diffusion denoising with inter-chunk autoregressive causal generation. Our core innovation is an action-guided, variable-length chunking mechanism that partitions video based on the semantic context of robotic actions. This aligns chunk boundaries with robotic interaction events and motion dynamics, enabling the model to flexibly generate diverse dynamics. We further introduce a Context-aware Mixture-of-Experts (CMoE) framework that adaptively activates specialized experts for each chunk during generation to produce high-quality videos with coherent chunk transitions. Extensive experiments on two embodied benchmarks demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art generation performance. Notably, LongScape maintains high generation quality and temporal consistency over 1,000-frame rollouts. When used as synthetic training data, LongScape videos lift the success rate of a downstream VLA policy by 9.8 percentage points over real-only training, outperforming augmentation with other long-video generators. Our code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GazeVLM: Gaze-Guided Foveated Inference for Efficient Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16476)**  `arXiv:2509.16476`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lei Wang, Jiawen Qi, Qinyu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) achieve remarkable multimodal performance but introduce substantial computational cost due to the large number of visual tokens. Existing approaches typically rely on token pruning, sparse attention, or additional control modules, often introducing accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. This paper presents GazeVLM, a training-free framework that leverages human gaze as a natural guidance signal for efficient inference. GazeVLM allocates computation to gaze-relevant regions through a gaze-guided region-of-interest (ROI) representation and an optional two-scale design that preserves global context. We also construct GazeVLM-VQA, to the best of our knowledge, the first real-world wearable-gaze visual question answering (VQA) dataset specifically constructed for VLM inference, containing reference images, human gaze traces, and gaze-aligned question-answer pairs. Experiments on the GazeVLM-VQA benchmark demonstrate that GazeVLM improves both answer quality and efficiency across Qwen2.5-VL-3B/7B and Qwen3-VL-8B models. It reduces visual tokens by up to 84.2%, prefill FLOPs by up to 92.8%, and achieves up to 11.6 times prefill speedup, while improving answer-match accuracy by up to 8.6 percentage points (pp). These results show that aligning VLM computation with human visual attention provides a promising path toward efficient VLM inference. We publicly release the GazeVLM-VQA dataset (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Delta-SVD: Efficient Compression for Personalized Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16863)**  `arXiv:2508.16863`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tangyuan Zhang, Shangyu Chen, Qixiang Chen, Junyu Yan, Jingwen Ye, Jianfei Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalizing a text-to-image diffusion model by full fine-tuning gives the strongest subject fidelity but produces a full copy of the model per subject: a thousand personalized Stable Diffusion checkpoints approach five terabytes, so storage, not compute, is what fails to scale. We present Delta-SVD, which compresses the weight change that personalization makes rather than the model. Given only a finished checkpoint and its base, it takes a truncated SVD of each layer's weight delta and keeps the few components that carry its spectral energy, selected by a per-layer energy threshold or by a global byte budget filled by water-filling, optimal for the continuous relaxation and empirically near-optimal for discrete rank allocation. Nothing is trained and no subject image is read, so the method applies to legacy and third-party checkpoints for which training-time adapters such as LoRA were never produced. The factors merge back into dense weights at load time, so serving runs at the uncompressed model's exact latency, whereas whole-model 8-bit and 4-bit quantization slow inference by 1.5-3.4 times. On the 30-subject DreamBooth benchmark, Delta-SVD matches the uncompressed model to within 0.01 DINO at 7-fold compression and keeps 98% of its fidelity at 33-fold compression. Among post-hoc baselines, only dense-delta quantization at 411 MiB matches it; below that size Delta-SVD dominates pruning, random-rank selection, and both whole-model and dense-delta quantization on the storage-quality frontier. The same holds on 84 diverse CustomConcept101 subjects, where a 9 MiB adapter reproduces the uncompressed outputs at paired DINO 0.77, and on a fully fine-tuned SD3.5-medium MMDiT, where a 531 MB adapter matches the uncompressed model to within 0.01 DINO.
  </details>

- **[Towards High-Resolution Industrial Image Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.12931)**  `arXiv:2508.12931`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ximiao Zhang, Min Xu, Xiuzhuang Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current anomaly detection methods primarily focus on low-resolution scenarios. For high-resolution images, conventional downsampling often misses subtle anomalies due to the loss of fine-grained discriminative information. Although recent studies have explored lightweight networks or image tiling with ensemble strategies, they still fall short of industrial requirements for both accuracy and efficiency. To address the above issues, we propose HiAD, a general framework for high-resolution anomaly detection that identifies anomalies of varying sizes under constrained computational resources. Specifically, HiAD employs a dual-branch architecture that integrates anomaly cues across different scales to comprehensively capture both subtle and large-scale anomalies. Furthermore, it incorporates a multi-resolution feature fusion module that injects multi-scale semantic context into patch-level representations, thereby suppressing ultrafine texture variations while retaining anomaly-relevant structural details. To improve adaptability and efficiency, HiAD constructs a detector pool with an adaptive strategy selection mechanism that automatically identifies the optimal detector assignment strategy for varying input data, enabling robust anomaly detection while effectively controlling computational costs. HiAD requires neither additional supervision nor auxiliary learnable parameters and can be seamlessly integrated with a wide range of anomaly detection methods. Extensive experiments are conducted on multiple high-resolution anomaly detection benchmarks, including the specifically constructed MVTec-HD, VisA-HD, and RealIAD-HD benchmarks, as well as the existing MVTec 2 benchmark. The results consistently demonstrate the effectiveness and superior performance of HiAD across diverse experimental settings. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AliceAI-Foundation-80B-A3B-Base](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34529)**  `arXiv:2611.34529`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Nikolay Skachkov, Ekaterina Redina, Maksim Konoplev, Evgenii Uskov, Veronika Zykova, Ivan Tsarikov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present AliceAI-Foundation-80B-A3B-Base, a mixture-of-experts language model with 80 billion total parameters and approximately 3 billion active parameters per token, trained from scratch at Yandex and released under the Apache 2.0 license. The model combines Kimi Delta Attention with full attention, uses Attention Residuals, and is trained with Muon. Training comprises general pretraining on 17.5 trillion tokens, two context-extension stages, and a dedicated stage for reasoning and agentic interaction. We describe the data collection and augmentation pipelines, evaluation protocols, architectural ablations, and scaling-law experiments. We identify the limitations of held-out training loss as a proxy for downstream performance and introduce an auxiliary loss on mixture-of-experts outputs to stabilize training at the predicted learning rate.
