Domain-specific data selection and augmentation improve factual knowledge, mathematics, and programming skills, while agent trajectories introduced during pretraining improve performance on agentic benchmarks after supervised fine-tuning. In zero-shot reasoning evaluation, the model achieves leading results among open base models of comparable size, with 88.7% pass@8 on IMO-AnswerBench and 82.9% pass@8 on LiveCodeBench v5-v6. To evaluate Russian-language factual knowledge, we introduce and release two benchmarks with their evaluation protocols: WikiWebFacts, which tests short factual questions drawn from encyclopedic sources and search queries, and HardMultiQA, which tests more demanding factual questions inspired by user interactions. Our model scores 86.5% on WikiWebFacts and 67.9% on HardMultiQA, compared with 83.2% and 65.4% for DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base (284B total and 13B active parameters) and 72.8% and 54.5% for Nemotron-3-Super-120B-A12B-Base (120B total and 12B active parameters), outperforming both larger models on these benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[ChemDeduce: Benchmarking LLMs for Unknown Chemical Identification through Interactive Simulated Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33848)**  `arXiv:2611.33848`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hengyi Cai, Yuqi Tang, Zichang Li, Huajun chen, Qiang Zhang, Keyan Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance in chemical knowledge and reasoning, yet it remains unclear whether they can solve problems whose answers must be discovered through experimentation. We investigate this question through Unknown Chemical Identification, where a model must actively design experiments, interpret observations, update hypotheses, and ultimately identify a hidden substance. We introduce ChemDeduce, an interactive evaluation framework for assessing LLMs through simulated experiments. ChemDeduce formulates chemical identification as a sequential decision process and provides a stateful simulated laboratory with explicit instrument and operational constraints, enabling scalable and reproducible evaluation of iterative experimental reasoning. We benchmark eight frontier LLMs on 200 chemicals, yielding 1600 model--target pairs and 4800 episodes over three independent repetitions, and evaluate identification accuracy, operational compliance, interaction efficiency, and trajectory-level reasoning quality. Despite generating seemingly plausible experimental plans, current models achieve an average identification accuracy ranging from 23.83% to 67.33%, revealing substantial limitations in experiment selection, evidence interpretation, and sequential hypothesis refinement.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Recurrence: Internalizing Depth-Induced Preferences in Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33468)**  `arXiv:2611.33468`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hui Chen, Joshua Elsdon, Can Goksen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A looped language model repeatedly applies the same block to its hidden state before predicting each token. The number of passes is its recurrent depth. Increasing this depth gives the model more computation per token with the same parameters. Can the shared block learn from the predictions it makes at greater depth? Following prior work, we retrofit pretrained OLMo-2-1B with a shared recurrent block and continue mid-training over varying recurrent depths. In the four-pass model, additional passes mainly redistribute probability among tokens that already rank highly after one pass. We use the continuations generated at greater depth to train only the shared block through a single pass, without reference answers or correctness filtering. We call this transfer preference write-back. Write-back further improves one-pass and multi-pass accuracy on most tasks. At their target depths, the updated models outperform a non-recurrent control matched for mid-training data and token budget on all six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, with a mean relative gain in accuracy of up to 46.2% across tasks. At four-pass inference, one-pass training outperforms full-depth training on the same targets in the model with input injection. This advantage disappears in the matched model without injection. On GSM8K, using the updated block only on passes two through four recovers nearly the entire four-pass gain, showing that an update learned in one pass can improve later applications of the same block.
  </details>

- **[Attention Routing Stabilizes Early: Working-Set Inference for Recurrent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27373)**  `arXiv:2609.27373`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ke Wan, Chen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recurrent-depth language models, such as looped Transformers, repeatedly apply shared network blocks to refine latent representations, enabling additional test-time computation without generating explicit intermediate reasoning tokens. However, recurrent inference repeatedly accesses long contexts: full attention incurs quadratic computation during prefill, while autoregressive decoding repeatedly reads large key-value (KV) caches, introducing substantial memory traffic and capacity pressure. We study how attention routing evolves across recurrent depth and uncover a consistent separation in convergence timescales: routing-related quantities, including the attention support and the attention distribution, stabilize substantially earlier than representation-related quantities such as hidden states and attention outputs. This suggests a two-stage structure in which the model discovers a sparse working set and then refines representations over largely the same routing support. Motivated by this structure, we introduce WISE (Working-set Inference with Support Exploitation), a training-free method that uses unrestricted global attention during early recurrent steps to discover a block-structured working set, and then reuses it as the routing support in later steps while keeping recurrent refinement and within-support attention computation dynamic. By combining sparse recurrent attention with compact KV-cache management, WISE reduces both redundant attention computation and memory movement during inference. Across reasoning and long-context benchmarks, WISE closely approaches full-attention accuracy on key evaluated workloads while outperforming early-static support baselines. At 64K context, our implementation achieves up to a 6.46x decode throughput improvement under GPU memory pressure. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Lingual Gaps in Visual STEM Reasoning: Auditing VLMs Across Six Indian Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26742)**  `arXiv:2603.26742`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Swastik R_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are benchmarked on diagram-grounded science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) reasoning mostly in English, so it is unclear how much their accuracy changes when the same problem is posed in another language. We audit this for six Indian languages: 980 questions from MathVista, ScienceQA, and MMMU are machine-translated into Hindi, Bengali, Marathi, Tamil, Telugu, and Kannada with IndicTrans2 while the images stay unchanged. We evaluate seven open-weight VLMs (Qwen2.5-VL-7B/32B, Qwen3-VL-30B, InternVL2.5-8B, Aya-Vision-8B, Gemma 3-27B, Llama-4-Maverick) and GPT-4o, plus no-image and chain-of-thought (CoT) ablations on Qwen2.5-VL-7B (68,600 inference records). Accuracy falls 9.8-24.9 percentage points (pp) from English to the Indian-language mean, and every model loses more on the Dravidian than on the Indo-Aryan languages (by 0.1-13.2 pp). For Qwen2.5-VL-7B with our prompt, CoT lowers Bengali (-14.4 pp) and Kannada (-11.4 pp) accuracy while barely changing English, but these losses coincide with more answer-extraction failures. Neither Aya-Vision-8B, trained for 23 languages of which only Hindi is among ours, nor scaling Qwen2.5-VL from 7B to 32B closes the gap, and response-level diagnostics show that fluent target-language output and correct answers can come apart. We quantify how machine translation, answer extraction, and English text in the images bound these measurements. Data and code are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[CONCOR: Benchmarking Contrastive Decoding for Visual Hallucination Mitigation Beyond Final-Output Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30460)**  `arXiv:2611.30460`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Ting Chen, Geng Li, Guohao Chen, Hanjia Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contrastive decoding (CD) mitigates object hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by contrasting token distributions under different conditions to adjust token scores. However, existing generative and discriminative benchmarks primarily measure final task scores, which can be confounded by candidate filtering, changes in generation trajectories, and response biases, making it difficult to isolate the effects of the underlying contrastive adjustments. To address these limitations while complementing existing evaluations, we introduce CONCOR (Contrastive Correction Evaluation), a controlled benchmark that directly evaluates the competition between ground-truth and different types of hallucinated candidates using the Contrastive Grounding Margin (CGM). CGM also enables analysis of directional consistency, composition, and reliability of contrastive corrections. Our evaluation reveals that existing CD methods exhibit distinct correction behaviors: some consistently favor ground-truth candidates, whereas others produce strong corrections but also introduce more errors. Crucially, these differences help explain their contrasting responses to increased contrast strength, which improves some methods while degrading others. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating the contrastive correction itself, rather than relying solely on final-output performance, and CONCOR provides a controlled and interpretable framework for understanding and improving CD methods for hallucination mitigation.
  </details>

- **[Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces for Hard Math Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34928)**  `arXiv:2611.34928`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lukas Aichberger, Adam Golinski, Arno Blaas, Arwen Bradley, Jacob Springer, Mehrdad Farajtabar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training large language models (LLMs) for mathematical reasoning often relies on accepted traces whose final answers pass a verifier. For hard math problems, however, such traces can be rare under independent Monte Carlo (MC) sampling. To address this, we propose Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces (DART), a trace-mining method that starts from a rejected trace, decomposes it into reasoning steps, and ranks them by position-weighted uncertainty. It then generates deliberately distinct alternative candidates for highly ranked steps using reference-answer guidance and a meta-thought before completing the resulting traces. On greedy-unsolved DeepMath-103K problems, DART recovers 37% more problems than MC sampling at a matched number of generated traces. Its estimated generation cost per recovered problem is nearly identical, while its cost per accepted trace is 30% lower. Post-training Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on traces mined by DART achieves the highest point estimate in five of six evaluations across MATH-500 and AIME benchmarks. These results demonstrate that revisiting rejected traces with DART can expand problem coverage and provide effective post-training data.
  </details>

- **[PersonaFactory: Stylistic User Prompt Augmentation with Synthetic Persona Bank](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34785)**  `arXiv:2611.34785`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bich Ngoc Doan, Hanna Yukhymenko, Imanol Schlag_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly replace real people in the AI development pipeline, as they are used to generate training dialogues at scale and to drive alignment and evaluation. However, out-of-the-box models produce monolingual and stylistically flat texts as synthetic user prompts, failing to replicate the real user-AI communication mechanics. We present PersonaFactory, an end-to-end pipeline that generates detailed user personas and uses them to augment existing user prompts from their point of view, producing a bank of over 740000 structured synthetic personas detailing their background and communication behaviors, and a corpus of roughly 1.24 million persona-styled synthetic conversations spanning 85 languages. We evaluate the effect of personal features on style adoption and intent preservation, using an LLM-judge and heuristic metrics to assess augmentation fidelity together with the downstream impact on assistant persona control through training data. Results show that our structured, attribute-based style representation enables per-axis control and better intent preservation at comparable style fidelity than free-text persona descriptions used in existing methods. A human expert evaluation found the augmented conversations plausible, with favorable ratings for intent preservation, style naturalness, and multilingual authenticity. Finally, PersonaFactory conversations achieve comparable performance to original training data on capability benchmarks, are preferred on real user prompts at equal reply length, and adapt their reply style more closely to users without explicit steering.
  </details>

- **[Collect for Coverage, Replay with Evidence: Adaptive and Efficient On-Policy Distillation for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34756)**  `arXiv:2611.34756`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haowen Hou, Gen Li, Ruilin Li, Yicheng Liu, Zhen Huang, Shuai Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fully online on-policy distillation (OPD) is costly for long-horizon agents because each update depends on fresh environment rollouts. Prefix replay offers an efficient alternative by sampling current-student responses from stored histories, reducing idle time for long-tail rollouts and allowing each collected trajectory to support multiple updates. However, our analysis of the complete prefix replay pipeline identifies two limitations in long-horizon settings. In trajectory collection, teacher-only trajectories miss student-induced histories, while broader history coverage can reduce teacher reliability. In trajectory reuse, fixed position-based schedules cannot identify critical decisions that may occur at any trajectory depth. We introduce CARE-OPD (Collect for coverAge, Replay with Evidence) to address both limitations. Student-seeded teacher completions and response-level confidence gating expand history coverage while filtering unreliable updates. Hindsight replay allocation then uses downstream execution evidence to select consequential turns. Across long-horizon tasks Software Engineering and Deep Research, CARE-OPD outperforms online OPD and prior prefix replay baselines. On SWE-bench Verified, it raises task success to 59.5% while reducing end-to-end training time by 45% and environment actions by 44% relative to asynchronous online OPD.
  </details>

- **[Puzzling-Bench: Evaluating Language Models on 23,777 Human-Written Puzzles with Community-Verified Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34331)**  `arXiv:2611.34331`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Abhinav Sharma, Li Li, Chien Van Nguyen, Sai Karthik Navuluru, Bo Ni, Xiangbo Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Puzzling-Bench, a benchmark of 23,777 puzzles with community-verified answers drawn from the Puzzling Stack Exchange archive (2014-2026): riddles, ciphers, logic grids, lateral-thinking problems, wordplay and recreational mathematics, a third of them with images. Every puzzle carries the answer accepted by its author, a short reference answer taken from it (LLM-extracted for the evaluation sets and checked on a 50-puzzle human review), the author's own hints, community tags and a creation date, so that the benchmark supports hint ablations, category analysis and a rolling post-cutoff slice (254 puzzles posted after the training cutoff of six of the nine models we evaluate, including the best one) as a contamination check. We evaluate nine frontier and open models with a two-tier scorer (normalized exact match, then an LLM judge) and validate the judge with three judges from two model families: they agree on 95-97% of verdicts (kappa 0.82-0.96), move no model by more than 1.6 points, and show no own-family preference. The best model, GPT-6.1, reaches 74.8% on a shared 255-puzzle set; a hard core of 17 puzzles defeats all nine models; model failures are strongly correlated, and the second-best model contributes a single puzzle to a greedy ensemble, yet an oracle over all nine reaches 91%. On a 100-puzzle multimodal pilot, withholding the question's image costs the two best models 24 and 35 points. We release the dataset, the scoring pipeline and all per-row provenance.
  </details>

- **[Extraneous Cognitive Load in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33197)**  `arXiv:2611.33197`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Arushi Gupta, Sheng Liu, James Zou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cognitive Load Theory (CLT) shows that human problem-solving is affected by both intrinsic load, arising from task difficulty, and extraneous load, arising from how task information is presented. Large language models (LLMs) show analogous effects: presentation alone can affect model performance, but these effects have not yet been systematically isolated from task difficulty. To address this gap, we introduce CogLoadBench, a controlled benchmark that varies five CLT-inspired presentation factors across three reasoning task families. Across models, we find that higher extraneous load conditions often reduce accuracy by either adding competition or complicating the reasoning path. To support diagnosis and mitigation of load-related effects, we propose the Extraneous Load Score (ELS), a prompt-time metric computed from activations that estimates a model's extraneous load, and show that it generalizes across reasoning task types and load levels. We further show that shifting model representations toward lower-load directions at inference time can improve average model performance without changing prompt content.
  </details>

- **[$μ$-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-User Task Completion in Shared Interaction Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34920)**  `arXiv:2611.34920`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qihao Lin, Guanxu Chen, Shuhang Zheng, Xiaoya Lu, Jingyi Yang, Terumi Chiba, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Voice agents in shared spaces must satisfy interdependent multi-user requests while upholding per-user authority, privacy, and task constraints. Existing evaluations offer limited coverage when participants react throughout a session. We introduce $\mu$-Bench, a benchmark for joint task completion in closed-loop, full-duplex multi-user voice interaction. Simulated participants act from isolated profiles of goals, knowledge, and permissions, adapting to the agent's speech and tool outcomes. Across 20 domains, 100 scenarios vary group size, acoustics, and follow-up demands to form 800 cases (408 tool-augmented, 392 tool-free). Joint Task Success (JTS) requires all user goals, privacy boundaries, and terminal states to hold jointly, while Pure JTS further disallows unauthorized tool calls; Attribution, Threading, and Coordination (ATC) diagnostics localize failures. Across 13 voice agents, proprietary systems peak at 62.24\% tool-free and 65.44\% tool-augmented success, while open-weight models remain below 20\%. ATC diagnostics show that open-weight models lag primarily in multi-turn attribution and threading, and controlled contrasts reveal that larger groups consistently degrade joint task success across capable systems. By coupling reactive multi-user simulation with verifiable session-level outcomes, $\mu$-Bench provides a rigorous testbed for shared voice agents.
  </details>

- **[DRBench Pro: A Knowledge-Centric Benchmark for Professional Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34852)**  `arXiv:2611.34852`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Han Huang, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Yijun Li, Chao Deng, Jie Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research agents gather information from multiple sources and synthesize it into reports on complex, open-ended questions. Existing benchmarks assess report quality along several dimensions, but lack a dedicated knowledge-centric evaluation of which research directions a report covers and how deeply it explores them. We introduce DRBench Pro, a benchmark that makes structured knowledge coverage the primary evaluation target. Our automated pipeline builds local knowledge trees from Wikipedia and uses sampled subgraphs to generate research queries and evidence-grounded rubrics. Each rubric corresponds to a relation in the tree, preserving the structure of the knowledge being evaluated. Agents receive only the research query. This structure allows us to measure breadth across major research directions and depth along related knowledge chains, with their geometric mean giving overall coverage. The benchmark includes 144 cases across 24 domains and 44,102 rubrics. Evaluating 16 deep research agents reveals different strengths in breadth and depth, with the best-performing agent reaching 57.04% overall coverage. Rubric judgments agree closely with human annotations, and agent rankings remain stable across the tested metric configurations.
  </details>

- **[MINE: A Training-Free Defense against Indirect Prompt Injection via Mutual Information and Embedding Variance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34188)**  `arXiv:2611.34188`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Truong Phan, Ngoc-Quan Pham, Dat Quoc Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing defenses against indirect prompt injection in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) are inherently reactive: prompt-engineering methods are often overridden by the injected instructions, while supervised detectors must be retrained for each new attack style. We propose a different paradigm: defend by maximizing relevance, not by detecting attacks. Our method, MINE, is training-free and attack-agnostic, and it also serves as a query-conditioned reranker. To evaluate defenses under realistic conditions, we introduce LC-Inj-SQuAD, a long-context benchmark with an average of 4,032 words per context. We evaluate on four datasets spanning short-context QA (Inj-SQuAD, Inj-TriviaQA), long-context QA (LC-Inj-SQuAD), and agent tool-call injection (InjecAgent). Across all four, MINE achieves the best accuracy--security trade-off among training-free defenses. It is also the only defense that stays usable against a fully adaptive attacker, generalizes to agent tool-call injection, and achieves the lowest inference latency on long contexts.
  </details>

- **[Refine, Don't Reinvent: RASA-Bench for Source-Grounded Hindi Prose Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29750)**  `arXiv:2611.29750`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Abdur Rahman, Pratham Singla, Mohammad Saifullah, Vasu Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For a producer of Hindi creative prose, drafting is cheap and editing is not: a machine-written episode, story, or essay reads as under-written and flat, laced with translationese, and it sometimes quietly alters what the source says. RASA-Bench targets that editing task across three Hindi prose forms, measuring how well a model refines an under-written AI draft, a task distinct from writing from scratch. Across 2,050 drafts and eight refiners (16,400 rewrites), a four-layer protocol credits a rewrite only after it passes a faithfulness check against its own source, then ranks the survivors by holistic editorial preference through per-form Bradley-Terry fits whose centered coefficients are standardized before equal-weight averaging. Staying faithful proves to be a decisive test that behaves differently by form: gate-failure rates range from 9.1% on short stories to 29.5% on audio fiction, and the drift that disqualifies a rewrite shifts from events and identity in narrative to claims and evidence in essays. A 600-case human audit puts gate agreement at 0.925 (0.91 to 0.94 across forms), whereas subjective preference is form-sensitive and least settled for essays, among human readers no less than the model judge. The benchmark, evaluation protocol, and Hindi prose diagnostics are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Achieving Precise Text-To-Cypher Via Grounded Knowledge Graph Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14325)**  `arXiv:2606.14325`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Francesco Cazzaro, Jessica Lennon, Ariadna Quattoni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Property Graphs are rapidly being adopted as database frameworks for representing heterogeneous data sources. To enable precise access to the information contained in them we need conversational interfaces based on Text-To-Cypher (Text2Cypher) parsers. This paper presents an automatic synthetic data generation method that can be leveraged to fine-tune small LLMs for this task. We conduct experiments on all the major Text-To-Cypher benchmarks, demonstrating that with our synthetic data generation approach we can significantly increase the performance of small LLMs, allowing them to compete with much larger proprietary models. This means that in settings in which models must be locally deployed we can ensure data-sovereignty without sacrificing accuracy and without costly annotation campaigns.
  </details>

- **[Polyrating: A Cost-Effective and Bias-Aware Rating System for LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.00696)**  `arXiv:2409.00696`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jasper Dekoninck, Maximilian Baader, Martin Vechev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rating-based human evaluation has become an essential tool to accurately evaluate the impressive performance of large language models (LLMs). However, current rating systems suffer from several important limitations: first, they fail to account for biases that significantly influence evaluation results, second, they require large and expensive preference datasets to obtain accurate ratings, and third, they do not facilitate meaningful comparisons of model ratings across different tasks. To address these issues, we introduce Polyrating, an expressive and flexible rating system based on maximum a posteriori estimation that enables a more nuanced and thorough analysis of model performance at lower costs. Polyrating can detect and quantify biases affecting human preferences, ensuring fairer model comparisons. Further, Polyrating can reduce the cost of human evaluations by up to $59\%$ for new models and up to $77\%$ for new tasks by leveraging existing benchmark scores. Lastly, Polyrating enables direct comparisons of ratings across different tasks, providing a comprehensive understanding of an LLMs' strengths, weaknesses, and relative performance across different applications.
  </details>

- **[The Linguistic Knowledge Benchmark: Do Language Models Know the Foundations of Early-Grade Literacy?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34823)**  `arXiv:2611.34823`  `cs.CL`  
  _Maxime Lelièvre, Alasdair Mackintosh, Dominic Andrews, Tobias Murray, Moritz Boos, Oliver G. B. Garrod_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many languages of instruction in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) are poorly represented in the data used to train language models. Before these models can support early-grade literacy in such languages, we need to know whether they hold the linguistic knowledge of each language that literacy teaching relies on. To test this, we present the Linguistic Knowledge Benchmark and the reproducible, largely automated pipeline that turns curriculum documents, teacher guides and pupil books into a vetted question set organised on a fixed grid of nine linguistic domains and seven grade levels. The pipeline combines LLM-driven source survey, feature consolidation, item generation and quality control with an item-selection step inspired by item response theory and a final expert review. We instantiate it for Kiswahili (Kenya; 135 items) and English (United Kingdom; 137 items) and evaluate 121 and 119 models respectively. Frontier models exceed 90% accuracy in both languages, but accuracy falls steeply for smaller and cheaper models, and more so in Kiswahili. The two best models under 10 billion parameters reach 91.7% and 73.0% in English but only 51.9% and 40.7% in Kiswahili, and the mean accuracy of models deployable on mobile and tablet hardware is 47% in English against 24% in Kiswahili. The models most practical for low-resource classrooms are therefore the least capable in the languages they would serve. We release a 20-item sample per language, all pipeline prompts, open-source code, and a public leaderboard. This is the first rung of a three-level evaluation ladder for measuring and generating decodable text to support foundational literacy.
  </details>

- **[VulnSafe: Measuring Vulnerability-Aware Relational Safety in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34819)**  `arXiv:2611.34819`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaming Luo, Jiacheng Ji, Haoan Jin, Xiaoning Sun, Qiuzhi Xiong, Yuxin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emotionally supportive interactions require large language models not only to avoid overtly harmful content, but also to recognize vulnerability-related risk, regulate their relational stance, preserve user agency, and provide context-appropriate support. Existing safety benchmarks often measure isolated violations, leaving unclear what underlying response competence their scores represent. We introduce VulnSafe, a Chinese capability assessment centered on Vulnerability-Aware Response Competence (VARC). VARC organizes relational safety into five complementary constructs: Risk Perception, Safety Preservation, Autonomy Protection, Supportive Response, and Boundary Management. Following an evidence-centered design, 41 scenarios elicit construct-relevant behavior through 2,050 balanced prompts and scenario-specific scoring rubrics. The assessment further separates proactive should-do behavior from prohibitive should-not behavior. Across eight representative LLMs, results show distinct competence profiles and larger disparities than prohibitive requirements. VulnSafe therefore makes relational safety interpretable as a multidimensional model capability rather than as refusal compliance alone.
  </details>

- **[DFly2: Turbocharging Long-Context Speculative Decoding by Sharing the Target's Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34776)**  `arXiv:2611.34776`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hong Liu, Rui Cen, Junhan Shi, Lin Niu, Runzhi Fan, Zhaojing Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To avoid the quadratic cost of dense attention over long contexts, models such as DeepSeek-V4 and Hy4 have turned natively sparse, and speculative decoding, which matters most where decoding is slowest, increasingly runs on them. Their drafters, however, still keep a context cache of their own, over the whole prompt or a sliding window. Beyond memory, such a cache costs prefill time, prefix-cache hits and decode concurrency. Drafters that read the target's cache avoid one of their own, but settle only which cache, not which positions, and still read everything or a recent window. Neither rule is made for the query at hand, and computing a selection per query in the drafter would cost more as the context grows. Yet a natively sparse target already picks positions for each query, over a cache it keeps anyway. Both of the drafter's choices are therefore already made, and paid for, by the target: its cache and its selection. We measure how far one selection carries: made a step early, it still covers close to nine tenths of what each later token selects on DS-V4.1-Flash. We present DFly2, a block-parallel drafter that shares the target's sparsity. Each draft layer reads the KV its paired target layer cached (KV sharing), at the positions that layer's indexer selected, a fixed number anywhere in the context (Index sharing). DFly2 thus runs no scoring pass and keeps no cache of its own. On DS-V4.1-Flash, GLM-5.3-Flash and Hy4~flash, whose sparse attention and architecture differ, it accepts more tokens per step than DFlash and DSpark trained on the same recipe, over general, long-context and agentic benchmarks. Its margin over the stronger baseline widens from 3.9%-10.5% on general workloads to 10.0%-31.5% on long-context and agentic ones. At high concurrency on DS-V4.1-Flash, at matched per-user decode speed, its throughput is 62% and 81% higher on two 64K sets.
  </details>

- **[PoVi: Agentic Poetry-to-Video Generation with Knowledge Grounding and Figurative Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34690)**  `arXiv:2611.34690`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuxia Zhu, Shiying Zhang, Lingzhuang Sun, Song Yang, Siheng Wu, Xinglong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Poetry-to-video generation requires more than translating text into visual scenes. Poetic language is highly compressed, often depends on historical and cultural knowledge, and frequently conveys meaning through metaphor, imagery, and other non-literal expressions. We introduce PoVi, a benchmark and agentic framework for grounded poetry-to-video generation, evaluating semantic grounding, discourse coherence, and audiovisual realization. PoVi reconstructs poem semantics into an evidence-bounded representation and progressively transforms them into narration, interpretation, character assets, and shot-level visual plans, followed by narration-aligned video generation. Across three planning backbones and four video generators, PoVi consistently improves over single-call generation, with the largest gains on implicit background knowledge and metaphorical relations. PoVi provides a testbed for studying knowledge-grounded, long-horizon multimodal generation beyond direct text-to-video synthesis.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Unreliable Operator References: Claim-Level Rewards for Customer Support Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34341)**  `arXiv:2611.34341`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dmitrii Stoianov, Olga Tsymboi, Danil Taranets, Daniil Strizhakov, Ilya Buryak, Almaz Dautov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Even a correct operator reply can be an unreliable training target for a customer-support agent. About a quarter of the factual claims in these replies lacked full support in the agent's knowledge base and tool records. We treat operator replies as claims to verify rather than answers to imitate. Unverified claims guide revisions to the knowledge base and tools. Verified claims serve as coverage targets in a claim-level GRPO reward that scores factual precision against the evidence the agent retrieved for its answer. On an offline benchmark, 8.6% of the GRPO agent's text answers were flagged for hallucinations, compared with 13.6% for the supervised fine-tuning baseline. In a randomized A/B test against the existing procedural bot, the deployed system increased the share of conversations closed without a human operator from 63.3% to 80.8%.
  </details>

- **[IRTIQA: Evolving in English, Evaluated in Urdu -- Does Agent Self-Evolution Transfer from English to Urdu and Code-Switched Input?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34303)**  `arXiv:2611.34303`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abdullah Hashmat, Danish Javed, Usman Naseem, Agha Ali Raza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents increasingly learn from their own experience, the tasks they learn from and are evaluated on are almost always in English. We ask whether self-evolution transfers from English to Urdu, and release IRTIQA, a benchmark of aligned English, Urdu-translated, and Urdu code-switched variants of DROP, SNIPS, AppWorld, and SkillsBench, built with a matrix-language code-switching pipeline and human verification. Using IRTIQA, we evaluate three self-evolving frameworks, Tool-R0 (self-play RL), AgentEvolver (experience-guided RL), and Autogenesis (protocol-level evolution), against their unevolved backbones. Self-evolution often raises capability: AgentEvolver lifts AppWorld goal completion from near zero to 20-36% across all conditions, and Autogenesis improves SkillsBench pass rate from 16.48% to 22.22%. But it rarely closes the language gap. Evolved systems remain stronger in English, by up to 0.26 DROP F1, the gap is largest on multi-step tasks, and code-switching behaves as a distinct condition rather than degraded translation. On a shared Gemma-4 31B backbone, the gap exists before evolution, and RL-based evolution can hurt Urdu reasoning more than English. Language parity must therefore be an explicit design target, as it does not emerge from self-improvement alone.
  </details>

- **[Lightweight Reasoning Transfer via Chain-of-Thought Vector Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34183)**  `arXiv:2611.34183`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yulin Qi, Shuo Xu, Shuxia Lin, Qingjian Ni, Xu Yang, Xin Geng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transferring reasoning capabilities from large language models to smaller ones often requires extensive teacher supervision or costly student adaptation. We study projected cross-model Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Vector transfer, a two-stage approach that keeps both models frozen. First, we learn a task-level CoT Vector in the teacher using dataset-provided solution traces and final-answer supervision. We then train a lightweight linear projector to map the fixed vector into a smaller student's residual space. Student-side training uses only final-answer cross-entropy on a small subset of task training examples; it requires neither teacher outputs nor intermediate solution traces. We select the student injection layer and intervention scale on a labeled support set and report results on the disjoint remainder of each evaluation split. On GSM8K and MATH, the projected vector improves base students by 10.37-21.98 accuracy points across the evaluated teacher-student settings. On four code-generation benchmarks, the Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct student gains 3.23-4.67 pass@1 points, while the base Qwen2.5-7B student gains up to 9.65 points. These results show that a teacher-side reasoning vector can be reused across smaller students with different residual dimensions through projector-only adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Reliable User Simulation in Complex Multi-Step Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34044)**  `arXiv:2611.34044`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pusala Pavan Teja, Jayasimha Talur, Saab Mansour, Marcello Federico_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based user simulators are increasingly used to evaluate conversational assistants. Yet, these simulators can drift from their assigned goals or fail to exhaustively explore all specified options, causing evaluation errors incorrectly attributed to the assistant. Prior work has examined goal alignment in single-task settings, but many practical scenarios require simulators to execute multi-step workflows with sequential tasks, fallback logic, and cross-task dependencies, where simulator fidelity has not been measured. To close this gap, we introduce a benchmark of 500 workflows represented using the Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) abstraction. Our experiments reveal that the simulator's fidelity degrades sharply with workflow complexity, and that goal drift accounts for the majority of failures. Fidelity also depends on the assistant's response style, with the same simulator scoring substantially better or worse across styles. To address this, we propose ReUST (Reasoning + User State Tracking), a simple prompting strategy to maintain state information across dialogue turns. ReUST improves goal success rate by more than 12 points for mid-sized and large models, and reduces simulator sensitivity to the assistant's response style.
  </details>

- **[JHealthBench-L: Clinician-Reviewed Localization of HealthBench for Rubric-Based Medical LLM Evaluation in Japan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34024)**  `arXiv:2611.34024`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shohei Hisada, Keisuke Orimo, Kenji Igarashi, Kota Sato, Masahiro Kazama, Mitsuhisa Ota, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based benchmarks such as HealthBench evaluate large language models (LLMs) on open-ended health consultations using case-specific physician criteria, many of which presuppose the healthcare institutions, medications, and care pathways of their source region. Under machine translation alone, these regional assumptions remain in the rubric, leaving responses appropriate in Japan uncredited or falsely penalized under foreign clinical rules. To adapt established clinical evaluation perspectives to Japan without authoring rubrics from scratch, we construct JHealthBench-L (79 patient-facing consultations, 1,022 criteria) through source-cited LLM drafting, single-specialist selection and revision, independent dual-physician review, and meta-review. Specialists rewrote only 4.4% of criteria from scratch, and independent reviewers approved 93.6% of Stage-2 criteria without issue. Comparing JHealthBench-L with a machine-translated baseline across 18 LLMs reveals that translation-only evaluation preserves coarse model rankings ($\tau = 0.817$, mean shift +0.027) yet flips verdicts on 17.7% of modified criteria (vs. 7.3% re-grading noise) and reduces false penalties on Japan-adapted negative criteria by 7.1 percentage points, masking item-level clinical mismeasurement behind stable leaderboards. Axis-level and paired analyses further show that context awareness and Japan-specific clinical criteria remain primary bottlenecks, whereas Japanese continual pretraining and reasoning post-training substantially improve locally grounded performance. Datasets, physician revision logs, and evaluation code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Knowledge Distillation for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33670)**  `arXiv:2611.33670`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuxuan Sun, Yuanjian Xu, Jianing Hao, Yu Li, Sowmen Das, Zhong Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation has recently proven highly effective for improving autoregressive models, but its applicability to DLMs remains poorly understood. We present the first systematic study of KD for DLMs and uncover two non-intuitive failures of classical forward-KL (FKL) distillation: (i) student perplexity does not improve monotonically with more teacher-generated data, and (ii) on the same teacher-generated corpus, FKL distillation underperforms simply training the student with the original DLM denoising objective. To address them, we propose a unified, data-free distillation framework for masked DLMs. First, the framework runs an off-policy distillation phase that absorbs general knowledge from a static set of teacher-generated data. Then, the framework employs on-policy distillation which consists of three techniques: a trajectory loss along the student's own reverse process, confidence reweighting that down-weights tokens where the teacher is unsure, and a trust-region anchor that stabilizes the moving target. On MDLM, the resulting student attains a GPT-2 perplexity of $24.75$, surpassing the teacher's $27.04$; on Dream-7B distillation to a 93M student model, the framework reduces Qwen-2.5 perplexity from the FKL-distilled baseline of $25.23$ to $19.83$ while preserving performance on zero-shot general capability benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Failure Broadens, Success Sharpens: Continual Adaptation of Tool-Use Agents Across Diverse Tasks with Dual-Polarity Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33612)**  `arXiv:2611.33612`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianmai M. Zhang, Hoda Shajari, Amr Hendy, Xiaohu Liu, Piyush Behre, Omar Zia Khan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents can self-improve over time by utilizing procedural memory derived from prior task trajectories, yet the distinct utilities of memories derived from local successes and failures remain underexplored. This work studies this question in a realistic setting with heterogeneous streams of multi-domain tool-use tasks and various environment constraints common in real-world scenarios. We develop \emph{Dual-Polarity Memory with Periodic Maintenance} (DualPM), a simple, retrieval-free memory workflow characterized by (1) customizable reflection and procedural memory generation from both local successes and failures within each trajectory, and (2) periodic memory consolidation over long task streams under a given memory capacity, preventing memory explosion. Online prequential evaluation with fine-grained metrics reveals that failure memory more strongly broadens coverage, success memory better streamlines execution, while combining both polarities effectively integrates both benefits. Further studies demonstrate that DualPM enables cross-domain memory generalization and continual memory adaptation, while external evaluation on EvoMemBench reveals the portability and effectiveness of DualPM as a general long-term procedural memory method.
  </details>

- **[LogToPersona: New Benchmark and Efficient Persona Prediction through Semantic Log Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33510)**  `arXiv:2611.33510`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ahmed Alajrami, Ondrej Bohdal, Seokwoo Song, Taegeon Um, Peyman Hosseini, Savas Ozkan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detailed knowledge of a user's persona is valuable for personalizing generative AI models and agents, yet such details are difficult to obtain and change over time. We address the task of inferring persona attributes from a single day of mobile activity logs. No suitable publicly available benchmark exists for this task, so we introduce a synthetic benchmark of 1,600 persona-log pairs. We target on-device inference so that raw logs never leave the phone, which makes memory rather than speed the limiting constraint: a day of activity yields roughly 30K tokens, whose key-value cache alone exceeds the INT4 weights of a 4B-parameter model. We therefore propose a log filtering method that retains only behaviorally distinct entries. Filtering reduces the memory footprint by up to 39% across nine models from four families and more than halves prefill time, while maintaining or improving prediction quality. For Qwen3-4B it removes 2.5GB, bringing the model from well beyond a realistic per-process budget to a manageable one. Within our benchmark, these results indicate that preserving diverse behavioral events matters more for persona prediction than retaining every semantically similar log entry.
  </details>

- **[Lost in Translation, Not in Confidence: Benchmarking and Calibrating Multilingual Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33307)**  `arXiv:2611.33307`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingzhe Du, Thong Thanh Nguyen, Nguyen Tran Cong Duy, See-Kiong Ng, Luu Anh Tuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models are language models fine-tuned to return a probability for each option of a typed query, which routers and guardrails threshold directly. They are built on multilingual bases but ranked almost entirely in English. We evaluate 25 top-ranked open-weight decision models on 17 tasks in 14 languages, comparing each language with English on parallel items. Every model loses accuracy in every language, from about 2 points in high-resource languages to 8 in Tamil, while confidence falls only half as much: a threshold set for 95\% accuracy in English yields 90% in Tamil. The size of the loss is set by the language and the base model rather than by leaderboard rank. In 81 controlled fine-tuning runs, task skill learned in English transfers to other languages and a few thousand target-language items narrow large losses, but neither more data nor full-parameter tuning closes them. Finally, the same test items are answered more accurately in all 23 benchmark--language pairs we compare when an LLM rather than a person translated them, so LLM-translated test sets understate the gap.
  </details>

- **[Focus On Facts: Stylistically Invariant and Factually Sensitive Text Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33250)**  `arXiv:2611.33250`  `cs.CL`  
  _Maximus Adeola_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text embedding models can conflate surface-form similarity with factual identity. Given an altered near-copy with one corrupted fact and a differently worded fact-preserving rewrite, the base model prefers the altered near-copy. We test whether hard-negative contrastive supervision can reduce this surface-similarity shortcut. We construct FOF-Bench, an 83,263-triplet benchmark spanning five domains. Each anchor is paired with an LLM-generated, fact-preserving stylistic rewrite and a matched fact-altered near-copy. We fine-tune FOF-80M (Focus On Facts), an 80M-parameter embedding model, with a triplet contrastive objective that ranks the rewrite above the factual alteration. On 8,327 held-out test triplets, the base model chooses the factually altered text in 99.58% of cases (0.42% factual preference rate; mean separation -0.1650). After fine-tuning, mean positive--negative separation is +0.0329, 98.5% of individual margins move in the intended direction, and the preference rate reaches 29.37%. On DiSC, an external style-transfer corpus, mean cross-style similarity increases from 0.7905 to 0.9559. The model also penalizes omission and transfers poorly to directional tasks such as NLI and summarization. We characterize FOF-80M as a soft factual fingerprint for studying factual retention under stylistic variation, not as a general-purpose factuality metric.
  </details>

- **[When Ink Meets Pixel: Intent-Aware OCR with VLMs for Human-Corrected Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32446)**  `arXiv:2611.32446`  `cs.CL`  
  _Akshata A Bhat, Daman Deep Singh, Niyati Chhaya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human annotations on documents (such as arrows, carets, strikethroughs, and insertions) express corrective intent yet existing OCR and document understanding systems treat these marks as visual noise rather than semantic edit operations. We introduce intent-aware OCR, the task of generating corrected transcriptions by jointly reasoning over original document content and visual intent cues (handwritten or digital) that convey edit intent. To support this setting, we present DocIntent, a multimodal dataset of 1,600 financial documents with over 5,700 annotated visual intent cues, each paired with layout-aware, intent-resolved OCR targets. We further introduce IntentBench, a 216-document benchmark designed to evaluate model robustness under diverse real-world annotation conditions. Leveraging DocIntent, we fine-tune DocIntentOCR, a suite of 3B and 7B parameter vision-language models, under direct intent supervision on corrected transcriptions. On IntentBench, the fine-tuned 3B and 7B models achieve F1-scores of 0.81 and 0.83, respectively, substantially outperforming commercial closed-source (e.g., GPT-4.1 (0.626)), and open-source models (e.g., LLaMA-3.2-Vision-90B (0.50)) demonstrating that intent-aware supervision enables compact domain-specific models to outperform substantially larger general-purpose systems on this task. This work establishes intent-aware OCR as a novel human-in-the-loop document understanding task and provides foundational resources (dataset, benchmark, models) to advance research in interactive document processing.
  </details>

- **[Can Language Models Learn to Forecast Stock Prices?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36914)**  `arXiv:2609.36914`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Guo, Suozhi Huang, Shuzhen Li, Yunlong Gao, Zerui Cheng, Jason Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We build BETA, a chronological sandbox over S&P 500 stocks in which a model calls tools to retrieve prices, indicators, and news available up to a decision date, and then forecasts the stock's future return. Evaluating frontier models in this sandbox reveals three systematic weaknesses: their scores decline as the test period approaches the present while a simple momentum baseline stays stable, they predict gains far more often than gains occur, and they predict moves much smaller than those that are realized. We then train models directly in the sandbox. We first fine-tune on filtered, hindsight-generated tool-use trajectories for historical tasks judged predictable from the evidence available at the time, teaching the model how to investigate a stock. We then apply reinforcement learning with rewards based on realized returns. During RL, long investigations frequently exceed the context window. We therefore introduce a selective compaction tool that allows the model to compress chosen portions of its interaction history. We address these challenges with Context-Aligned Turn-Level PPO, which uses a turn-level critic and computes policy updates using each turn's actual context. The resulting models, which we call AURA, substantially reduce all three weaknesses. Training improves the 8B model's score from 24.0 to 47.0 and the 30B model's from 27.4 to 54.7, with the latter surpassing all evaluated frontier models on our benchmark and achieving its largest margin on the most recent test period. These scores describe tasks selected for alignment between the available evidence and the realized return. Across all 412 candidates in the latest period, the trained 30B model still leads (35.17), while the trained 8B model scores below momentum (25.91 versus 28.33).
  </details>

- **[AdaTutoRank: Learning to Rerank Document Sets via Adaptive Tutoring Optimization for RAG and Deep Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32472)**  `arXiv:2609.32472`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kailin Jiang, Jian Xi, Lei Liu, Yangqi Chen, Hui Xu, Hongwei Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document rerankers determine what evidence reaches the downstream model in RAG and deep research, yet mainstream rerankers select by relevance matching, and individually relevant documents rarely constitute the complete, complementary, non-redundant set a complex information need demands. Prior work rewards a set by its aggregate rubric score, shifting the objective from ranking documents to composing sets. Yet that score is one scalar shared by every document in the set, so the supervision is sparse: a redundant document is rewarded with the rest whenever the set scores well, and a decisive one penalized with the rest whenever it does not; credit assignment leaves contributors indistinguishable from free riders. On-policy distillation could densify this supervision, but existing methods give every rollout the same fixed guidance, too prescriptive for strong rollouts and too abstract for weak ones. We therefore propose AdaTutoRank, a setwise reranker trained with Adaptive Tutoring Optimization (ATO) under a three-level hierarchy of nine rubric dimensions, which supplies silver labels for the cold start, rewards for reinforcement learning, and hints for distillation. ATO draws three hint forms of increasing specificity from the policy's own frozen snapshot: the rubrics alone, a self-selector's sibling-set chosen under rubrics, and a self-reflector's reflection contrasting the rollout with that sibling-set; each rollout receives the form matched to its quality. Re-scoring that rollout under the hint-conditioned frozen teacher and the hint-free snapshot distills the hint's effect into a token-level advantage that complements the group-relative outcome advantage. Across ten benchmarks spanning RAG, deep research, and setwise evaluation, AdaTutoRank attains the best overall performance while issuing fewer retrieval calls.
  </details>

- **[NOLLI: A Difficulty-Calibrated English-Korean Puzzle Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04397)**  `arXiv:2608.04397`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dasol Choi, Joonyong Park, Daegon Yu, Soo Yong Kim, Youngsook Song, Seunghyeok Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce NOLLI, a procedurally generated, difficulty-calibrated English-Korean puzzle benchmark with 15 puzzle types (25 tasks; 7,500 items). Each instance is seed-regenerable, has a verified unique solution, and is scored deterministically. We calibrate difficulty behaviorally by tuning generator parameters to target accuracy bands on a reference model. NOLLI has three task families: matched direct translations, script adaptations over Hangul jamo (sub-syllabic letters), and Korean-only cultural or orthographic tasks. We evaluate 15 frontier, open-weight, and Korean-developed models; gap analyses use the 12 above a 3% overall-accuracy floor. On matched direct translations, English and Korean accuracy is equivalent within a prespecified $\pm 10$ percentage-point margin. Script adaptations behave differently: Korean Cipher trails English by up to 68.7 percentage points, while Cryptarithmetic over the same jamo shows no systematic Korean penalty, and Jamo Composition strongly correlates with Korean Cipher performance. Korean-only tasks are also heterogeneous: rule-application performance varies across models, while Kinship remains below each non-floor model's Korean direct-task baseline. Finally, structural size does not increase from Easy to Hard in 7 of 15 types, showing that size alone is an unreliable proxy for empirical difficulty.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Relevance-Centric Retrieval: Rubric-Oriented Document Set Selection and Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19747)**  `arXiv:2607.19747`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kailin Jiang, Lei Liu, Jian Xi, Hui Xu, Junlin Liu, Baochen Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models and AI agents become the primary consumers of search results, document set quality determines the upper bound of downstream generation. Yet existing evaluation systems remain confined to scoring documents independently and aggregating via nDCG, ignoring inter-document interactions (redundancy, conflict, complementarity) and unable to answer what makes one document set better than another. To address these issues, we propose a complete evaluate-diagnose-optimize framework. We design SetwiseEvalKit, a three-level, nine-dimension document set evaluation benchmark covering both short-form and long-form scenarios, comprising approximately 28K high-quality evaluation rubrics. We systematically evaluate 12 rerankers: even the best method achieves no more than 45% coverage, cross-document coordination dimensions are universally weak, and no single method maintains top performance across both settings. Building on this, we propose Rubric4Setwise, a training-free method that converts rubric-based evaluation criteria into document set selection signals, achieving the best downstream generation performance with fewer documents and search rounds. It is the only method that maintains state-of-the-art results across both scenarios, validating the effectiveness of closing the loop from evaluation to optimization.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge-Preserving LLM Architecture Standardization: Analytic Normalizer Mapping with Equal-Size Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25676)**  `arXiv:2605.25676`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kisu Yang, Yoonna Jang, Hyeonseok Moon, Hwanseok Jang, Taewoo Lee, Hyungjin Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Competent pretrained LLMs are often locked into architectures that mainstream tooling no longer supports, which strands their knowledge. The conventional remedy, uptraining, needs heavy compute plus a corpus covering everything the model knows. We study a common class of such conversions, architecture standardization, converting a model's normalization layers and interface to the Llama-family template while preserving dimensions, tokenizer, and attention layout. Our KEPT combines direct parameter copying, a training-free LayerNorm-to-RMSNorm initialization (OPM) that is near-optimal under activation assumptions we verify on three models, and standard equal-size distillation from the frozen source, without access to the source model's pretraining corpus. Converted from Orion-14B with ~123M tokens in four A100-days, Llamion stays within one point of the source's H6 average and MT-Bench score, shows no significant difference on Korean MMLU, HumanEval, or MBPP, and enters a Korean leaderboard closed to the custom-loader source, where its 66.87% KoMMLU accuracy exceeds the next-best similar-size entry by more than seven points at submission time. In controlled runs, the default normalizer initialization fails to recover the model under the same budget, and both FLOPs-matched uptraining and sequence-level distillation trail on MT-Bench.
  </details>

- **[AutoE2E: Don't speak - Language free end-to-end autonomy for L2++ ADAS and L4 Robotaxis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22374)**  `arXiv:2611.22374`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Muhammad Zain Khawaja, Zaynab El Mawas, Ryota Yamada, Gabriela Cordova, Arseni Lysak, Intisar Chowdhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AutoE2E is an open-source end-to-end driving model developed by the Autoware Foundation Robotaxi Working Group. It introduces a temporal bird's-eye-view (BEV) architecture that combines multi-camera perception, HD-map information and route conditioning to predict future vehicle motion. The evaluated model uses a pretrained camera-BEV backbone, temporal feature fusion and a lightweight recurrent trajectory planner. It contains approximately 79.9 million parameters, of which approximately 1.45 million are trainable in the evaluated configuration. The v1.0 release provides model checkpoints, training and evaluation code, and DataModelConsole, a dashboard for visualizing and inspecting model experiments. On the KITScenes validation benchmark, the fine-tuned checkpoint achieves an average displacement error (ADE) of 0.745 m and a final displacement error (FDE) of 2.020 m at a three-second prediction horizon. The paper also reports preliminary real-vehicle research conducted with the Technical University of Munich's EDGAR platform, alongside ongoing collaboration with the Karlsruhe Institute of Technology. AutoE2E v1.0 is an early open research release. Its quantitative evaluation is open-loop, and the model has not yet demonstrated validated closed-loop autonomous driving. The release is intended to provide the autonomous-driving community with an inspectable baseline for further research, experimentation and collaborative development.
  </details>

- **[MeterRouter: Budget-Aware Dynamic Routing for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34327)**  `arXiv:2611.34327`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yue Xu, Leikun Yin, Ruiming Min, Yufeng Lai, Justin Andrew Johnson, Sitao Luan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) outperform single-agent systems, but inter-agent communication incurs substantial token overhead. While assigning a large LLM to every agent is common, it results in inefficient resource utilization, leaving the system far from the Pareto frontier. The cost of answering a query is set jointly by how many agents are recruited, the role and LLM assigned to each, and communication topology among them. Most existing routers treat cost only implicitly rather than as an explicit optimization constraint. A fixed accuracy-cost preference also cannot express how much a caller is willing to pay for a particular query. We introduce MeterRouter, a budget-aware dynamic router that incrementally synthesizes a collaboration graph by selecting each agent's role, LLM, incoming connections, and termination conditioned on the query and a per-query monetary budget. We initialize the router by imitating successful execution graphs, then jointly refine its decisions with a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) whose reward favors correct answers and penalizes spending and budget overruns. Across five benchmarks and a three-tier LLM pool, MeterRouter is (1) accurate, achieving performance comparable to the strongest MAS; (2) economical, reaching that accuracy at 82% lower cost than the strongest MAS router and extending the accuracy-cost frontier beyond the ceiling of single-agent systems; and (3) controllable, adapting its spending to the specified budget while rarely exceeding it.
  </details>

- **[HoMe: Toward Practical Harness Optimization of Memory Systems for AI Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34624)**  `arXiv:2611.34624`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yifei Xu, Li Yu, Zhaoyang Liu, Ce Wang, Sen Huang, Yanyan Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The behavior of memory systems for long-running AI assistants is governed by a harness, which consists of the code, prompts, and configurations for memory construction, maintenance, retrieval, and answer generation. However, optimizing the harness of a memory system remains largely manual, and unrestricted editing alone does not ensure structural improvements. In this paper, we introduce HoMe, an evaluation-driven framework that iteratively evaluates candidate harnesses and derives each new candidate from any previously evaluated harness by editing any part of it. HoMe combines session-level memory versioning with execution traces to identify shared failure mechanisms across cases and localize them to the responsible sessions and harness components. It encourages early structural exploration through candidate gating and reduces evaluation cost through smoke tests, adaptive sampling, and the reuse of constructed memory. We validate HoMe with the open-source memory system ReMe on two large-scale long-horizon memory benchmarks, LongMemEval-S and BEAM. With six formal candidate evaluations per benchmark, after a full initial evaluation, HoMe discovers harnesses that improve end-task performance over the originally strong harness while consuming far fewer query-time tokens. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MetaDecomp: Adaptive Task Decomposition via Meta-Learned Procedural Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33822)**  `arXiv:2611.33822`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziyi Wang, Yixiao He, Yichen Wu, Ruite Lu, Jiayu Wang, Ying Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reusing past experience in long-horizon tasks requires more than storing successful procedures: Language agents must distinguish which procedures apply and what work remains as execution progresses. We introduce MetaDecomp, an in-context meta-learning framework for adaptive task decomposition that jointly learns a task-relative state space and reusable procedures. Its outer loop builds procedural memory from successful training executions, representing task progress as abstract states and procedures between states as directed acyclic graphs of operations and dependencies. As new progress distinctions are learned, MetaDecomp revisits historical execution evidence to reassess state assignments and reorganize the procedures they support. With memory fixed at test time, the inner loop refines an initial decomposition and revises it from environmental feedback, combining applicable procedure fragments with task-specific steps missing from memory. Evaluated on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld over three seeds, MetaDecomp achieves the highest mean performance among the compared methods in four of five settings, for example, an 86.3% success rate on ALFWorld with Qwen3-4B. Fixing the initial schema or omitting historical reprojection reduces performance in component ablations. MetaDecomp also uses fewer inference tokens than ReAct and AutoRefine in all five settings. Cross-benchmark analysis identifies a low-performance area where partial coverage degrades success relative to hidden memory; high coverage improves success, while low coverage changes little. Code will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[ConceptARC at Scale: Studying Conceptual Understanding in Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06732)**  `arXiv:2611.06732`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Philipp Mondorf, Adrien Deliège, Solim LeGris, Claas Beger, Ryan Yi, Barbara Plank, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) perform well on challenging benchmarks, yet it remains unclear whether they form generalizable abstractions across conceptually related reasoning tasks. The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) provides a controlled testbed for studying abstraction, requiring solvers to infer transformation rules over two-dimensional grids from a small set of examples. While ARC evaluates rule abstraction within individual tasks, different tasks may share higher-level structure despite requiring different transformations$\unicode{x2014}$for example, several tasks may involve reasoning about objects contained within other objects. Benchmarks such as ConceptARC capture this structure by grouping ARC-style tasks into recurring spatial and semantic concepts, but they often contain only a few manually constructed tasks per concept. We introduce ConceptARC at Scale, a generator for creating thousands of ARC-style tasks targeting a specific concept. Using this generator, we construct ConceptARC-100, a dataset of 1,600 new, manually reviewed tasks, with 100 tasks for each of the 16 concepts defined by ConceptARC$\unicode{x2014}$ten times the size of the original benchmark. We evaluate a range of recent LRMs and models trained from scratch on ConceptARC-100 to characterize which concepts they solve reliably and where they struggle. We further analyze the models' internal representations and find clear semantic structure: task representations cluster according to the operations applied to objects, such as recoloring, removal, or translation, even when realized through substantially different rules and scenarios. Together, our results suggest that models form stable abstractions of transformation operations that generalize across diverse tasks.
  </details>

- **[CHD-Bench: Diagnosing Congenital Heart Disease through Interactive Examination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33775)**  `arXiv:2611.33775`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Gautham Krishnan P, Shrisharanyan Vasu, Aniketh Vijesh, Rajagopalan Rameshbabu, Harikrishnan Anil Maya, Sarin Xavier, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diagnosis of congenital heart disease (CHD) requires understanding the spatial relationships between cardiac structures, which clinicians assess by scrolling through CT or cardiac magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) slices and rotating 3D reconstructions. Although automated methods have advanced cardiac segmentation, identifying congenital heart defects from volumetric scans remains an open challenge. The two existing approaches rely on handcrafted diagnostic rules limited to static anatomical abnormalities. We introduce CHD-Bench, the first benchmark for interactive CHD diagnosis, defining a multi-label task and evaluation protocol over 247 CT and MRI scans from three cohorts covering 38 CHD types. We also present CHD-Harness, open-source software that runs a VLM in a 3D Slicer environment through 22 tools, manages each examination, and records the resulting diagnosis and supporting views. We evaluate off-the-shelf medical, open weight, and closed weight VLMs and compare them with a 3D CNN classifier and published rule-based results. Gemini 4 Argon with CHD-Harness achieves the highest macro F1 across all three cohorts (0.63, 0.63, and 0.43). Allowing for interactive examination raises its macro F1 by up to 20 points over static views. These results establish CHD-Bench and CHD-Harness as a foundation for developing and evaluating VLMs for interactive CHD diagnosis.
  </details>

- **[From Shared Patterns to Individual Evidence: Two-Stage Reasoning for Emerging Entities in Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34708)**  `arXiv:2611.34708`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinchuan Zhang, Lei Gao, Hongyu Jiang, Chong Mu, Chenang Cai, Ke Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs) continually introduce emerging entities whose interaction histories evolve from zero-shot debuts into sparse, bursty few-shot trajectories. However, current paradigms are fragmented: pattern-transfer methods lack catalog-wide event memory, whereas online memory models suffer from endpoint-role asymmetry that degrades unseen-answer ranking. Furthermore, sparse early interactions exhibit both long-range dispersion and local redundancy under skewed distribution. To address these challenges, we propose INSPIRE (Integrating Shared Patterns and Individual Evidence for Reasoning), a two-stage forecasting framework over a known catalog. In Stage I, INSPIRE learns an availability-aware shared-pattern predictor that fuses event-updated memory across the full candidate catalog while applying coordinated query- and catalog-side masking. Subsequently, Stage II freezes this predictor and optimizes a lightweight individual-evidence adapter (EA) combining textual semantic priors with correlation-weighted long-range event statistics under support- and role-balanced co-answer supervision with prior-odds debiasing and soft residual alignment. In addition, we decompose evaluation by endpoint roles and track longitudinal emerging-answer cohorts via query-micro and answer-entity macro metrics. Across four benchmarks based on the ICEWS and GDELT event datasets, INSPIRE improves Emerging MRR by 16.73%-88.83% over the strongest baselines. Relative to paired Stage I predictors, EA further increases Emerging MRR by 8.28%-32.63%, Answer-new MRR by 7.81%-41.24%, and cohort macro MRR by 9.64%-39.94%, gains attributed to semantic priors and candidate-level score competition suppressing mismatched established competitors without zero-support prior shortcuts. The official code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A History-Aware Visually Grounded Critic for Computer Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11078)**  `arXiv:2606.11078`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Jaewoo Lee, Zaid Khan, Archiki Prasad, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Supriyo Chakraborty, Kartik Balasubramaniam, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Various test-time interventions for Computer Use Agents (CUAs), including critic models, have been developed to improve performance through pre-execution action evaluation in complex Graphical User Interface (GUI) environments. However, existing critics suffer from two key limitations: they (1) focus primarily on short-sighted decision loops (e.g., forgetting earlier actions) and (2) lack the visual grounding needed to detect flawed actions (e.g., clicking wrong UI elements). To address these, we introduce HiViG, a History-aware Visually Grounded test-time framework, built around a multimodal critic trained on real GUI trajectories to abstract past interactions into a compact record and to evaluate actions with visual grounding. At test time, HiViG integrates the critic into the policy decision loop to provide macro-action history, which summarizes the policy's completed achievements, and visually grounded critique, which verifies raw execution coordinates against the current screenshot to intercept errors before execution. Across web, mobile, and desktop benchmarks, HiViG consistently outperforms existing scalar and verbal critics, improving average success rates over the strongest baseline by 5.8% for Qwen3-VL-32B and 9.0% for Gemini-3-Flash, and demonstrates strong cross-platform generalization. Ablations show that macro-action history mitigates short-sighted planning and visually grounded critique reduces execution errors, with both components being critical for test-time scaling in long-horizon GUI tasks.
  </details>

- **[MedSkill-E$^{2}$: Evaluating and Evolving Skills for Medical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34250)**  `arXiv:2611.34250`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jingjing Hua, Shaocong Wei, Jianghan Shen, Junzhi Ning, Min Cen, Cheng Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills to access domain knowledge and complete real-world tasks. We observe that even when a skill specifies the rules and procedures needed for a task, agents may fail to apply them effectively during autonomous execution, a phenomenon we call skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). This issue is particularly consequential in medical tasks, where overlooking a single critical constraint can yield a plausible but incorrect result. To evaluate and address EBU, we introduce MedSkill-E$^{2}$, a framework for evaluating and evolving skills for medical agents. We construct EBU-Bench, a challenging benchmark covering 12 medically relevant task groups and associated skills, to evaluate agents' ability to use available skills autonomously. We quantify the EBU gap as the difference in skill-use accuracy between autonomous use and explicitly guided reading and application of the same skill. On EBU-Bench, all evaluated model-harness combinations exhibit EBU, with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points (pp). For the same model, skill-use accuracy under autonomous use differs by up to 38.96 pp across harnesses. Motivated by these observations, we propose EBU-Evolve, which uses execution feedback from autonomous and explicitly guided skill use to distill effective guidance into reusable skill instructions. Relative to their pre-evolution versions, the revised skills improve skill-use accuracy during autonomous execution by 33.33 to 55.17 pp and continue to yield improvements when transferred across multiple models and harnesses. Our results show that autonomous skill-use accuracy varies across harnesses with the model and skill held fixed, and model rankings can change. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened. EBU-Evolve improves autonomous skill-use accuracy on unseen tasks within the same skill, with gains transferring across models and harnesses.
  </details>

- **[SOLO: Towards Sample-Faithful and Detectable LLM Watermarking under Repeated Sampling via Content-Anchored Single-Owner Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34190)**  `arXiv:2611.34190`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongliang Guo, Yan Lin, Yifei Qian, Shuai Zhao, Luwei Xiao, Daizong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Providers watermark the outputs of large language models, while applications sample many responses per prompt for self-consistency, best-of-n selection, and uncertainty estimation. We show that deployed configurations of distortion-free watermarks, which deduplicate keyed contexts only within a response, couple the responses of a request: answer templates make all responses query the watermark with the same context before the answer, so collisions across responses, usually assumed rare, become the norm. Key pools reduce or avoid the coupling at the cost of multiple testing, and the exact remedy of a context history shared across the request leaves many positions unwatermarked. We propose SOLO, which keeps a request-scoped context history but anchors its keyed contexts on content selected by min-hash, so that siblings rarely compete for a context and random edits stay local. Its responses are jointly distributed as independent unwatermarked responses, each detectable with a single key. On five language models from three families and three benchmarks, SOLO preserves self-consistency and uncertainty estimation and, at a 1% false positive rate, detects more responses than four-token key pools on most tasks, also after randomly editing 10% of the tokens, and more than the context history on most clean tasks.
