Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Aligned Machine: A Benchmark for Evaluating Multimodal Embedding Similarity via Human Comparative Judgements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33594)**  `arXiv:2611.33594`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiechen Li, Hannah Groos, Brinnae Bent_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embedding models increasingly inform how people search, recommend, and retrieve information, yet we lack credible ways to test whether these similarity judgments reflect how humans understand meaning. Existing benchmarks typically rely on static, expert-labeled pairs with coarse Likert-style annotations, limiting scalability and making it difficult to obtain consistent, high-confidence annotations. We introduce Aligned Machine, an interactive, public-facing platform that transforms broad participation into a continuously growing Human-Aligned Similarity Benchmark (HASB) for evaluating semantic alignment in embedding-based systems. Participants complete a cognitively-natural, forced-choice task, comparing two pairs of items (text or images) and selecting which pair is more similar. This comparative design is accessible to non-experts, yields clean preference data, and supports robust aggregation. We report results from an initial deployment with 115 participants contributing 2,875 total labeled pairs and 872 unique pairs. Using HASB, we evaluated 12 widely used embedding models and found only partial alignment between embedding similarity and human judgments. Text-only models consistently achieve higher overall agreement than multimodal models, while multimodal performance varies systematically by task type: alignment is strongest for image-image comparisons, weaker for image-text comparisons, and lowest for text-text comparisons. Aligned Machine is designed to evolve over time as participation grows, supporting longitudinal analysis, domain-specific studies, bias detection, and measurement of concept drift across model versions. By combining accessible public participation with rigorous comparative evaluation, HASB provides a scalable and accountable framework for auditing semantic alignment in embedding models.
  </details>

- **[PeerReveal: Learning to Generalize in AI Peer Review Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33555)**  `arXiv:2611.33555`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zi Li, André V. Duarte, Lei Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can we distinguish fully AI-generated reviews from those using AI only to rewrite human judgement? Furthermore, can we do so reliably when reviews are generated from diverse AI models and workflows across scientific domains? To this end, we introduce PeerReveal, a new detector that grounds its decisions in learned relationships between a review's claims and reference claims drawn from AI-generated reviews of the same paper, complemented by review-level textual signals. We evaluate PeerReveal on PeerShift, a new, challenging test set of 5,430 reviews spanning multiple domains, including machine learning, life sciences, and social sciences, combining variation in disciplinary review conventions with diverse AI review-production mechanisms. With regular training, PeerReveal achieves 70.52% pooled Macro-F1 on this benchmark, improving over the strongest baseline by 11.48 points. We develop an effective and practical adversarial training framework that further improves the detector's generalization using a small local generator optimized to produce challenging reviews, raising pooled Macro-F1 from 70.52% to 82.40%. These results show that robust AI-review detection benefits from richer review-level evidence and adversarial training on more challenging generation processes.
  </details>

- **[Partial Routing: Trace-Tuned Delegation for Resource-Efficient Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33402)**  `arXiv:2611.33402`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Siddharth Arya, Kshitij Jain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI models vary enormously in capability and price. Model routing exploits this by sending easier tasks to cheaper models. However, a single agentic task often combines easy and difficult subtasks, making one model choice for the entire task inefficient. We introduce partial routing, which guides a frontier lead model to delegate suitable subtasks to a cheaper worker model. From both models' past runs, we identify recurring subtasks and measure each model's quality and cost on them. These measurements produce delegation guidance, with a parameter, $\delta$, controlling how much is delegated. The lead decides when and what to delegate during execution, without training either model. On held-out tasks from ViBench and Terminal-Bench 4.0, partial routing achieves higher mean task performance than the lead alone and task-level routing. With $\delta = 0.75$, it raises the lead's ViBench score from 70.2 to 78.5 at 19% lower cost, and its Terminal-Bench pass rate from 54% to 61% at similar cost. Giving the lead only the subtask breakdown, without a worker, also reduces cost on both benchmarks and raises the Terminal-Bench pass rate. These results show that analysis of past runs can support more efficient planning and more effective delegation, with little cost and implementation overhead.
  </details>

- **[MathArena: Evaluating LLMs on Uncontaminated Math Competitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23281)**  `arXiv:2505.23281`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Mislav Balunović, Jasper Dekoninck, Ivo Petrov, Nikola Jovanović, Martin Vechev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid advancement of reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs) has led to notable improvements on mathematical benchmarks. However, many of the most commonly used evaluation datasets (e.g., AIME 2024) are widely available online, making it difficult to disentangle genuine reasoning from potential memorization. Furthermore, these benchmarks do not evaluate proof-writing capabilities, which are crucial for many mathematical tasks. To address this, we introduce MathArena, a new benchmark based on the following key insight: recurring math competitions provide a stream of high-quality, challenging problems that can be used for real-time evaluation of LLMs. By evaluating models as soon as new problems are released, we effectively eliminate the risk of contamination. Using this framework, we find strong signs of contamination in AIME 2024. Nonetheless, evaluations on harder competitions, such as CMIMC 2025, demonstrate impressive reasoning capabilities in top-performing models. MathArena is also the first benchmark for proof-writing capabilities. On IMO 2025, top models achieve slightly less than 40%, demonstrating both notable progress and significant room for improvement. So far, we have evaluated over $50$ models across seven competitions, totaling $162$ problems. As an evolving benchmark, MathArena will continue to track the progress of LLMs on newly released competitions, ensuring rigorous and up-to-date evaluation of mathematical reasoning.
  </details>

- **[REE: A Scientific Research Entrance Exam for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34646)**  `arXiv:2611.34646`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zeqi Zhou, Minghao Liu, Jiarui Liu, Qingcheng Zeng, Rui Yang, Tianyu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous research agents are emerging as a promising approach to scientific problem-solving across disciplines, owing to their growing ability to improve a solution through repeated experiments. Evaluating this ability requires benchmarks that pose open-ended research problems and measure improvements over established scientific methods. We introduce REE (Research Entrance Exam), a benchmark that tests AI agents on scientific research. It consists of 100 open-ended research problems spanning five broad scientific areas and 23 domains. To ensure high quality, every problem is drawn from active research in its field and individually reviewed by domain experts. Each problem is posed as a task of iterative improvement: an agent has up to 12 hours to improve a working starter solution, and REE scores the result on a common scale against an established expert solution evaluated under comparable conditions. By this measure, two of the four frontier agents we evaluate, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol, outperform the expert solutions on 86% and 81% of problems, across all five scientific areas. Their runs also show how they do so: both agents surpass the expert solutions within the first two hours on average, and their best solutions mostly tailor or combine established methods of the field. REE thus provides a unified framework for evaluating autonomous scientific research, capturing both improvements over established methods and the research processes behind them.
  </details>

- **[EgoUseBench: Benchmarking Computer-Use Agents under Evolving First-Person Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34466)**  `arXiv:2611.34466`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yurun Chen, Biao Yi, Qihang Yu, Keting Yin, Shengyu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI wearables, such as smart glasses, extend the context available to agents beyond digital environments to users' physical surroundings. With these devices, computer tasks can originate from users' ongoing activities rather than solely from explicit instructions. Through first-person observations, agents can identify objects and activities relevant to user requests and ground digital tasks in the surrounding context. We call this interaction paradigm Ego Use. However, first-person observations are limited by the wearer's viewpoint and may provide incomplete or ambiguous evidence of ongoing activities. As new observations arrive, agents may need to revise their understanding of the physical context and user intent, and reassess whether prior digital actions remain valid. Whether current agents can reliably handle these changes remains insufficiently evaluated. We introduce EgoUseBench, a benchmark for evaluating Ego Use across three interaction settings with different levels of first-person context. Computer-Use relies on explicit instructions, Offline Ego Use provides prerecorded first-person context before execution, and Online Ego Use provides continuous observations as user intent evolves. Based on over 74 hours of real-world first-person recordings, we construct 693 tasks across the three interaction settings. To capture task evolution, we track first-person observations, user requirements, and website state separately. We further group tasks by how information changes during execution, resulting in three types: Graft (revised user requirements), Refresh (new first-person observations), and Clarify (agent-initiated information seeking). We evaluate advanced models and find that average success rates in Online Ego Use are 26.3% lower than in Computer-Use. This result reveals the difficulty of keeping task execution aligned with evolving first-person context and user intent.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Aviation Safety Knowledge Extraction with SmolLM2: From Zero-Shot to Retrieval-Grounded Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34432)**  `arXiv:2611.34432`  `cs.AI`  
  _Aziida Nanyonga, Mustafa Tahsin Yilmaz, Amir Hussain, Ugur Turhan, Graham Wild_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aviation accident narratives contain safety knowledge that is difficult to capture using structured occurrence fields alone. This study evaluates prompting-based knowledge extraction across four dimensions: concept accuracy, verbatim evidence grounding, structural compliance, and directed relation extraction. SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct was evaluated under zero-shot, one-shot, fixed few-shot, and retrieval-grounded prompting using 300 National Transportation Safety Board narratives from 2000-2020, containing 3,763 reference concepts and 467 directed relations. Evaluation combined structural checks, verbatim evidence matching, expert-reviewed semantic matching, and paired bootstrap confidence intervals. No prompting strategy performed best across all dimensions. Retrieval-grounded prompting achieved the highest concept micro-F1 of 32.27%, although its advantage over fixed few-shot prompting was uncertain (95% CI: -0.92 to 2.40 percentage points). One-shot prompting achieved the highest relation F1 of 6.95%, identifying directed relation extraction as the principal SmolLM2 bottleneck. Compared with fixed few-shot prompting, retrieval improved verbatim grounding (59.45% vs. 54.02%) but reduced complete structural compliance (33.3% vs. 42.7%). Verbatim matching establishes source presence rather than evidential relevance. An exploratory comparison using 35 benchmark narratives showed that Qwen3-8B achieved higher concept micro-F1 across all prompting conditions and higher relation F1 under demonstration-based prompting, reaching 51.37% and 34.62%, respectively, under one-shot prompting. The small subset limits generalization. These findings support evaluating semantic correctness, source traceability, structural reliability, and directed relation accuracy separately.
  </details>

- **[EvoLink-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Self-Evolving Agents in Long-Horizon Interdependent Task Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34400)**  `arXiv:2611.34400`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Chen, Xiaodong Lu, Weihao Shen, Gongxun Li, Xingyu Guo, Qihang Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are evolving from passive chatbots into autonomous systems that operate continuously in dynamic environments. Such agents need not only solve current tasks but also learn from past interactions to improve future performance. However, existing streaming benchmarks often rely on short task sequences or largely independent tasks, providing limited evidence of whether agents can effectively reuse experience when later tasks depend on earlier outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{EvoLink-Bench}, a benchmark for evaluating self-evolving agents in long-horizon, interdependent task streams. It covers daily office work, multi-turn dialogue, and AutoResearch-style workflows, with persistent workspaces and dependencies across tasks. The benchmark includes two complementary levels: \textbf{L1}, which evaluates the acquisition and revision of language-expressible knowledge, and \textbf{L2}, which assesses the development of task-level capabilities through execution and feedback. Its evaluation further distinguishes within-task recovery, persistent-state updates, and experience reuse in later tasks. We evaluate five agent frameworks across three backbone configurations under three persistence conditions, covering 19,635 scored task slots from selected evaluation cohorts. Results show that no framework consistently performs best across both levels, retries recover only a limited proportion of failed tasks, and persistent-state updates do not necessarily improve subsequent performance. Furthermore, the effects of evolution differ between knowledge- and capability-level tasks, suggesting that these two forms of evolution require different learning mechanisms. Performance gains also vary across domains and task streams, with improvements on some tasks not necessarily extending to others.
  </details>

- **[Crucible: Evaluating LLM Agents in Executable Chemistry Research Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34254)**  `arXiv:2611.34254`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yinjun Liu, Xinye Li, Ruisen Peng, Lang Gao, Zixiang Xu, Zirui Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) perform well on chemistry knowledge benchmarks, which mainly assess question answering and isolated tasks. Chemical research, however, requires using specialized tools and interpreting their outputs to obtain chemically valid results. To address this gap, we introduce Crucible Bench to evaluate this ability across all four stages of the design-make-test-analyze (DMTA) cycle. The benchmark contains 1,078 tasks in 12 task families curated from 18 published datasets, covering molecular design, synthesis planning, experimental execution, closed-loop optimization, simulation, and analytical characterization. Agents access 75 domain-specific tools through the Model Context Protocol in seven isolated environments. Sandboxed graders verify the resulting synthesis routes, experimental protocols, and other outputs. Laboratory tasks use robotic simulators or previously measured experimental data. We evaluate 14 LLMs as agents and find that the best achieves an overall score of 0.545, with synthesis planning and closed-loop optimization emerging as the hardest domains. Overall performance does not predict optimization performance well: the model ranked twelfth overall achieves the highest score in closed-loop optimization. Error analysis also shows that agents often submit chemically invalid results despite running their tools successfully. Stronger models benefit more from tool access, but reliable use of chemical software remains a challenge that Crucible Bench can track as models improve.
  </details>

- **[EGSE: Evidence-Grounded Skill Evolution for Medical Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33909)**  `arXiv:2611.33909`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mingyuan Li, Tong Jia, Ziyuan Yang, Wenbo Qi, Haoran Wang, Jie Gui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical reasoning agents combine foundation models with specialized tools to interpret medical images, yet reusing their historical experience remains challenging. A correct final answer can conceal flawed reasoning or tool use, synthesized advice may lack source support, and superficially similar cases may require different diagnostic procedures. In this paper, we propose EGSE, an evidence-grounded skill evolution framework that distills historical dialogues from similar cases into reusable conditional workflows and progressively refines a multi-faceted skill library through supported and validated updates to improve medical agents. EGSE evaluates intermediate reasoning and tool use and contrasts repeated successful and failed rollouts to extract useful practices and evidence-supported avoidance guidance. These experiences are synthesized into workflows with applicability conditions and checks traceable to source dialogues. Library additions and merges require source-grounded review and execution validation. At inference time, EGSE routes queries to skill nodes organized by imaging modality, question task type, and disease category, then retrieves and selects applicable workflows based on their conditions, the current images, and the question to guide evidence acquisition and interpretation. Experiments across four chest X-ray benchmarks show that EGSE outperforms representative medical agents and skill evolution methods in reasoning accuracy while reducing tool calls. It also delivers consistent accuracy gains across seven foundation models and supports skill transfer across datasets.
  </details>

- **[EATS: Scaling Tool-Use Experience for Medical Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33897)**  `arXiv:2611.33897`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mingyuan Li, Tong Jia, Haoran Wang, Wenbo Qi, Ziyuan Yang, Bowen Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical reasoning agents combine large language model (LLM) reasoning with clinical workflows and specialized neural tools. However, general-purpose LLMs receive limited training exposure to domain-specific medical tools, and the uncertainty and distributional shifts of neural tool outputs make selecting tools from simple descriptions significantly more challenging than in deterministic settings such as calculators or dictionary lookups. In this paper, we propose EATS, an experience-augmented tool selection strategy that leverages accumulated tool-use trajectories to improve tool selection in medical reasoning agents. EATS constructs a memory of past interactions, including queries, tool choices, arguments, and self-evaluated feedback, and retrieves relevant experiences to guide tool selection for new queries. As memory construction does not require ground-truth supervision, EATS scales naturally with test-time experience. We evaluate EATS on tool-augmented medical reasoning tasks, including closed-ended visual question answering and free-form report generation. Experiments on four public datasets, ChestAgentBench, CheXbench, MIMIC-CXR, and SLAKE-VQA, across three multimodal LLMs, Qwen3-VL 2B, Qwen3-VL 32B, and GPT-5.5, show consistent gains in tool selection behavior and downstream medical reasoning performance. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FORK: Co-Evolving Safe Disclosure and Decision Skills Against Attackers Who Read Every Refusal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33879)**  `arXiv:2611.33879`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hieu Man, Chao Shang, Rui Dong, Devang Kulshreshtha, Yanjun Qi, Sandesh Swamy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a guardrail rejects an LLM agent's action, its message reaches any attacker observing the trajectory: a fixed refusal leaves benign users unable to recover, whereas an explanation can tell the attacker what to try next. We study this as safe disclosure: explaining rejections so that authorized tasks recover without increasing adaptive attack success. Safe disclosure depends on both the decision and the message: what the guardrail blocks and what it reveals to the agent and the attacker. Because hand-written rules alone cannot anticipate how adaptive attackers probe decisions or use messages, we introduce FORK, which co-evolves a guardrail's natural-language decision and disclosure skills with an attacker that reads every public message. Each exploited message and permissive decision gives FORK evidence for a gradient-free revision of its natural-language skill library. We evaluate FORK on four benchmarks. Averaged over three agents on FORK-World, FORK cuts attack success rate (ASR) under adaptive attack from 78.4% to 14.1% and keeps utility above 85%, outperforming baselines on both ASR and utility. Notably, on MT-AgentRisk, whose environments and tasks are unseen in training, FORK lowers ASR from 70.6% to 9.1% with 93.1% benign utility.
  </details>

- **[ExtractBench Pro: The Long Tail of Document Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33791)**  `arXiv:2611.33791`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boyang Zhang, Justin Xu, Adrian Lyjak, Eli Stewart, Simon Suo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present ExtractBench Pro, a schema-guided document extraction benchmark that tests six challenging modes of extraction underrepresented in existing datasets. It is created following a framework for synthetic document generation, in which deterministic document generators assemble challenging components into documents that are designed to be difficult at a level calibrated for frontier systems. We synthesize 100 realistic documents spanning a diverse range of styles. On this data, we evaluate ten coding agents and eight extraction API configurations for accuracy, grounding, and their ability to perfectly extract documents. No extraction system extracts more than 61% of documents error-free. The strongest general-purpose coding agents, Claude Code Opus 5.5 and Codex GPT-6 Astra, outperform specialized extraction APIs on both perfect-document rate and Value F1, but at a higher cost per page. Among specialized extraction APIs, LlamaExtract spans the lower-cost frontier, with Agentic Plus Max Effort approaching the strongest coding agent on both perfect-document rate and Value F1 at lower cost per page. Our analysis of coding agent traces identifies intelligent image processing and programmatic reasoning, but also failures in applying document-specific definitions and recovering relationships from diagrams. We release ExtractBench Pro and these evaluation results as resources for the future development of improved frontier extraction systems.
  </details>

- **[TransHarness: From Benchmark Overfitting to Transferable Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33757)**  `arXiv:2611.33757`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianze Xia, Zimo Wang, Heng Tong, Zhaoxiang Liu, Shiguo Lian, Fan Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness evolution offers a path to improving coding agents without updating model weights. However, existing methods can overfit their evolution benchmarks, producing gains that fail to carry over to new tasks. To address this challenge, we introduce Transferable Harness Evolution (TransHarness), which evolves harness intervention policies from same-task success-failure contrasts. These policies couple execution-context triggers with diagnostic guidance, enabling task-dependent checks and corrections beyond the source executions. Cross-task replay and independent semantic selection retain policies that intervene selectively and ground corrections in current-task evidence rather than source-specific solutions. We further curate an independent evolution set for cross-distribution transfer and conduct a cross-benchmark evaluation covering multiple strong models and a range of lightweight and mature harnesses. Experiments show that, with only 300 source trajectories from outside the test distribution, TransHarness further improves strong agents across distinct target benchmarks, demonstrating transferable harness evolution beyond benchmark overfitting. Specifically, TransHarness achieves state-of-the-art scores of 76.4% on DeepSWE v1.1 and 95.5% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, surpassing the strongest baseline model, GPT-6 Astra, by 2.4 and 5.6 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[JOVE ICE: Intent Guided Cloud Edge Device Collaboration for AI Glasses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33735)**  `arXiv:2611.33735`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuyang Jin, Bingfeng Pi, Jiamei Sun, Wei Jin, Dongyang Wang, Yunpeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and LLM based agents have expanded the capabilities of AI glasses, enabling egocentric visual question answering, memory retrieval, and proactive assistance. These applications involve diverse inputs, including speech, images, and video, and range from simple device control to complex visual reasoning, posing challenges for efficient computation and communication. While video centric capabilities have received considerable attention, simple text based and device executable tasks remain underexplored despite their prevalence in daily interactions. Efficiently supporting diverse workloads within a unified assistant remains an open challenge. We propose JOVE ICE, an intent guided cloud, edge, and device collaborative framework that routes requests among AI glasses, a paired smartphone, and cloud servers according to user intent. JOVE ICE integrates hierarchical intent classification with dynamic subagent orchestration to select efficient execution paths and flexibly incorporate new applications through specialized subagents. Analysis of real world AI glasses requests reveals that simple device control tasks account for over 30% of interactions, which JOVE ICE handles directly on device to avoid unnecessary cloud computation and costs. Evaluations on three public benchmarks demonstrate that JOVE ICE reduces time to first token by approximately 13% and token usage by over 11% compared with conventional device and cloud architectures, while maintaining task quality. These results demonstrate the potential of intent guided collaboration to improve the efficiency and scalability of AI glasses.
  </details>

- **[Asset4D-Bench: Benchmarking Coding Agents for Editable 3D and 4D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33728)**  `arXiv:2611.33728`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chengyi Sun, Deyuan Liu, Lei Zhao, Jungang Li, Qi Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A cart can follow its reference trajectory while its cargo floats above the deck. Asset4D-Bench tests whether coding agents recover objects, motion and interactions from visual references as editable Blender scenes. Its 114 conditions span image editing, video reconstruction with matched full or partial starter assets, and independently selected from-scratch tasks. We independently reopen projects and measure them against native reference states. The 228 archived scores include original delivery outcomes and versioned measures of scene quality. On 24 from-scratch tasks, each model earns full dynamics credit on 14. Of these, three GPT-6.1-Sol and eight GPT-6-Astra reconstructions earn zero relation points. Under the fixed scoring weights, relations contribute 5.15 points to the 6.50-point Sol-minus-Astra difference in mean recovered fidelity. Differences in execution and recovery policy across archived runs preclude a causal estimate of starter-asset effects.
  </details>

- **[KeyDeck: Multi-Paper-to-Slide Generation via Cross-Paper Synthesis and Reference-Guided Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33715)**  `arXiv:2611.33715`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tarik Can Ozden, Sachidanand VS, Ram Prakash Reddy Motati, Ozgur Kara, Dilek Hakkani-Tür, Junho Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-paper-to-slide generation (multi-PTSG) is the task of constructing a unified presentation from multiple research papers by selecting, organizing, and synthesizing information across sources. This task introduces two main challenges: (i) combining multiple sources into a coherent narrative requires identifying cross-paper relationships and synthesizing their content, and (ii) the desired organization and emphasis varies substantially across presenters, making personalization essential for user satisfaction. Motivated by these challenges, we propose KeyDeck, the first multi-PTSG framework that integrates per-paper and cross-paper analysis to identify relationships among sources and synthesize their contributions into a coherent narrative, and reference-guided personalization to infer presenters' narrative, content, textual, and visual preferences and adapt slide generation accordingly. To evaluate generation quality, personalization adherence, and conversational goal satisfaction, we introduce KeyBench, a benchmark of 100 expert-authored multi-paper presentations, together with a comprehensive evaluation framework. Our results demonstrate that KeyDeck outperforms existing approaches in multi-PTSG quality, personalization adherence, and conversational goal satisfaction.
  </details>

- **[PowerFix: Physics-Guided Iterative Repair for LLM-Assisted Power Converter Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33712)**  `arXiv:2611.33712`  `cs.AI`  
  _Can Wang, Shi Qiu, Fanfan Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly being explored for automated engineering design. However, repairing power-converter designs that fail engineering validation remains challenging: the system must identify physical causes, locate responsible design variables, and resolve violations without compromising other constraints. We introduce PowerFix, a physics-guided iterative repair framework that converts validation failures into structured diagnoses and executable patches while preserving topology and design requirements. PowerDiagGraph links failed checks to possible physical causes and constrains repair routes. An LLM prioritizes these routes, while a deterministic compiler translates selected repairs into authorized field-level edits. Candidates are validated on isolated design copies and committed only after all required engineering checks pass; failed validation can guide replanning. On our constructed 96-task flyback repair benchmark, comprising injected single faults, coupled faults, and held-out natural failures from our design generator, PowerFix achieves 78.7% verified repair success rate (VRSR), compared with 30.1% for the strongest of three LLM baselines. Three additional replanning rounds increase VRSR to 88.4%. These offline results provide pilot-stage evidence within a simulation-based design workflow, with hardware prototype validation and transfer to a second converter topology as the next steps toward deployment.
  </details>

- **[Faster and Better Query (FBQ): Learning Future-Position Probes from the First Generated Token](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33679)**  `arXiv:2611.33679`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiankun Zou, Daixing Chen, Tongtong Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a large language model (LLM) evicts prompt keys and values at the end of prefill, it must decide what to keep before it sees the queries from subsequent decoding steps. Generating a draft continuation can approximate those queries at the cost of extra autoregressive steps. Can we use the embedding of the first output token, already available from the final prefill logits, to construct low-cost pseudo queries that approximate future decoding queries? We start with FTQ-8, which places eight copies of the first token ID at consecutive future positions and reads their attention to the prompt in one causal forward pass. Each copy sees a different prefix, but a generic opening such as the offers a poor literal guess for seven successive tokens. Faster and Better Query (FBQ) preserves the first embedding and learns an additive offset at each of the seven remaining positions. We train these offsets to predict the average prompt attention of a full-cache continuation. On LongBench, FBQ performs comparably to eight-step Lookahead Q-Cache at the same cache budget, using a single forward pass to compute all probe queries.
  </details>

- **[Interactive Proofs as a Unified Framework for Evaluating and Leveraging Verifiable Proof Generation by LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33676)**  `arXiv:2611.33676`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yankai Shu, Xingyu Zhao, Chuan Mao, Congchi Yin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in LLM-assisted theorem proving and program verification have attracted widespread attention. Beyond determining whether a proposition is true, LLM agents must produce evidence that can be independently checked by humans or formal verification tools. To evaluate the underlying, protocol-agnostic ability of LLM agents to construct verifiable proofs-rather than their proficiency with any particular proof language or verification tool, we introduce Merlin-Bench. Drawing on interactive proof systems from computational complexity theory, we cast an LLM agent as the prover: under a designated protocol, the agent interacts with a formal verifier to substantiate its claimed answer to a problem instance. We develop an automated framework for constructing test cases and use it to create 100 cases across 15 problem families, each formulated within this prover-verifier framework. Experiments with 10 frontier LLMs across multiple agent harnesses show that reliably producing verifiable proofs in accordance with specified protocols remains a substantial challenge. Our case studies further provide actionable insights into improving agents' ability to generate verifier-accepted proofs: tool use is essential for completing practical proof tasks; different forms of guidance, ranging from engineering instructions to problem-specific knowledge, substantially affect success rates on interactive-proof tasks; and proof-solving experience distilled from abstract tasks can transfer to concrete, real-world verification tasks.
  </details>

- **[What, Who, and When: Structured Harm Assessment for Mental Health Safety via Short Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33263)**  `arXiv:2611.33263`  `cs.AI`  
  _Catarina Botelho, António Farinhas, John Mendonça, Areti Vassilopoulos, Inês Gonçalves, Maya D'Eon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growing use of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support raises important safety concerns. Existing guard models primarily identify categories of harmful content, often overlooking the context needed to determine an appropriate response. We introduce a clinically grounded taxonomy that characterizes harm along three dimensions: what the harm is, who is involved, and when it occurs. It further separates harm characterization from intervention decisions, thus enabling safety policies to be specified outside the model weights through explicit, clinician-curated instructions that can be updated and audited. We validate the taxonomy with expert clinicians and validate a panel of LLM judges to scale annotation. Relabeling public benchmarks in this taxonomy shows that they rarely contain harm directed at the user, which accounts for nearly half of the harm in real conversations. We then train MindGuard2, a 35B-A3B reasoning-based guard model using supervised fine-tuning on short reasoning traces followed by reinforcement learning with a reasoning-length penalty, to minimize inference overhead while preserving the benefits of reasoning. On two public benchmarks and real-world conversations, MindGuard2 achieves balanced accuracy comparable to the substantially larger GLM-5.3 at approximately 10x lower median latency, advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier. Furthermore, it achieves low false-positive rates at high recall, helping balance the need to detect unsafe situations against the risk of unnecessary interventions that may disrupt support and undermine user trust. We release a safety guardrail model with SOTA performance trained on synthetic data, together with synthetic conversations and annotations of public benchmarks in our taxonomy.
  </details>

- **[AutoformBench: A Lean Formalization Benchmark for Advanced Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33165)**  `arXiv:2611.33165`  `cs.AI`  
  _Charles Arnal, Ahmad Rammal, Niket Patel, Fabian Gloeckle, Bartosz Piotrowski, Amaury Hayat, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems are increasingly capable of producing machine-checked Lean proofs of advanced mathematical results. Real mathematical formalization, however, is not isolated theorem proving: it requires building the definitions and intermediate lemmas that connect a result to a formal library. Existing evaluations often provide isolated theorem statements in settings where much of this supporting theory is already available in Mathlib. We introduce AutoformBench, a benchmark of 341 Lean 4 formalization tasks drawn from 24 advanced mathematical sources. Lean-experienced mathematicians formalized and audited each target statement, leaving the corresponding Lean proof to the evaluated agent. Each problem is embedded in a broader mathematical development: agents receive the informal proof but must translate it into Lean while constructing the missing definitions, intermediate lemmas, and supporting infrastructure. We compare the performance of frontier models on these problems. We find a largely shared difficulty frontier: success depends less on mathematical domain than on models' ability to construct missing formal infrastructure. Data and code atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Constraint-Aware Test-Time Scoring for LLM and VLM Candidate Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33161)**  `arXiv:2611.33161`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rangarajan Ramesh, Yongchao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an LLM or VLM produces several candidate answers, picking the best one usually relies on the model's own confidence, a learned judge model, or a domain check. Each has a known weakness: an answer can be fluent but insecure, mathematically plausible but physically infeasible, or rated highly by a judge while quietly breaking a task requirement. We propose a scoring framework that combines a small set of hard checks every answer must pass with a wider set of softer checks whose scores are blended into one number per candidate. This adaptive scoring framework consists of three components: a drafting model that produces the candidates, a configuring model that selects and weights the checks for each task, and scoring models that rate properties no grounded tool can compute. We apply our proposed method to four tasks: writing code that is both correct and secure (CWEval), generating robot manipulation plans (LIBERO with added safety variants), passing competitive-programming hidden tests (LiveCodeBench), and answering questions from retrieved documents (HotpotQA). An ablation study isolates the contribution of each component and finds that the largest gains come from combining hard checks with candidate comparison: on tasks where the first sampled answer is often broken or unsafe, this pairing lifts joint correctness-and-safety by up to +0.21 (retrieval-augmented QA) and code-generation safety by up to +0.28 (CWEval), with the size of the gain tracking how weak the first sample is, falling to near zero or slightly negative where it is already strong. The per-task layer that selects and weights the softer checks adds no measurable value over simpler alternatives. This builds a unified scoring framework, together with an ablation that identifies the grounded hard checks, not the soft-factor configuration, as the source of the gain.
  </details>

- **[Learning World Models through Contrastive Identification of Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32776)**  `arXiv:2611.32776`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hayeong Lee, Youngjun Suh, Byung-Jun Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models enable goal-directed planning from offline visual data, but prediction alone does not specify which action-related distinctions their representations should preserve. Inverse dynamics provides auxiliary supervision, yet squared-error action regression rewards additional information only through changes in the conditional action mean. We propose Contrastive Identification of Actions (CIA), which identifies the executed action among alternatives sampled from a history-conditioned behavior proposal. Comparing actions plausible in the same context targets information supplied by the next latent beyond history. Under exact behavior matching, we show that CIA can reward action information that leaves optimal inverse-regression error unchanged, and verify this distinction through a controlled learning example. CIA achieves higher planner-averaged mean success than the base world model and a regression inverse-dynamics baseline across five visual control benchmarks, without modifying the planner.
  </details>

- **[EffectWAM: Learning Action-Grounded Effects for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32053)**  `arXiv:2611.32053`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuan Zhang, Hao Cui, Jingfei Ni, Meiyi Wang, Xin Nie, Chi Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models connect visual prediction with executable robot control. We present EffectWAM, which develops this connection through three complementary components: Effect, camera positional encoding (CaPE), and Action Grounding. Effect represents visual change as the difference between independently encoded future observations and a shared current-observation anchor. CaPE conditions endpoint pooling on relative camera pose when reliable extrinsics are available, while sharing the same learned parameters with pose-free pooling for uncalibrated trajectories. Action Grounding trains the visual representation to retain measured changes in end-effector pose and gripper state. An exponential moving average of the observation encoder supplies Effect targets. A shared generation expert learns to jointly predict Effect and an executable action chunk through flow matching, and reuses the sampled pair to generate future video on demand. This allows the controller to execute actions without waiting for video synthesis. We train on more than 20,000 hours of heterogeneous embodied data, excluding unused chain-of-thought data, and accommodate dataset-specific joint, end-effector, and hand-pose representations. Our evaluation uses the Small (8B) configuration on all five benchmarks and additionally evaluates the Large (26B) configuration on RoboDojo. Experiments examine closed-loop manipulation across complementary benchmarks, and comparisons across multiple baseline policies show consistent improvements from Action Grounding.
  </details>

- **[Reproducible by Seed? Not on GPUs: Batch-Dependent Non-Determinism in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30545)**  `arXiv:2611.30545`  `cs.AI`  
  _Javier Conde, Xiuyuan Qi, Shanshan Liu, Pedro Reviriego_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In text to image AI models, fixing the prompt and the random seed is widely assumed to fix the image. We show that it does not on GPUs. We evaluate six text-to-image models (diffusion and autoregressive) under four numerical precisions and quantizations on three GPU types (RTX A6000, RTX 6000 Ada, and H100 NVL), across up to 100 prompts per set for all six models. For each configuration we compare, with the prompt and seed fixed, the image generated alone with the image generated inside a batch, and we also compare across GPUs. Every replay control reported here is bit-exact, so the variation is not random noise: the execution setting, mainly the batch composition and the GPU, decides the output. The effect is large and model dependent. Structural similarity between the single-image and the batched output falls to 0.580 for an autoregressive model in bf16 and to 0.551 with int8. For a second autoregressive model in bf16 it falls to 0.261 with sampled decoding and to 0.229 with greedy decoding, and for an fp8 diffusion model to 0.68. A diffusion model in bf16 stays close to the batch-1 output (0.96-0.98) but changes every image. In most cases the batched image is a different but equally valid image; in some, such as prompts with digits and counters, it is visibly worse. Cross-GPU differences are of the same order as the batch effect: for one autoregressive model in bf16 compared across two GPU architectures (RTX 6000 Ada versus RTX A6000), no image is bit-identical and the cross-GPU LPIPS (0.355 to 0.383) matches the batch effect within one GPU (0.347 to 0.381). The consequences are direct: seeds do not make results reproducible, benchmarks and image-quality comparisons that mix batch sizes or hardware can be biased, and safety audits and A/B tests on generated images need the serving setup reported.
  </details>

- **[Learning Heterogeneous Reasoning in Large Language Models: From Mode Compatibility to Local Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30281)**  `arXiv:2611.30281`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sunbowen Li, Dandan Song, Shengkun Ma, Xuanning Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training large language models routinely pools reasoning trajectories from diverse teachers or algorithms, yet it remains unclear whether standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can learn such heterogeneous strategies without explicit mode conditioning. We study the learnability and local bottlenecks of heterogeneous reasoning through a formal mixture formulation and controlled experiments. We first formalize the representational compatibility of the optimal autoregressive model with latent reasoning mixtures: the target next-token distribution weights modes by their prefix posteriors and concentrates on a single mode once alternative modes assign negligible likelihood to the prefix. In a verifiable symbolic graph-search environment with paired breadth-first and depth-first search trajectories, no consistent disadvantage is observed for unlabeled mixed SFT relative to homogeneous or labeled baselines under matched token budgets while preserving both modes. Scaling across 0.6B-14B models and data budgets largely eliminates global mode confusion, shifting the primary bottleneck to local state-tracking transitions. Because a local mistake affects the final outcome only when subsequent reasoning is sensitive to that step, we find that only a small fraction of local errors are outcome-determining Critical Reasoning Nodes (CRNs), and that single-step repairs at these nodes rescue failing trajectories across symbolic search and natural language benchmarks whenever the model has sufficient downstream competence to complete the remaining derivation. Our environment is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoPILOT: A Visual Agent Harness for Real-Time Gameplay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28202)**  `arXiv:2611.28202`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mark Zhao, Pengcheng Wang, Rui Yang, Jiarui Yao, Xirui Jiang, Yuxi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal foundation models can play video games well when the game waits for them, but often fail when it keeps running. We find that much of this gap lies in the harness, which decides what the model sees, what it remembers, and how its outputs become actions over time. Existing harnesses such as VISTA improve the first two, yet turn each model call into at most one action, so the agent acts only as often as its model thinks. We introduce AutoPILOT, which changes the third: in each model call, the model can send a primitive action, start an open-loop macro, or write a closed-loop controller that keeps playing from the screen without further model calls until it hands control back. To evaluate agents in real-time games, we build AFK-Bench, 120 diverse browser games grouped by how they respond to inaction and playable in real time or with the clock paused. With GPT-6.1 Sol under the same budgets, AutoPILOT reaches 68.6% real-time success against 38.3% for VISTA, and on GameWorld it keeps 80.0% in real time while VISTA drops from 84.1% to 42.9%. Its gains hold across closed and open-source models, and it reaches VISTA's final success rate at a fifth of its API cost. Our results suggest that how a harness turns model outputs into actions over time is a central yet underexplored part of agent design, and we hope AutoPILOT and AFK-Bench will serve as a foundation for agents in a world that does not wait.
  </details>

- **[Can Vision-Language Models Synthesize Executable Game-Playing Programs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27741)**  `arXiv:2611.27741`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yao Tong, Jiaxin Qi, Dingyu Zhang, Jianqiang Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in vision-language models (VLMs) have endowed multimodal agents with increasingly strong visual perception and long-horizon reasoning capabilities, making VLM-based autonomous game playing feasible. Existing game benchmarks primarily evaluate whether an agent can select appropriate actions from the current visual state across different scenarios and games. However, most of them adopt a step-by-step interaction paradigm where the current visual observation is sent to the agent before each action. In practical deployment, this paradigm introduces two fundamental limitations: the game loop must wait for model inference, making real-time interaction impractical, and repeated VLM calls incur substantial cost. Therefore, we propose PlayScriptBench, a script-based game benchmark that evaluates whether VLMs can synthesize an executable game-playing program from game states. This setting jointly evaluates visual grounding, understanding of game dynamics, long-horizon strategic reasoning, and executable program synthesis, providing a more comprehensive assessment of game-playing agents. To support more tractable evaluation for current general-purpose VLMs, we further introduce a four-stage pipeline --- Explore, Perception, API Construction, and Strategy Composition --- that decomposes controller synthesis into game exploration, state perception, interface construction, and strategic control, enabling agents to progressively construct an executable program. Experiments on 16 games spanning 4 genres show that even strong VLMs struggle on our benchmark, revealing a substantial gap between passive visual game understanding and executable closed-loop game control. These results establish script-based controller synthesis as a complementary direction for benchmarking VLM-based game-playing agents.
  </details>

- **[RSI-PDEbench: A Benchmark for Self-Improving Agents in PDE Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14949)**  `arXiv:2611.14949`  `cs.AI`  
  _Binghang Lu, Lang Cao, Changhong Mou, Yige Sun, Cindy Xiangrui Kong, Zheyuan Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying and solving partial differential equations (PDEs) from sampled solution data often requires substantial expert effort in equation discovery, coefficient estimation, and numerical solver configuration, especially under noise and distribution shifts. Can large language model (LLM) agents automate this workflow accurately and efficiently? To investigate this question, we introduce RSI-PDEbench, a benchmark of 1,001 cases spanning 42 equation templates, including both classical and fractional PDEs. It provides three evaluation settings: Full evaluates performance on noise-free solution data; Noisy tests robustness to Gaussian noise with an RMS equal to 50\% of the clean-signal RMS; and Hard-OOD tests challenging out-of-distribution generalization to unseen equation templates and shifted coefficient ranges. We further introduce RSI-PDE, a recursively self-improving agent harness that shortlists candidate equations by residual loss, uses relative $L^2$ feedback to refine coefficients and numerical solvers, and applies a validation gate to policy and memory updates. We compare RSI-PDE with single-run baselines and native agent harnesses (Claude Code or Codex) across four frontier models. Across the three settings, RSI-PDE improves the pass rate averaged across the four models by 106.5--181.9\% relative to single-run baselines and by 3.0--19.6\% relative to native harnesses, while reducing total recorded model calls by 46.9\% across the 12 model--setting pairs. These results highlight the potential of validated recursive self-improvement in LLM agents to advance scientific computing.
  </details>

- **[The WoW: A Large-Scale World Knowledge Corpus for Full-Lifecycle LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13487)**  `arXiv:2611.13487`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianze Sun, Yongyao Duan, Sam Chen, Yuanjian Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Chinese educational corpora often suffer from fragmented coverage and a lack of systematic organization. In this paper, we propose WoW (\textbf{Wo}rld Kno\textbf{w}ledge), a large-scale dataset designed for the full-cycle training of foundation models. The construction of this dataset is driven by an Educational Knowledge Graph, which serves as a taxonomic backbone for the systematic collection and completion of missing educational modules across all levels. Compared to previous versions, this iteration provides enhanced systematicity, superior quality, and a larger scale, while incorporating multimodal signals and supporting every stage of model development. Experiments demonstrate that WoW significantly improves model performance across diverse benchmarks. To facilitate community research, we further provide an interactive platform for graph-based data selection and retrieval.
  </details>

- **[OwlSight: A Panoramic Monitor for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23670)**  `arXiv:2610.23670`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lewen Yan, Yanxu Zhu, Jie Zhang, Jing Shao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring offers actionable evidence for oversight AI systems, with the potential to enable early detection and intervention. However, existing approaches are focused on specific tasks, whereas the regulation of AI, particularly reasoning models, requires monitoring a broader range of tasks. We introduce Factor for CoT monitoring, a strategy that supports customizing monitoring requirements in the prompt, which enables a single monitor to cover different tasks by relying on different factors. Furthermore, we introduce two types of factors, fine-grained and coarse-grained (e.g., monitoring whether a specific email address has been leaked versus monitoring privacy leakage), to accommodate diverse user monitoring needs. Based on this, we develop a CoT monitor named OwlSight that relies solely on factor to monitor behaviors related to multiple dimensions, including capability, safety, and trustworthiness. Experiments across 15,971 reasoning trajectories show that OwlSight surpasses general-purpose LLMs by 1.32-30.57 and 11.71-27.81 points at fine and coarse granularity, respectively, and achieves state-of-the-art results on OpenAI's CoT monitorability benchmark. It also generalizes to unseen models' CoTs and remains robust to Factor rephrasings, CoT compression, and obfuscation, offering a promising paradigm for CoT monitoring that can contribute to the oversight of frontier AI.
  </details>

- **[ACTR: Aligning Thoughts and Responses for Multilingual Safety in Reasoning LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37054)**  `arXiv:2609.37054`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xianhui Zhang, Jian Yu, Chengyu Xie, Chenhang Cui, Shuyi Miao, Pengyang Shao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensuring the safety of reasoning large language models (LLMs) across languages is essential for their reliable deployment. However, when exposed to jailbreak attacks in non-high-resource languages, these models may generate unsafe responses even when their reasoning traces identify safety risks. To address this issue, we propose aligning cross-lingual thoughts and responses (ACTR), a framework that improves multilingual safety alignment by strengthening the use of existing safety reasoning. Specifically, we first present the think gap score (TGS) to compare the normalized contributions of reasoning traces to attention outputs during response generation across languages, and use reasoning- trace substitution to measure the cross-lingual safety gap. Next, using a corpus of jailbreak queries, we assess neuron importance through changes in response representations caused by neuron masking and compare the high-importance neuron sets obtained with reasoning enabled and disabled to identify safety think neurons that support the use of safety reasoning. Finally, we devise neuron-selective consistency optimization (NSCO), which uses a frozen judge model to reward agreement between the safety categories of reasoning traces and responses while updating only the parameters associated with the selected neurons, requiring no human-annotated responses or preference data. Across two reasoning models, ACTR achieves lower average attack success rates than the evaluated state-of-the-art methods on AdvBench-X and MultiJail, with safety gains extending to unseen languages, while preserving or improving average performance on multilingual knowledge and mathematical reasoning tasks and limiting false refusals of benign requests. Warning: this paper contains examples with unsafe content.
  </details>

- **[iBrain: A Unified Foundation Model Reading the Brain from Surface to Spikes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06960)**  `arXiv:2609.06960`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ying Chen, Tiou Wang, Zhifeng Yue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Invasive neural recordings provide high-fidelity measurements of brain activity, with signals such as intracranial EEG (iEEG) and intracortical spiking activity capturing neural dynamics at different spatial and temporal scales. Yet existing neural foundation models have largely been developed independently for different invasive recording paradigms, leaving joint pretraining across heterogeneous invasive signals underexplored. In this work, we introduce iBrain, a unified foundation model that jointly learns from iEEG and spiking activity. iBrain employs signal-specific encoders to accommodate their distinct signal characteristics and a shared spatiotemporal Transformer backbone to model dependencies across recording channels and time. We pretrain iBrain on over 7,000 hours of heterogeneous neural recordings using masked signal reconstruction and channel-view alignment, promoting contextual modeling of neural dynamics and robustness across different channels. iBrain consistently outperforms single-signal pretraining baselines and achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks. Further experiments demonstrate that iBrain exhibits transferability and data efficiency across diverse recording settings. These results highlight the potential of joint pretraining on heterogeneous invasive neural recordings to support scalable neural modeling and transferable representations across recording settings and downstream tasks.
  </details>

- **[Fisher-R1: Training LLM Agents for Reliable Hypothesis Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07437)**  `arXiv:2608.07437`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiacheng Miao, Jin Mu, Guanhua Chen, James Zou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable hypothesis testing is the foundation of many empirical scientific claims. Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to automate this process, as they can inspect datasets, generate code, and produce analyses end-to-end. However, we show that they frequently make subtle inferential errors that lead to incorrect conclusions despite correctly executed analyses. Existing benchmarks fail to capture this failure mode, as they rarely assess whether a reported p-value is statistically valid given the assumptions underlying the data. We address this gap by building P-Bench, a benchmark comprising 425 open-ended, realistic hypothesis-testing tasks spanning economics, biology, and medicine. Each task requires an agent to select a statistical method, compute a p-value, and draw a conclusion given only a scientific hypothesis and a dataset. We further introduce Fisher-R1, an open-weight LLM agent trained for rigorous hypothesis testing using synthetic tasks and reinforcement learning. On P-Bench, Fisher-R1-14B substantially improves over its backbone and outperforms strong proprietary and open-source baselines, including GPT-5.4 and DeepSeekV4-Pro, achieving a 21% average relative improvement in single-trial success over DeepSeek-V4-Pro, with gains up to 26% on the most challenging tasks. Our results demonstrate that current LLM agents lack reliable statistical reasoning for hypothesis testing and that reinforcement learning on tasks with verified statistical reward substantially improves reliability.
  </details>

- **[Benchmark Everything Everywhere All at Once](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06462)**  `arXiv:2606.06462`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shiyun Xiong, Dongming Wu, Peiwen Sun, Yuang Ai, Bokang Yang, Wencheng Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks provide standardized measures for evaluating and advancing large language models (LLMs) and multimodal LLMs (MLLMs). As models improve, existing benchmarks can approach saturation, making it harder to challenge models and distinguish among them. While reusing annotated datasets can reduce data collection and labeling effort, constructing new benchmarks remains labor-intensive, and existing agent-based approaches often target specific tasks or automate only parts of the process. To address these challenges, we introduce BenchHarness, an autonomous harness for end-to-end benchmark customization. It coordinates multiple agents and tools to analyze user requirements, search for suitable datasets, and construct validated benchmarks, with support for refinement based on model performance. To evaluate BenchHarness, we construct 16 benchmarks spanning diverse tasks and modalities. Human review and LLM-as-a-judge assessments indicate high benchmark quality, while evaluations across multiple models reveal challenging tasks and meaningful performance differences. In comparisons with source benchmarks, the generated benchmarks yield lower scores and larger performance gaps, even when the originals approach saturation. BenchHarness thus provides a reusable approach to benchmark construction, supporting informative evaluation as model capabilities and user needs evolve. Our code and benchmarks are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Towards Faithful Agentic XAI: A Verification Method and an Open-World Benchmark for Better Model Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27879)**  `arXiv:2605.27879`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jaechang Kim, Sunung Mun, Seungjoon Lee, Jaewoong Cho, Jungseul Ok_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explainable AI (XAI) helps users interpret model behavior and identify potential faults. Agentic XAI systems use Large Language Models (LLMs) to make explanations more accessible through natural-language interaction, but they can also produce plausible yet unfaithful explanations. This risk arises because unreliable XAI outputs for complex models can be amplified by LLMs and mislead users. We propose Faithful Agentic XAI (FAX), a verification method that improves the faithfulness of Agentic XAI explanations. FAX decomposes draft explanations into claims and tests them with inherently faithful tools that query the target model directly, filtering unsupported or contradicted claims before final generation. We also introduce CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, an open-world reinforcement learning benchmark with complex policies, diverse goals, and challenging scenarios for assessing model-specific faithfulness. On CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, FAX improves simulation faithfulness from 0.20 for the strongest baseline to 0.46 while maintaining high informativeness, relevance, and fluency. On three tabular benchmarks, FAX performs competitively with prior Agentic XAI baselines, but our analysis shows that these settings can conflate task accuracy with model-specific faithfulness. These findings show that explicit verification is essential for faithful Agentic XAI and that faithfulness benchmarks must be designed to test explanations against the behavior of the target model itself.
  </details>

- **[Personalize-then-Store: Benchmarking and Learning Personalized Memory for Long-horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25535)**  `arXiv:2605.25535`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yeonjun In, Wonjoong Kim, Sangwu Park, Kanghoon Yoon, Chanyoung Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing large language model (LLM) based memory systems apply universal, static policies that overlook a fundamental reality: the contexts that are worth storing in memory are different across users. This misalignment wastes limited memory budget on transient interactions while failing to preserve critical context for longhorizon tasks. To address this gap, we investigate an underexplored question: can LLM based memory systems learn personalized memory policies? We introduce PerMemBench, the first benchmark that jointly models multi domain interaction histories and cross user heterogeneity in agent use patterns to evaluate personalized memory systems. We further present the first empirical study of memory personalization, proposing session-level storage gating, a lightweight framework that selectively bypasses memory operations for transient sessions. Our study confirms that personalization yields substantial retention gains under perfect gating, yet reveals that accurate gating remains an open and critical challenge. Our benchmark and source code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[K12Vista: Exploring the Boundaries of MLLMs in K-12 Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.01676)**  `arXiv:2506.01676`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chong Li, Chenglin Zhu, Tao Zhang, Mingan Lin, Zenan Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models have demonstrated remarkable reasoning capabilities in various visual tasks. However, their abilities in K12 scenarios are still systematically underexplored. Previous studies suffer from various limitations including narrow subject coverage, insufficient data scale, lack of diversity in question types, and naive answer-centric evaluation method, resulting in insufficient exploration of model capabilities. To address these gaps, we propose K12Vista, the most comprehensive multimodal benchmark for Chinese K12 subject knowledge understanding and reasoning to date, featuring 33,000 questions across five core subjects from primary to high school and three question types. Moreover, beyond the final outcome, we are also concerned with the correctness of MLLMs' reasoning processes. For this purpose, we meticulously compiles errors from MLLMs' reasoning processes and leverage an automated data pipeline to construct K12-PEM-800K, the largest process evaluation dataset offering detailed step-by-step judgement annotations for MLLMs' reasoning. Subsequently, we developed K12-PEM, an advanced process evaluation model that integrates an overall assessment of both the reasoning process and answer correctness. Moreover, we also introduce K12-PEBench, the first high-quality, human-annotated benchmark specifically designed for evaluating abilities of reasoning processthis http URLexperiments reveal that current MLLMs exhibit significant flaws when reasoning within K12Vista, providing critical insights for the development of more capablethis http URLopen our resources atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MathConstruct: Challenging LLM Reasoning with Constructive Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.10197)**  `arXiv:2502.10197`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mislav Balunović, Jasper Dekoninck, Nikola Jovanović, Ivo Petrov, Martin Vechev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive performance in mathematics, existing math benchmarks come with significant limitations. Many focus on problems with fixed ground-truth answers, and are often saturated due to problem simplicity or the viability of guessing or memorization. Crucially, they capture only a narrow subset of relevant math problems. To address this research gap, we introduce MathConstruct, a new benchmark of 121 challenging problems sourced from various math competitions, which targets constructive proofs, a widely encountered problem type requiring the construction of mathematical objects with specific properties. These proofs are particularly suitable for LLM evaluation, as solution correctness can be easily verified. Our automated verifiers also enable MathConstruct to generate problem variations, used to evaluate robustness. State-of-the-art LLMs solve only 60% of MathConstruct problems, highlighting its complexity and importance for LLM evaluation.
  </details>
